# Environment-first research leads

**Purpose:** working file for obscure, marginal recordings found by researching environments first. This is a research queue, not a finished discography. Do not promote a lead without checking the recording itself, item-level provenance and rights. Visibility ratings are editorial judgements and should be revisited.

## Priority leads

### 1. ARSCH — Bitsch, Valais, Switzerland; commune/farm and barn studio

- **Status:** VERIFIED recording / HIGH PRIORITY for the project's obscurity remit.
- **Recording:** *ARSCH* (self-titled), released December 2020; limited cassette and digital issue. Bandcamp lists all tracks as written and produced by ARSCH; the cassette is a small edition. The label describes the group as an anarcho-syndicalist commune running a self-sufficient farm in Bitsch, with a 1970s-style recording studio in an old barn.
- **Environment connection:** the group is not merely a band who visited a commune: the label and artist describe ARSCH as a commune/collective that made music within its own living environment. Members reportedly accumulated reel-to-reel recordings in the barn; a 2019 fire destroyed much of the archive and vintage equipment. The 2020 album was compiled from recordings that survived.
- **Why it fits:** highly specific community, self-organised recording infrastructure, marginal circulation and an unusually fragile tape archive. Strong example of the environment-first method.
- **Cautions / next steps:** independently corroborate the community history and fire account; ask the label/collective for recording dates, tape formats, surviving reel inventory, who performed/recorded, and which tracks derive from pre-fire tapes. Do not repeat the label's colourful fire anecdote as independently verified fact. No audio auditioned in this research pass.
- **Sources:** https://www.empty-space.ch/work/arsch-arsch ; https://arsch.bandcamp.com/album/arsch ; https://tobirarecords.com/products/arsch-arsch-tape

### 2. The Holy Church of Yahweh — Quezon City, Metro Manila, Philippines; closed faith community

- **Status:** LEAD / PROMISING; archive and cassette recordings documented, but music content and access need item-level verification.
- **Recording corpus:** an ethnomusicological case study reports several dozen digitised cassette recordings made by members from 1978 to about 1991. They capture sermons, healing sessions, prayers, trance utterances in which the founding mother or deities speak or sing, and ritual sounds including bells. The community has an archive of recordings, transcripts, logs, photographs and documents.
- **Environment connection:** a community of about 200 members, founded by a woman and described by the researcher as a syncretic independent church. After the founder's death in 1992, the recordings remained active in communal religious practice. The church later split into three groups; possession and interpretation of the archive became tied to competing claims of continuity and legitimacy.
- **Why it fits:** exceptionally specific, obscure sound archive from a small religious community, recorded by members for internal listening and ritual use rather than commercial circulation. It may contain music, sung trance material or ritual sound, but this must be checked before a music-discography entry is made.
- **Cautions / next steps:** use the community's preferred term “faith community/church”; do not call it a cult without evidence or impose that label. The article's author worked with the archive, so approach the scholar and community through appropriate channels. Ask whether recordings contain discrete songs, chants, instrumental pieces or other musical performances; whether listening copies can be accessed; and what the community permits to be described or shared. Respect internal/sacred restrictions and factional sensitivities.
- **Source:** Earl Clarence L. Jimenez, “Memories of Sounds: An Archiving Project in Two Aural Communities,” *Asian-European Music Research Journal* 5 (2020), pp. 1–8, especially pp. 5–7: https://www.academia.edu/144206820/_Examining_Unintentionality_and_Intentionality_of_Sound_in_Prehistoric_Malta_AEMR_EJ_5_44_50

### 3. Grand Valley Institution for Women — Kitchener, Ontario, Canada; women's federal prison

- **Status:** VERIFIED recording / PROMISING to HIGH PRIORITY (small public footprint; anonymous performers by design).
- **Recording:** *Undisclosed Location*, ten original songs recorded at Grand Valley Institution for Women in 2017–2018. The Pros and Cons Program states that the songs were written, sung and recorded anonymously by incarcerated women; the album is available to listen to/download. Producer Aimee Copping; mixed by Hugh Christopher Brown.
- **Environment connection:** recordings made inside the institution, with songs created and performed by residents rather than visiting musicians. The anonymity is deliberate to protect participants and others.
- **Related recordings:** *Private Town* (2020), the second album made inside Grand Valley; official program page also lists *Paint in the Forest* (Joyceville Institution, 2017–2019) and *Postcards from the County* (Pittsburgh Institution, Ontario).
- **Why it fits:** lesser-known, anonymous prison-made music; the institutional provenance and authorship are unusually clear, and the recording is not a celebrity prison concert or a famous artist's prison-themed work.
- **Cautions / next steps:** preserve anonymity; do not attempt to identify performers. Check consent and reuse terms before reproducing lyrics, names or audio. Determine whether the program and participants want the albums included in a public discography.
- **Sources:** https://prosandconsprogram.com/undisclosed-location/ ; https://prosandconsprogram.com/ ; https://prosandconsprogram.bandcamp.com/album/private-town ; contemporary report: https://kitchener.citynews.ca/2018/03/13/womens-prison-kitchener-releases-music-album-canadian-first/

### 4. Parchman Penitentiary — Mississippi, USA; women's camp, 1930s

- **Status:** VERIFIED recordings / PROMISING; strong archival basis, but the 1987 specialist LP is not itself obscure to all prison-music collectors.
- **Recording corpus:** Library of Congress field recordings made at Parchman in 1936 and 1939 include music by women prisoners. The 1987 Rosetta Records LP *Jailhouse Blues: Women's A Cappella Songs from the Parchman Penitentiary* compiled a selection. Named performers include Mattie Mae Thomas, Josephine Douglas, Annabell Abrams, Beatrice Perry, Mary James, Hattie Goff and others. The LOC Mississippi guide documents many more Parchman recordings across 1933, 1936 and 1937, including women and men; individual disc and preservation-reel identifiers are listed.
- **Environment connection:** direct field recordings of incarcerated people performing in the prison, not later prison-themed songs.
- **Why it fits:** the women's recordings are much less visible than the familiar male Parchman/Lomax canon. The item-level LOC collection guide offers a route beyond the commercial compilation and toward unissued or less-circulated material.
- **Cautions / next steps:** distinguish the 1933, 1936, 1937 and 1939 sessions and each fieldworker; do not assume every LOC item is by women. Prioritise unissued sides and overlooked performers rather than simply adding the known LP. Investigate the original recordings and the role of recordists/institutional authorities in arranging access.
- **Sources:** Library of Congress, “Mississippi Collections in the Archive of Folk Culture”: https://wwws.loc.gov/folklife/guides/Mississippi.html ; Library of Congress Lomax chronology: https://www.loc.gov/static/collections/alan-lomax-manuscripts/documents/lomax_chronology.pdf ; Duke University on *Jailhouse Blues*: https://english.duke.edu/events/jailhouse-blues-music-parchman-farm ; Rosetta Records discography: https://www.wirz.de/music/rosetta.htm



### 5. Kalaupapa Settlement — Moloka‘i, Hawai‘i, USA; patient choirs and community music, 1952

- **Status:** VERIFIED recording / HIGH PRIORITY.
- **Recording:** a 60-minute audio cassette recorded in 1952, with the Kana‘ana Hou Boys Chorus and Kana‘ana Hou Choir on Side 1 and St. Francis of Assisi Church Choir on Side 2. The Hawai‘i Conference United Church of Christ hosts digitised audio excerpts and a two-page track/recording note. The note identifies Steve Dawson and Robert Kihe—both patients—as the recordists, and credits Nancy Talino for making the cassette available. Side 1 contains eleven songs, including an original song, “E Nā Kini o ka ‘Āina,” written by choir director Ernest Kalā; the note identifies several other singers and choir members by name.
- **Environment connection:** this is not an outside choir visiting a hospital. The recording documents choirs within the settlement community, with patients among the musicians and both named recordists themselves patients. The settlement had a substantial musical life, including choirs, bands and resident composers.
- **Why it fits:** exceptionally strong, little-circulated primary audio; obscure beyond local/community and specialist histories; resident-created and patient-recorded, with unusually useful provenance and track metadata. This is one of the best finds in the current pass.
- **Cautions / next steps:** treat the historic term “leper colony” only as quoted archival terminology; use Kalaupapa Settlement and Hansen's disease in current editorial prose. Ask the community and archive for preferred credit, full access to the cassette, complete metadata, and permission/conditions for linking or reproducing audio. Verify Hawaiian orthography and uncertain song titles with community knowledge-holders; the source itself flags one title as uncertain. Do not assume the two choirs were exclusively patients unless confirmed for each ensemble.
- **Sources:** HCUCC page with audio links: https://www.hcucc.org/kalaupapa-sunday ; accompanying recording notes (two-page PDF): https://www.hcucc.org/_files/ugd/1c51b0_974ec3094c53448d93230effc8ebec64.pdf ; DePauw University separately catalogues an item titled “Elizabeth Turnell - Leper Colony (Kalaupapa Choir), guest Rev. James Seger,” 26 November 1958, reel 94: https://depauw.libraryhost.com/repositories/2/archival_objects/181793 (contents not yet inspected; do not conflate with the 1952 cassette).



### 6. Partheni detention camp, Leros, Greece — clandestine cassette, c. 1970–71

- **Status:** VERIFIED recording / VERY HIGH PRIORITY.
- **Recording:** *Τα Τραγούδια της Εξορίας (1967–71)* / *The Songs of Exile*. A clandestine portable-tape recording made in Partheni camp on Leros in late 1970 or early 1971 by political detainees under the Colonels' dictatorship. A surviving copy was preserved by former detainee Kyriakos Ypsilandis; the original tape reportedly passed to him after the junta fell. The source describes 16 songs. Some songs were composed during transfers between Gyaros and Leros camps; the recording itself was made at Partheni. Do not call the whole collection “recorded at Lakki.”
- **Makers / performers:** Nikos Damigos wrote most lyrics; Christos Louretzis composed most music. The source names instrumentalists including Louretzis (mandolin), Yorgos Papalopoulos (bouzouki), Damigos and Tasos Theodoridis (guitar), Ypsilandis (accordion), and Yannis Kranakis (drums); singers included Damigos, Louretzis, Stavros Skourtopoulos, Thanassis Ladas, Theodoridis and Panayotis Kazakos. The source says Mitsos Stolidis, Damigos and Louretzis introduce songs on the original tape. Verify spellings against Greek primary testimony.
- **Recording circumstances:** a cassette recorder was obtained clandestinely through Charilaos Florakis; only a few detainees knew where it was hidden. Ypsilandis constructed a makeshift recording hut inside the barbed-wire enclosure. Two or three prisoners acted as lookouts. The tape contains abrupt interruptions when guards approached. The tape survived through Damigos's care and was later given to Ypsilandis. Three songs said not to have survived on the tape (“Το Κάστρο”, “Καρτέρα με”, “Σύρμα αγκάθι”) were reportedly re-recorded by Louretzis on 45 rpm; do not merge these later discs into the original cassette without separate object records.
- **Why it fits:** a genuinely obscure, non-commercial, clandestine prison-camp recording with specific makers, recording circumstances, original medium and survival chain. This is one of the strongest environment-first finds so far and is exactly the kind of overlooked object the project should prioritise.
- **Cautions / next steps:** locate and inspect the surviving cassette or best-generation transfer; establish its current custodian, complete track order, duration, tape generation, condition, and whether any of the 16 songs have been digitised. The 2018 French-language scholarly article calls it a little-known document and states that it had not been published on disc at that time; a later source describes a CD faithfully copying the clandestine recording, so clarify whether that CD was ever actually issued, by whom, and when. Preserve distinction between the original cassette, any CD transfer, and separate 45 rpm rerecordings. The prison camp was part of a dictatorship's political repression; avoid romanticising the detainees' circumstances.
- **Sources:** Makis Solomos, “21 avril 1967, 11 septembre 1973 : que peut la musique ?,” *Filigrane* 23 (2018), full text: https://www.researchgate.net/publication/338935637_21_avril_1967_11_septembre_1973_que_peut_la_musique (HAL ID hal-02455333); Makis Solomos, “Música y sonido en detención política. Escuchar, recordar y empoderarse” (2023): https://portal.amelica.org/ameli/journal/645/6454649008/html/ ; item/song page with names, recording story and four song texts: https://antiwarsongs.org/canzone.php?id=47742&lang=en .

### 7. Drapchi Prison — Lhasa, Tibet; “Drapchi 14” cassette, 1993

- **Status:** VERIFIED recording / CONTEXT ONLY for obscurity priority.
- **Recording:** fourteen imprisoned Tibetan nuns secretly recorded songs and short messages on a smuggled cassette in June 1993. The tape was smuggled out and copied/circulated; a contemporary Tibet Information Network version contains 18 of the clearest songs, with personal messages edited out and one singer's voice electronically altered to protect her identity. Other accounts cite 16, 18 or 27 tracks depending on original, edited and later copies; do not collapse these variants.
- **Why context only:** a powerful and well-documented prison recording, but it has been widely covered in human-rights reporting and issued/distributed outside Tibet. Keep it as a benchmark and source trail, not a priority “unknown find.”
- **Sources:** contemporary TIN account/reproduction: https://www.columbia.edu/itc/ealac/barnett/pdfs/link17-nunssong.pdf ; Amnesty International 24 Feb 1994: https://www.amnesty.org/en/documents/asa17/011/1994/en/ ; International Campaign for Tibet / Ngawang Sangdrol testimony: https://savetibet.org/ngawang-sangdrol-testifies-on-human-rights-before-us-congress/ ; TIN *Rukhag 3*: https://savetibet.org/wp-content/uploads/2013/05/Rukhag3.pdf .

### 8. Clifton Hill Community Music Centre — Melbourne, Australia; performance cassette archive, 1976–83

- **Status:** VERIFIED archive / CONTEXT ONLY or secondary research route.
- **Recording corpus:** a substantial online archive of binaural cassette recordings made by regular performer Ernie Althoff at an alternative community music centre in an old organ factory. The centre operated 1976–1983; recordings sat unorganised for more than 40 years and were digitised/organised in 2020–21. The archive lists performances, artists, dates, durations, posters and publications; examples include junk-electronic workshops, improvisation, tape experiments and works by currently unknown performers.
- **Why context only:** the site and scene are now reasonably discoverable and some participants later became recognised Australian experimental artists. Nevertheless, its incomplete index and “currently unknown” credits make it a useful route for overlooked individual recordings, missing tapes and anonymous works.
- **Next steps:** audit the catalogue for performers/items with no later discography, especially anonymous/unknown events and recordings not otherwise issued; contact the archive contributors with specific item-level questions. Do not treat all recordings as obscure merely because they are cassette documents.
- **Source:** https://www.listeningtothearchive.com/artists/ernie-althoff (archive overview and index).



### 9. National Hansen's Disease Center / Carville — Louisiana, USA; patient cantata cassettes

- **Status:** PARTIALLY VERIFIED / HIGH PRIORITY archival lead (catalogued sound carriers; content not auditioned).
- **Recording objects:** The Daughters of Charity Archives' 2023 finding aid for the Carville collection lists cassette tapes in Audio-Visual series 11-2-8-4, Box 48: “Carville organ music” (folder/item 5); “Carville Easter cantata performed by patients, narrated by Buddy Glacis, organ played by Sr. Laura Stricker” (folder/item 6); “Christmas cantata” (folder/item 7); and “Christmas cantata by Sr. Laura Stricker & patients” dated 1 January 1981 (folder/item 8). These are actual catalogue entries for cassette carriers, not merely programme references.
- **Environment connection:** Carville was the National Hansen's Disease Center, where people diagnosed with Hansen's disease were compulsorily confined by law until 1950; the hospital closed in 1998. The archive describes the patient-run newspaper, social and religious life, and chapel records. The cantata catalogue descriptions explicitly identify patients as performers.
- **Why it fits:** little-circulated patient-created institutional music preserved in a specialist archive; unlike the famous Kalaupapa choir releases, these Carville cantata tapes do not appear to have a commercial issue or public online audio link. Potentially one of the best next archive-access targets.
- **Cautions / next steps:** request digital surrogates or supervised listening copies and exact dates/credits for all three cantata-related cassettes. Confirm whether “Christmas cantata” and the 1981 “Christmas cantata by Sr. Laura Stricker & patients” are separate recordings or duplicate catalogue descriptions. Confirm patient performers' consent/identification and any rights-holder permissions. The finding aid requires permission from the Daughters of Charity Communications Director before any publication of archival text, images, video or audio; follow archive reproduction rules. Use “Carville” / “National Hansen's Disease Center” and “Hansen's disease” in current prose, not stigmatizing historic labels except when quoting a catalogue title.
- **Sources:** Daughters of Charity Archives, *Carville, LA – National Hansen's Disease Center* finding aid (2023), especially pp. 1 and 14: https://docarchivesblog.org/wp-content/uploads/2023/02/11-2-carville-la-national-hansens-disease-center-finding-aid.pdf ; archive exhibit: https://docapsl.omeka.net/exhibits/show/the-daughters--history-in-heal/the-national-hans-s-disease- ; HRSA institutional history: https://www.hrsa.gov/hansens-disease/history .

## Calibration and exclusions

### Zomba Prison Project — Malawi

- **Status:** CONTEXT ONLY / DO NOT PRIORITISE for new discovery.
- **Reason:** the 2015 album *I Have No Everything Here* and follow-up received substantial international press and a 2016 Grammy nomination. It is a valuable comparison point and demonstrates prison-made music, but its visibility is too high for the project's first-line obscure discovery queue. Search for other Malawi prison recordings or smaller local projects instead.
- **Sources:** https://www.sixdegreesrecords.com/zomba-prison-project/ ; https://www.wgbh.org/news/2016-10-30/i-have-no-everything-here-from-an-overcrowded-prison-in-malawi-moving-music

### Buffalo State Hospital / “The Language of Schizophrenia” — New York, USA

- **Status:** REJECTED as a music entry; archival sound lead only.
- **Reason:** a Ladd School Historical Society account describes six sides of recordings made by Dr. Unger inside Buffalo State Hospital, but the material is patient interviews/voices and ambient sound, not established musical recordings. Do not admit merely because the recordings are eerie or institutionally situated.
- **Source:** https://www.theladdschool.com/collections/language-of-schizophrenia-buffalo-state-hospital/

## Immediate next verification tasks

1. Contact/trace ARSCH and Empty Space for original tape chronology, surviving reel inventory and recording personnel.
2. Identify the scholar and community archive contact for the Holy Church of Yahweh case study; establish whether the cassettes contain music and whether access is permitted.
3. Obtain track-level credits/recording notes for the Pros and Cons albums while preserving participant anonymity.
4. Audit LOC item-level catalogue records for Parchman women's recordings and identify unreleased sides, names and access conditions.
5. Continue with similarly obscure environments in the Balkans, Eastern Europe, Latin America, South/Southeast Asia and Oceania; do not inflate the list with famous cases.

**Research limitations for this pass:** English-language web/catalogue searches only; no audio was auditioned; no physical carrier inspected; no archive or artist contacted; no permissions obtained. “Verified recording” means existence and core provenance are documented by sources, not that this researcher has listened to the audio.


### 10. Svenskt visarkiv prison-song recordings — Sweden; multiple prisons, 1965–73

- **Status:** VERIFIED archival recording corpus / HIGH PRIORITY for item-level discovery, with an important obscurity qualification.
- **Recording corpus:** Bertil Sundin made five reel-to-reel tapes of prison songs and interviews between 1965 and 1967. In 1969, Sundin and student Elisabeth Skarpe made three further documentations at Hall Prison (outside Södertälje) and the Norrköping institution. Together they recorded 46 songs by eight male singers. Archive references are SVA BA97–100, BA722–723 and BA758. Gunnar Ternhag's subsequent project made around 50 songs plus interviews in 1971–72; in 1973 he recorded communal singing at Hinseberg women's prison (SVA BA1891).
- **Environment connection:** these are direct recordings of people singing inside prisons, collected by researchers who had access to the institutions. The archive's own history explains that Sundin first asked prison staff about prison songs, but staff generally did not know the tradition; he found singers through inmate networks. The material documents a cultural world not necessarily visible to prison personnel.
- **Why it fits:** the original archival reels are distinct from the later commercial LP canon. Dan Lundberg's 2017/2018 study reports that Sundin's recordings had been classified as confidential and were not registered in the archive database; Lundberg located them through 1960s tape logs. The 46-song corpus and later sessions therefore offer item-level discovery potential beyond the more visible *Kåklåtar* LPs and Jailbird Singers releases.
- **Obscurity qualification:** Swedish prison songs as a genre have been studied and some performers/releases became known. Do not present the whole tradition as undiscovered. Prioritise the unissued or lesser-circulated archival performances, variant songs, singers not represented on commercial LPs, and the women’s-prison material. Lundberg notes that women were almost entirely absent from the archive’s early recordings; the Hinseberg communal singing session is an important corrective, though an attempt to interview individual women singers did not succeed.
- **Cautions / next steps:** request current catalogue descriptions, access conditions and listening copies from Svenskt visarkiv; establish which reels/sessions are publicly accessible and whether any have been digitised or issued. Preserve informant anonymity where required and do not attempt to reverse historical anonymisation. Separate original field recordings from later LP studio sessions, post-release recordings and research reconstructions. No audio auditioned in this pass.
- **Sources:** Dan Lundberg, *Singing through the Bars: Prison Songs as Identity Markers and as Cultural Heritage* (Svenskt visarkiv, 2017/2018), especially pp. 42–45 and 59: https://carkiv.musikverk.se/www/epublikationer/Singing_through_the_bars_Lundberg.pdf ; Svenskt visarkiv, “Recordings”: https://old.capricemusic.se/svensktvisarkiv/artikel/inspelningarna/?lang=en ; archive-history discussion of the 1960s collecting project: https://carkiv.musikverk.se/www/epublikationer/MFSV_51_Det_stora_inspelningsprojektet.pdf .


### 11. Anders Enevig field recordings — Denmark; homeless people, travellers and Romani communities, 1958 onward

- **Status:** VERIFIED archival corpus / PROMISING discovery route; individual music items require catalogue-level identification.
- **Recording corpus:** Danish police officer and folklorist Anders Enevig began recording songs and stories among Copenhagen's homeless and socially marginalised residents in 1958. His work expanded to travellers and other communities around Denmark. Nearly 400 hours of Enevig's tape recordings are preserved in Dansk Folkemindesamling at the Royal Danish Library. A recent research paper describes Enevig's archive as 205 tapes from 1958–1983 and identifies “Prinser og Vagabonder” as a collection of songs and music among Copenhagen's homeless people; reconcile the 205-tape count with the broader nearly-400-hour corpus and establish the relevant item numbers.
- **Specific audio lead:** the Danish Immigration Museum's archived “Romani People & Travellers” page links audio from Enevig's 1959 fieldwork, including traveller Agnes Sambleben singing a ballad and speaking in a mixture of Romany and German. The page also documents Blushøj camp in Valby, a municipal caravan site established in 1948 and closed in 1962. Do not assume the Sambleben recording was made at Blushøj; the page presents the camp and the audio on the same topic page but does not establish that exact location.
- **Environment connection:** the recordings document musical traditions and everyday life among people who were socially marginalised, itinerant or without stable housing, rather than music made within a formal institution. This is a useful expansion of the environment map toward informal encampments and precarious urban communities.
- **Why it fits:** substantial primary sound corpus with very little international visibility; named singers and a specific surviving audio example; strong potential for finding individual songs that never received commercial releases. The broad collection is not itself obscure to Danish ethnologists, so prioritise overlooked performers and recordings, not the already published book as a discovery.
- **Cautions / next steps:** use respectful contemporary language for communities and explain historical terminology only when quoting sources. Do not conflate Romani people, travellers, homeless people and alcohol-dependent people as one group; Enevig's corpus spans distinct communities. Identify exact archive call numbers, record dates, locations and consent/access conditions through the Royal Danish Library's audio registrants. The library notes that some folklore recordings are non-circulatory due to donor restrictions, privacy or rights. No audio auditioned in this pass.
- **Sources:** Danish Biographical Lexicon, “Anders Enevig” (nearly 400 hours deposited in Dansk Folkemindesamling): https://biografiskleksikon.lex.dk/Anders_Enevig ; Danish Immigration Museum archived page, including Agnes Sambleben audio and Blushøj camp context: https://www.danishimmigrationmuseum.com/the-romani-people-travellers/ ; Royal Danish Library, Folklore Archive and audio registrants: https://www.kb.dk/en/find-materials/collections/folklore-archive and https://www.kb.dk/en/find-materials/guides/folklore-and-material-about-culture-everyday-life ; research paper describing Enevig's 205-tape collection: https://rucforsk.ruc.dk/ws/files/81615823/LMVB_Specialet_.pdf .

### Correction / date discrepancy — Hinseberg women's prison recording

The Hinseberg group-singing recording has a date discrepancy in the published material. Lundberg's book describes Ternhag recording a community-singing event in 1973 (SVA BA1891), while the current Svenskt visarkiv page and another archive account identify the Hinseberg visit/recording as 1975. Keep the date as **1973 or 1975 — unresolved** until the original tape log or catalogue record is checked. Do not present 1973 as settled.


### 12. Margaret Valiant — California FSA migrant camps, USA; field recordings 1938–39

- **Status:** VERIFIED archival recordings / HIGH PRIORITY for overlooked individual camp performances.
- **Recording corpus:** Margaret Valiant recorded music in California's Farm Security Administration (FSA) migrant camps in 1938–39. The Library of Congress holds the 20-disc *Margaret Valiant Recordings for the Farm Security Administration* collection (AFC 1939/017) and the 13-disc *Margaret Valiant Southwest and California Recordings* collection (AFC 1940/008). The latter includes recordings from migrant camps alongside material from other locations in California and Arizona; do not treat every disc as camp music.
- **Specific camp recordings / performers:** the LOC card catalogue identifies the King family singing “It's Me, Oh Lord” at Calipatria Migratory Camp (AFS 3317 B01), and Curly Reeves singing “Lonesome Road” at Brawley Migratory Camp (AFS 3320 B02). The LOC National Sampler provides an audio selection of “CCC Blues,” sung by Jimmie Collins and recorded at Brawley Migratory Camp in 1939 (AFS 3563 A2). Other catalogued camp material includes group singing at Shafter FSA Camp and recordings of the King Family Orchestra. The King family were migrant musicians from Arkansas.
- **Environment connection:** these are performances by people living and working in government-established migrant camps during the Great Depression and Dust Bowl-era displacement, not later songs merely about migration. Valiant's fieldwork documents the musical life of residents in the camps.
- **Why it fits:** historian Sheryl Kaskowitz describes Valiant's recordings as little-known and reports that Valiant sought to make them public but FSA leadership did not support the proposal; the discs were deposited at the Library of Congress. The collection is discoverable to archival specialists, but individual camp performances and named performers remain relatively overlooked. This is a strong example of the project's environment-first approach.
- **Obscurity qualification:** the broader Todd–Sonkin *Voices from the Dust Bowl* corpus is well known and digitised; do not simply duplicate that canon. Valiant's own corpus and individual camp items deserve separate attention, especially recordings not included in later public presentations. The LOC sample track “CCC Blues” is already listenable, so it is a useful entry point but not itself an undiscovered recording.
- **Cautions / next steps:** distinguish camp residents' own performances from Valiant's organised radio/pageant events and from the “Christmas in a Migrant Camp” broadcast featuring outside entertainers. Establish precise recording dates, camp, performers and AFS matrix/item numbers for each candidate. Respect the historical context of displacement, poverty, labour and racialised camp conditions; avoid nostalgic “Okie” framing except when quoting contemporary sources. No audio auditioned in this research pass.
- **Sources:** LOC Arizona Folklife guide, AFC 1939/017 and AFC 1940/008: https://guides.loc.gov/arizona-folklife ; LOC National Sampler, “CCC Blues” (AFS 3563 A2): https://wwws.loc.gov/folklife/sampler/CAaudio.html ; LOC card catalogue, King family “It's Me, Oh Lord” (AFS 3317 B01): https://www.loc.gov/item/afc9999005.27238 ; LOC card catalogue, Curly Reeves “Lonesome Road” (AFS 03320 B02): https://www.loc.gov/item/afc9999005.9995 ; Sheryl Kaskowitz, “Government Song Women”: https://www.neh.gov/article/government-song-women ; Kaskowitz's recordings page: https://www.sherylkaskowitz.com/recordings .

### 13. Benjamin J. Harbert collection of music in prisons — Louisiana and California, USA; fieldwork 2004–2023

- **Status:** VERIFIED archival corpus / PROMISING for lesser-circulated performances, especially women's prison material and the New Folsom songwriting programme.
- **Archive:** Library of Congress American Folklife Center, *Benjamin J. Harbert collection of music in prisons*, AFC 2024/014. The 2025 finding aid describes 8,309 items, including 660 digital sound files and 530 video files; the collection is open to research by request. Harbert's fieldwork spans Angola, Elayn Hunt Correctional Center, Louisiana Correctional Institute for Women (LCIW), California Men's Colony, New Folsom Prison and other California prison material.
- **Specific recording leads:** LCIW material (2008–09) includes the LCIW Choir and incarcerated women singing in the yard and in lockdown. The finding aid itemises audio files from 19 October 2009, including an “End of choir performance” WAV (15:05; filepath LCIWT09.WAV), plus associated audio/video and transcripts. At New Folsom in 2009, Jim Carlson's songwriting programme is documented through recordings of the Prison Blues Band; an item dated 5 August 2009 includes the band introducing its original blues/traditional/fusion music and playing “Blues Man” (7:56; MEETINGT02.WAV). The collection also includes a 2007 compilation CD, *Loose These Chains*, with live inmate performances from nine Louisiana prisons, including LCIW.
- **Why it fits:** the collection is newly processed (finding aid prepared 2025), extensive, and open for research. Its item-level documentation gives a concrete path to performances that are less visible than the published Angola story. LCIW singing in lockdown and New Folsom's long-running songwriting community are particularly worth exploring.
- **Obscurity qualification:** this is not a hidden collection in the strict sense: Harbert's 2013 documentary and 2023 book make some of the material visible. Rate the corpus PROMISING, not an automatic high-priority discovery. Prioritise specific unissued audio files, women performers, lockdown recordings and material not used in the documentary/book or commercial compilations. Do not add Angola as a new environment entry merely because the finding aid is recent; connect this as an expanded archive trail to the existing Angola notes.
- **Cautions / next steps:** request files through the Library of Congress Folklife Reading Room and check third-party rights, consent, current performer preferences and reuse conditions. The finding aid states that Harbert's rights are under CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 during his lifetime, but third-party rights may differ. Do not identify anonymous or pseudonymous incarcerated performers beyond catalogue permissions. No audio auditioned.
- **Sources:** Library of Congress finding aid (2025), collection summary and access terms: https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/gdc/gdcfindingaidpdfs/af024007/af024007.pdf ; LCIW item-level entries: same finding aid, pp. 136–137; New Folsom Prison Blues Band item: same finding aid, pp. 140–141; related LOC lecture: https://www.loc.gov/item/webcast-10980/ .


### 14. Prussian Phonographic Commission / Berlin Phonogramm-Archiv — German POW camps, 1915–18

- **Status:** VERIFIED music-recording corpus / HIGH PRIORITY as an archival research route, with strong ethical and provenance cautions.
- **Recording corpus:** During the First World War, the Prussian Phonographic Commission and associated researchers recorded prisoners of war from Allied countries and colonial territories in German camps and nearby studios. A 2025 review in the *Journal of the Royal Musical Association* distinguishes the Berlin Lautarchiv's shellac speech discs from the music recordings: singing and instrumental performances were recorded on Edison wax cylinders and entered the Berlin Phonogramm-Archiv. Britta Lange's archival history gives a total of 1,022 wax cylinders with music recordings, alongside 1,651 gramophone records with voice, music and instrumental recordings in the wider project.
- **Specific research paths:** recordings of Tunisian, Georgian, Armenian, Estonian, Romanian, Romani and other soldiers are discussed in the literature. Examples of published subsets include *Echoes from the Past: Georgian Prisoners' Songs Recorded on Wax Cylinders in Germany 1916–1918* (2014), *1916–1918 Voice Imprints: Recordings of Russian Armenian POWs in German Camps* (2019), and the Italian POW collection studied by Ignazio Macchiarella and Emilio Tamburini in *Le voci ritrovate* (2018). These publications are starting points, not evidence that the entire corpus has been heard or catalogued adequately.
- **Why it fits:** Brian Fairley's 2025 review describes a “double obscurity”: the recordings are poorly suited to the linguistic catalogue categories for which they were made, and their archival descriptions often fail to preserve prisoners' individuality. He notes that Berlin access is comparatively restricted and much of the music remains understudied. This is a major source for overlooked performances by named and unnamed soldiers from regions whose musical histories are underrepresented in European sound archives.
- **Obscurity qualification:** this is not an undiscovered collection: several national subsets have been published and the colonial sound archive has attracted important critical scholarship. Prioritise less-published nationalities, performers, songs and cylinder objects, and avoid duplicating the Georgian, Armenian and Italian publications without a clear new angle.
- **Cautions / next steps:** the project emerged from colonial and racialised scholarship and took advantage of prisoners' captivity as a research opportunity. Do not frame the recordings as neutral ethnographic preservation or as consent-based field recordings. Researchers must examine the conditions under which prisoners were selected, asked or compelled to perform, the asymmetries of power, the original catalogue's racial/linguistic classifications and the current rights/access arrangements. Collaborate with scholars and cultural practitioners from the recorded communities; preserve names and identities only where reliable and appropriate. Distinguish the Berlin Lautarchiv speech-disc holdings from the Berlin Phonogramm-Archiv music cylinders and from Vienna's separate POW recording collection. No audio auditioned.
- **Sources:** Britta Lange, “Archive, Collection, Museum: On the History of the Archiving of Voices at the Sound Archive of the Humboldt University” (2017): https://www.researchcatalogue.net/view/326465/326466 ; Brian Fairley, “Sounds of Captivity: Prisoner-of-War Recordings in the First World War,” *Journal of the Royal Musical Association* 150.1 (2025), pp. 303–312: https://doi.org/10.1017/rma.2025.10025 ; Susanne Ziegler and Rusudan Tsurtsumia, *Echoes from the Past* (2014); Fennesz-Juhasz, “Sound Recordings of Romani Soldiers in German and Austrian Prisoner-of-War Camps, 1915–1918” (2017); Macchiarella and Tamburini, *Le voci ritrovate* (2018).

### 15. David Boder — displaced-persons camps and refugee institutions, Europe, 1946

- **Status:** VERIFIED sound recordings / PROMISING as an overlooked repertoire and source trail; some songs now publicly accessible.
- **Recording corpus:** In summer 1946, psychologist David Boder travelled through displaced-persons camps, children's homes, refugee hostels and vocational schools in France, Switzerland, Italy and Germany with a portable wire recorder. He conducted around 130 interviews in nine languages, totalling more than 90 hours. From late August he began setting aside separate wire spools for songs sung by Holocaust survivors and other displaced people. The recordings are among the earliest known sound recordings of Holocaust survivors and constitute an early sound archive of Holocaust songs.
- **Specific object:** a spool labelled “Henonville? Songs” was miscatalogued in the 1960s as “Heroville Songs” and remained overlooked for decades. It was rediscovered in 2016 and identified as songs recorded at Hénonville DP camp. The United States Holocaust Memorial Museum catalogue lists “Songs recorded in Henonville DP camp,” RG-91.2015, accession 2017.325. Related catalogued song recordings include “Songs of Bellevue” (RG-91.2265) and “Songs of Tradate” (RG-91.2266). The original wire recordings are held in the David Boder collection at the University of Akron; interviews and song-related records are also available through Voices of the Holocaust / IIT and USHMM.
- **Why it fits:** individual survivors performed songs they carried from ghettos and camps into the displaced-persons environment. The Hénonville spool is a concrete example of a lost/mislabelled recording recovered through archive research. There may be lesser-known songs and performers within the larger set beyond the small number of tracks commonly presented.
- **Obscurity qualification:** Boder's collection and the rediscovered Hénonville spool have received media and scholarly attention; some songs are already published online. Do not present the collection as unknown. Focus on lesser-circulated tracks, under-described singers, the relationship between the place of composition/performance and the DP camp where Boder recorded it, and the archive's original selection/editing practices.
- **Cautions / next steps:** Boder was a psychologist, not a trained ethnomusicologist; scholarship notes that he often solicited songs after interviews and shaped what was recorded through his prompts. Some singers were asked to prioritise clear words over melody, so the recordings are not transparent records of how songs were originally performed in ghettos/camps. Respect survivors' histories and avoid reducing songs to evidence of trauma alone. Verify exact camp, date, performer and wire-spool ID before creating individual entries.
- **Sources:** World ORT, “David Boder and the Songs on the Wire”: https://holocaustmusic.ort.org/memory/dp-camps/david-boder/ ; USHMM, “Songs recorded in Henonville DP camp,” RG-91.2015: https://collections.ushmm.org/search/catalog/irn719266 ; USHMM, “Songs of Bellevue,” RG-91.2265: https://collections.ushmm.org/search/catalog/irn620183 ; USHMM, “Songs of Tradate,” RG-91.2266: https://collections.ushmm.org/search/catalog/irn620184 ; IIT Voices of the Holocaust: https://voices.library.iit.edu/ ; Brian Fairley, 2025 review, pp. 303–312, for the methodological and historiographical cautions: https://doi.org/10.1017/rma.2025.10025 .


## 14. Indiana State Prison, Michigan City, Indiana, USA — Bruce Jackson field tapes, 1962

- **HIGH PRIORITY / exact unreleased-material route.** Bruce Jackson recorded at Indiana State Prison on 29–31 March, 20–25 April, and 1–3 July 1962. The original tapes are identified in the *Death Chants* Folk-Legacy editor's note as deposited at Indiana University's Archives of Folk and Primitive Music under accessions **62-4-28 (11), 62-6-18 (21), 62-8-9 (27)** and original tape numbers **T-7" 465–498, 503, 539–543**. The note explicitly says these tapes contain songs not included on the LP.
- The released record is not a transparent edition of the prison sessions: Hank Ferguson was released in late 1962 and most of his songs were re-recorded at his home in July 1963. The editor's note says only one band on the LP was taken from Jackson's original prison tapes, partly because prison recording conditions involved interruptions, noise and distortion. This makes the original tapes especially valuable for locating songs that disappeared from the commercial release and for distinguishing prison performances from post-release re-recordings.
- Smithsonian catalogue entries separately identify Bruce Jackson's Indiana prison songs, including reels 1 and 2 (FW-ASCH-7RR-4919 / 4918), performers Eugene Rhodes, Hank Ferguson, Billy Turpin, Charlie Bell and others. Some material was released through Folk-Legacy; do not assume the entire archive is obscure or unreleased.
- **Next verification:** request the original accession registers and tape logs from Indiana University's Archives of Traditional Music; reconcile the 1962 accession numbers with Smithsonian/Folk-Legacy copies and the later Jackson collection; identify the unissued tracks, performers, and whether any recordings are duplicates or post-release versions. Do not claim to have auditioned the tapes.
- **Access/rights:** Indiana University Archives of Traditional Music is the successor to the Archives of Folk and Primitive Music. Listening access, reproduction and publication require direct archive/rights enquiries. Smithsonian catalogue material has research-only listening restrictions and no duplication; publication permission is required.
- Sources: Folk-Legacy *Death Chants* editor's note (original tape numbers and explicit unissued-songs statement): https://folkways-media.si.edu/docs/folkways/artwork/FLG00013-LP.pdf ; Smithsonian catalogue: https://collections.si.edu/search/results.htm?q=%22Bruce+Jackson%22+%22Indiana+Prison+Songs%22 ; IU Archives of Traditional Music collection history: https://libraries.indiana.edu/policies/archives-traditional-music

## 15. Prussian Phonographic Commission — German POW camps, 1915–18; wax-cylinder music archive

- **HIGH PRIORITY as a large-scale item-level discovery programme; individual recordings still require screening.** Georg Schünemann recorded **1,022 wax cylinders of music** in German prisoner-of-war camps between 1915 and 1918. This cylinder corpus is distinct from Wilhelm Doegen's approximately 1,650 shellac gramophone studies, which are primarily speech recordings though some include music. Do not conflate the two media or describe all 2,672 objects as music cylinders.
- Recordings represent many national and colonial backgrounds, mostly European and overseas territories. The project was conducted under coercive captivity and within German racialised/colonial research structures; camp authorities and researchers selected prisoners, and the Commission used personal questionnaires. Treat the archive as an asymmetrical encounter, not an uncomplicated folk-music survey.
- **New practical access route:** the Humboldt-Universität Lautarchiv's online catalogue includes its digital files and associated documentation. The archive says the cylinders are digitised and searchable through the State Museums in Berlin database; requested audio and documents are supplied under a user agreement/download link. The Berlin Ethnological Museum project describes the intended integration of audio, song texts/translations, drawings, photographs and correspondence with Humboldt-Universität records.
- Research by language, nationality, camp and individual performer rather than treating “POW music” as a single genre. Potential under-covered groups should be discovered from catalogue records rather than presumed from broad collection descriptions. Prioritise named performers, songs with no later commercial issue, and records whose context or performer biographies remain thin.
- **Next verification:** query the database for cylinder signatures and corresponding documentation; build an item-level spreadsheet with performer, camp, date, language, genre/instrument, original cylinder number, digitisation/access status, and any later publication. Confirm whether specific records can be auditioned and cited before adding them as individual recordings.
- Sources: Ethnologisches Museum project overview: https://www.smb.museum/en/museums-institutions/ethnologisches-museum/collection-research/research/sound-recordings-made-by-the-prussian-phonographic-commission/ ; Lautarchiv catalogue/access policy: https://www.lautarchiv.hu-berlin.de/en/collections-and-catalog/catalog/ ; Lautarchiv collections and digitisation note: https://www.lautarchiv.hu-berlin.de/en/collections-and-catalog/collections/ ; chronology distinguishing 1,651 gramophone studies and 1,022 cylinders: https://www.lautarchiv.hu-berlin.de/en/introduction/chronology/ ; MIDA research overview of Commission's camp selection and methods: https://www.projekt-mida.de/reflexicon/lautarchiv/


## 16. Hénonville DP centre, France — David Boder wire recordings, 1946

- **PROMISING / item-level rediscovery within a known archive.** A wire spool labelled “Hénonville? Songs” was rediscovered in 2016 after decades of miscataloguing as “Heroville Songs.” It contains a roughly 22-minute session of six songs performed by displaced Jewish residents at the Hénonville château, where ORT operated agricultural and trade training. The US Holocaust Memorial Museum catalogues it as **RG-91.2015, accession 2017.325**, “Songs recorded in Henonville DP camp.”
- Performers identified in the specialist account include Joel Prizant (three songs), Malka Rosenberg (one), and Guta/Gute/Gita Frank (two; spelling differs across Boder's notes, museum catalogue and scholarship). The repertoire is mixed: Yiddish theatre and interwar songs, a Polish Yiddish song, a pre-war warning song, and a song originating in the Brande forced-labour camp. The recording documents songs sung in a postwar DP setting; it does **not** mean every song was composed at Hénonville or in a camp.
- The specialist account estimates that the eleven songs published online plus one additional USHMM publication represent less than one-sixth of Boder's recorded songs (excluding two religious services). The wider Boder collection is therefore a strong route to overlooked individual performances, but the archive itself is already the subject of specialist research and should not be presented as unknown.
- **Next verification:** inspect the USHMM catalogue/audio and accession documentation; confirm each performer spelling, track order and song attribution; compare with Boder's original notes and related “Songs of Bellevue” (RG-91.2265) and “Songs of Tradate” (RG-91.2266). Distinguish songs performed by DP residents from songs learned in earlier camps, ghettos or prewar life.
- **Ethical/context note:** displaced people were performing for an interviewer with a portable wire recorder in a postwar institutional setting. Preserve the circumstances and individual identities as documented; do not flatten survivors' repertoire into a generic “Holocaust camp music” category.
- Sources: specialist David Boder / Holocaust Music page, including spool rediscovery and song-level descriptions: https://holocaustmusic.ort.org/memory/dp-camps/david-boder/ ; USHMM Hénonville catalogue record: https://collections.ushmm.org/search/catalog/irn719266 ; related Bellevue and Tradate records: https://collections.ushmm.org/search/catalog/irn620183 and https://collections.ushmm.org/search/catalog/irn620184


### Indiana prison tapes — expanded Library of Congress item-level audit (AFC 2011/009)

The September 2020 Library of Congress finding aid provides a much larger inventory than the handful of reels first identified. It describes the 3,346-item Bruce Jackson and Diane Christian collection, including 1,062 sound reels and 197 cassettes, and explicitly states that the collection is open to research by request. Crucially, its manuscript series includes original tape logs, logs of songs recorded at various prisons, Indiana State Prison/Missouri Penitentiary catalogues, and three folders of Indiana State Prison transcripts (Boxes 1/3–1/11). These paper records may resolve the incomplete and conjectural tape-box descriptions. The finding aid warns that not all container information was transcribed and that digital images of containers are available on request.

**Additional Indiana State Prison reels / song-level clues:**
- **SR0751**, reel I-22, 22 April 1962, Hank Ferguson: “The Walls,” “You've Only Been My Friend,” “If I Lost You.”
- **SR0752**, reel I-23, 22 April 1962, Hank Ferguson: “Going Down That Road Feeling Bad,” “Rich Girl, Poor Girl,” “Wagon Yard,” “Gotta Travel On,” “Irene,” “Wreck of the Old #9,” “Wreck of the Old #97,” “Don't Take Your Guns to Town,” “Knoxville Girl,” “Cotton Fields.”
- **SR0755–SR0756**, reels I-26–27, 23 April 1962, Eugene Rhodes, blues; SR0756 includes a 12-string instrumental titled “What's It All About.”
- **SR0760–SR0762**, reels I-31–33, circa 1962, bluegrass attributed to Young, McCloud, Wells and Stillwell.
- **SR0764–SR0767**, reels I-35–38, circa 1962, hillbilly material attributed to Ferguson and others, blues by Rhodes and others, blues/jazz, and related group material.
- **SR0771**, reel I-42, circa 1962, Hank Ferguson folk songs including “Barbara Allen,” “Sweet Betsy from Pike,” “Little Mohee,” “Darlin' Nellie Gray,” “Down in the Valley,” “Blue-Tailed Fly” and “Careless Love.”
- **SR0730**, reel I-43, catalogue date 16 July 1962, Hank Ferguson folk songs including “Take This Hammer,” “There's One More River to Cross,” “Lonesome Road,” “I'm Not Living,” “The Walls,” “Life,” “Over There” and “Was You There.” Note the finding aid's sequence/date inconsistencies against the original session dates; verify against original tape logs.
- **SR0731–SR0733**, reels I-44–46, circa 1962: hillbilly, Ferguson and others, and spirituals.

**Indiana Reformatory, Pendleton:** SR0899–SR0901 (circa 1962) are labelled hillbilly, hillbilly/R&B, and jokes/blues/R&B. No performers or song titles are supplied in the public finding aid. This is a promising separate institution/session, but only provisional until logs and tapes establish that the labels refer to music performed by incarcerated people rather than other recorded material.

**Important caveats:** The finding aid's Indiana entries are derived largely from reel-box spines and not all descriptions are complete. Do not assume every reel is a discrete session, that all tracks are inmate-composed, or that a genre label establishes performer identity. Crosswalk the AFC IDs against the earlier Indiana University accessions and original logs before treating items as distinct. Some reels may be duplicates, compilations or re-recordings.

**Missouri State Penitentiary:** the same finding aid has a run of reels SR1059–SR0745, but many are explicitly prison/crime stories, interviews or ambiguous numbered tapes. SR0745 lists commercial gospel/blues tracks by Mahalia Jackson, the Ward Singers, Original Gospel Harmonettes, Buster Bennett, Bobby Bland, Big Bill Broonzy, Ray Charles and Jack Dupree; this appears to be a compilation of commercial recordings, not evidence of inmate-made music. Do not add these as prison-created performances without checking the tape and logs. SR0565 is labelled “Missouri State Prison / Bukka White” but its provenance and whether this is a visit, interview, dub or commercial recording remain unclear.

**Access:** Library of Congress AFC 2011/009 is open to research. Request Boxes 1/3–1/11 (especially 1/3–1/4 original logs and 1/7–1/11 catalogues/transcripts), container images, and reels SR0746, SR0751–SR0756, SR0760–SR0767, SR0771, SR0730–SR0733, SR0899–SR0901. Ask the reading room to help reconcile original IU accession numbers. No request has been sent and no audio has been auditioned.
- Primary source: Library of Congress, *Bruce Jackson and Diane Christian collection*, AFC 2011/009, finding aid (September 2020), especially pp. 4–6, 14–21 and 57–62: https://findingaids.loc.gov/exist_collections/ead3pdf/afc/2018/af018002.pdf


## 17. Texas prison farms and Huntsville penitentiary — Bruce Jackson field recordings, 1964–66

- **HIGH PRIORITY for item-level work-song recordings and source-tape provenance.** The LOC finding aid for AFC 2011/009 identifies recordings made at Ellis, Wayne, Ramsey, Retrieve farms and Huntsville penitentiary in summer 1964. The inventory gives specific singers, group names, song titles and work actions, and distinguishes source recordings from second-generation samplers and transcription reels.
- **SR0782**, reel 46, “Spirituals / Huntsville,” 6 July 1964: songs 1–4 are explicitly recorded at Retrieve Farm #1; songs 5–8 at “The Walls” (Huntsville). The first four include “Lemon Jefferson,” “49 [?] Hammers Ringing” (Virgil Asburn), “Hammer Ring” (White [?]) and “Run Rattler Run”; the Huntsville portion includes “Just a Closer Walk With Thee” and “Take the Lord With You.”
- **SR1040–SR1041**, reels 47–48, 6 July 1964: gospel performances at Huntsville / The Walls. Named singers include Morris G. Minter, Paul T. Parker, Leon C. Evans, Willie Lewis and Odie (possibly Delie) Lee Holmes; songs include “You Been Good to Me,” “While the Blood's Running Warm in Your Veins,” “Got Heaven on My Mind” and “I Shall Not Be Moved.” Dutch Erikson is separately named on reel 48.
- **SR0696**, “Negro prison worksong, blues and spirituals / Texas,” July 1964: described as a second-generation sampler from Ellis, Wayne, Ramsey, Retrieve farms and Huntsville; includes “Raise Em Up Higher,” “If You See My Mother,” “Move Along Gator,” “Go Down Old Hannah,” “Make a Longtime Man Feel Bad,” “Cold Penitentiary Blues,” “My Last Mile” and “Rattler.” Treat this as a derivative sampler, not the original recording.
- **SR0707**, “Negro worksongs from Texas state prison farms tape #1,” August 1965, 19 songs; **SR0706** is a transcription reel with item-level song / singer / work-action notes and explicit source reel cross-references. Examples: “You Should a Been on the River in 1910” (Sherrod), “Drop 'Em Down” (Chinaman and group), “Roberta (I)” (Mack Maze), “Long Hot Summer Days” (Tippen, Veasley and group), “Captain Don't Feel Sorry for a Longtime Man” (Phillips and group), “No More Cane on the Brazos/Godamighty” (Tippen), “I Got a Record” (Veasley), “Mack's Blues (I Told the Captain)” (Maze), “Jody's Got My Wife and Gone” (Willie Sandell and group), “Mr. Tom Moore” (Jackson), “Every Time I Get a Letter from the Governor” (Jackson and Young), “Choppin' Charlie” (Jackson and Young), “Plumb the Line,” “Fallin' Down (Timber Getting' Limber),” “Rattler,” “Down the Line,” “So Soon This Evening,” “Hammer Ring,” “John Henry,” “Yon' Come Roberta” and “This Old Hammer Killed John Henry.”
- **SR0708–SR0710** are described as second-generation dubs / sampler sides. SR0709 and SR0710 give additional titles and names including “Down the Line” (Page), “Hammer Ring” (Scott), “Drop 'Em Down” (Tippett), “Move Along Farther” (Jackson), “Crooked Foot John” (Chinaman), “Jack o Diamonds” (Jackson), “Poor Boy #2” (Smith), “Black Gal” (Chinaman and group), and “I'm Gonna Write My Mama” (Tippett). Cross-check these against source reels SR0881–SR0888, SR1033, SR0862, SR0402–SR0405 and SR0390–SR0393 before treating them as independent recordings.
- The same LOC finding aid lists two separate related collections: **Bruce Jackson Texas prison songs, 1964 (AFC 1965/011)** and **Bruce Jackson Texas prison songs, 1965–1966 (AFC 1967/009)**. These are distinct from the 2011/009 collection and deserve their own catalogue audit; do not assume they are duplicates.
- **Research priority:** locate original reels and compare with second-generation samplers, published Jackson work-song selections and later commercial releases. Focus on less-circulated individual voices, work-song variants and the exact prison-farm context; do not claim obscurity merely because a recording is archival. Respect the racialised forced-labour history and the historic catalogue language; use current contextual descriptions while preserving original titles as quoted metadata.
- **Access:** AFC 2011/009 is open to research by request; copying/publication may be restricted. The related AFC 1965/011 and AFC 1967/009 collections should be requested separately. No audio audition or archive request has been made.
- Source: LOC, *Bruce Jackson and Diane Christian collection*, AFC 2011/009, finding aid, especially pp. 30 and 60–62; related collections listed pp. 1–2: https://findingaids.loc.gov/exist_collections/ead3pdf/afc/2018/af018002.pdf

## 18. Anders Enevig's wider Danish sound archive — Copenhagen and itinerant communities, 1958–83

- **PROMISING archive-level route; the best-known Nørrebro subset is CONTEXT ONLY.** Anders Enevig's nearly 400 hours of recordings are held by Dansk Folkemindesamling. A recent research thesis describes the collection as 205 tapes from 1958–1983, covering songs and reportage among Copenhagen's klunsere and subsistence drinkers, circus people, Roma, travellers, and other overlooked communities. These are distinct contexts and must not be collapsed into one social category.
- The 1958 “Prinser og Vagabonder” project began with 18 hours of compressed song and conversation recordings among people in a Nørrebro “klunserhus.” Its songs were published in the book and later in the LP/cassette *Go'morgen Karl Baj!* (DFS 3), so that subset is already visible and should not be prioritised simply for obscurity.
- The stronger route is the wider 205-tape / nearly 400-hour archive, especially material not selected for the 1985 publication, recordings of travelling communities, and less circulated local repertoires. Exact DFS item IDs, track-level metadata, access restrictions and performer consent status have not yet been established.
- Historical terminology in older sources can be derogatory or police-centred. Retain it only in bibliographic titles or direct archival description; contextualise Enevig's position as a police officer and collector and seek community-informed descriptions where possible.
- Sources: Lex / Dansk Biografisk Leksikon biography (nearly 400 hours; collection at Dansk Folkemindesamling): https://biografiskleksikon.lex.dk/Anders_Enevig ; research thesis (205 tapes, 1958–83): https://rucforsk.ruc.dk/ws/files/81615823/LMVB_Specialet_.pdf ; Svend Nielsen's 1985 *Go'morgen Karl Baj!* publication details: https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/yearbook-for-traditional-music/article/abs/ethnomusicology-and-folk-music-research-in-denmark/1B2F611D426BD4B8482AFFDB73490011 ; Svenskt visarkiv explains its prison-song project was inspired by Enevig's Copenhagen project: https://old.capricemusic.se/svensktvisarkiv/artikel/inspelningarna/?lang=en


## 19. Ericson Acosta — Calbayog Sub-provincial Jail, Samar, Philippines; *Prison Sessions* (2011)

- **CONTEXT ONLY / verified in-jail recording, but already circulated through an advocacy campaign and CD.** Independent blogs were searched for prison-made recordings and surfaced the Free Ericson Acosta campaign blog and Renato Reyes's “like a rolling stone” blog. These document two live, raw sessions made while Acosta was detained at Calbayog Sub-provincial Jail, with BAYAN secretary-general and musician Renato Reyes Jr. playing guitar.
- **Session 1:** 6 April 2011, “Unplugged, Unrehearsed...Under Detention.” Nine listed tracks include “Dahil,” “Magsasama, Magkasama,” “Alamat,” and “Kumustahan” (Acosta / Alay Sining), alongside songs by Binky Lampano, Renato Reyes Jr. / Alay Sining and Tambisan sa Sining. The campaign blog describes the recording as live/raw voice and guitar; it was posted online and made downloadable.
- **Session 2:** 20 September 2011, again at Calbayog Sub-provincial Jail, with Reyes. Tracklist includes Acosta originals “Paalam,” “Balang Araw,” “Duyan” and “Pambihira,” plus songs by Renato Reyes Jr. / Alay Sining, Jess Santiago and Eraserheads. An unfinished song, “Isang Minutong Katahimikan,” was written inside the detention facility and concerns extrajudicial killings in Eastern Visayas.
- A CD edition was announced July 2011; the blog says proceeds supported Acosta's legal defence. Contemporary coverage confirms the sessions were recorded during a jail visit and posted online. Acosta was released in 2013; later reporting states he was killed in 2022. This is important as a documented political-prisoner music case, but its public advocacy circulation and CD issue make it a weaker obscurity candidate than unissued prison tapes.
- **Provenance nuance:** these are in-jail performances recorded during visits by an outside collaborator, not clandestine self-recordings by Acosta alone. Distinguish the jail sessions from later live performances and recordings of his compositions by other groups.
- **Access/rights:** original SoundCloud links were listed by the campaign; verify current authorised availability and rights with the campaign/recording participants. Do not assume that the CD campaign or old downloadable links confer reuse permission.
- Sources: Free Ericson Acosta campaign blog, session 1 and tracklist: https://freeacosta.blogspot.com/2011/04/ ; session 2: https://freeacosta.blogspot.com/2011/09/ ; CD release and tracklist: https://freeacosta.blogspot.com/2011/07/prison-sessions-cd.html ; Renato Reyes's contemporaneous account: https://natoreyes.wordpress.com/category/music/ ; Samar News contemporary report: https://samarnews.com/news2011/apr/b118.htm ; later biographical context: https://phkule.org/article/699/who-is-ericson-acosta/

## 20. Independent blogs and small-web discovery conduits — source register, not recording entries

These sites/directories are useful for finding rare releases, scene histories, personal archives and specialist collectors. None of the following is itself a verified Insane Music recording candidate unless an individual post is separately documented.

- **Terminal Escape** (daily cassette blog since 2009): https://terminalescape.blogspot.com/ — first-hand cassette transfers and short release notes, with country/genre labels. Search terms can be misleading: “ASYLUM” is a Stoke-on-Trent anarcho/noise band (not an asylum recording); “DOG PRISON” is a band name; “POLITICAL ASYLUM” is an anarcho-punk band. The blog itself says posts are rips from tapes physically held by the blogger and encourages acquisition of originals. Treat as a cassette lead index and cross-check each tape.
- **Archaic Inventions**: https://archaicinventions.blogspot.com/ — obscure, out-of-print experimental, avant-garde, home-taping and cassette releases; author says transfers are made from their own copies and invites contextual corrections. Search by environment, collective, commune, squatting, spiritual group and country; no candidate admitted from this pass.
- **1208 North Fuller Ave Apt 1**: https://1208fullerave.blogspot.com/ — physical-copy-only reviews of noise, power electronics, post-industrial, experimental and outsider-art music. Useful for label/catalogue/artist connections, but not a provenance archive.
- **Anomaly Index**: https://anomalyindex.com/ — conceptual anomalies, outsider and obscure music, with original interviews and research; useful for following collector/artist networks, but not a substitute for verifying institutional context.
- **Heavy Leather cassette archive**: https://heavyleatherblog.wordpress.com/category/cassette-archive/ — careful analogue transfer notes for overlooked DIY/post-punk cassettes; useful for transfer metadata and private-press trail building.
- **Weirdnet directory**: https://directory.weirdnet.org/ — hand-indexed small-web directory with categories for music scenes, music, archives, personal sites and small web. Relevant listed examples include the Archive of St. Louis Punk (https://stlpunkarchive.omeka.net/), BR Punk Player (https://brpunkplayer.neocities.org/), and Alice Rabbit's experimental-music blog (https://alicerabbit.blogspot.com/). The St. Louis archive preserves scene artefacts and collections, while BR Punk Player salvages links from older Brazilian underground punk blogs; neither is yet evidence of music made in a marginal institution.
- **Marginalia Search**: https://search.marginalia.nu/ — independent search engine focused on non-commercial/personal websites; valuable for discovering pages missed by commercial search. **Wiby** (linked by smolweb resources) also targets small websites.
- **Neocities music directory**: https://neocities.org/browse?tag=music and https://neocities.org/browse?tag=smallweb — browse individual musician pages, DIY archives and link hubs, but screen carefully for active original music versus historical recordings.
- **Small-web directories and context:** https://smallweb.cc/ ; https://smolweb.org/resources.html ; https://indieweb.org/smol_web ; https://cybernaut-zone.neocities.org/ . These are link-discovery tools, not music archives.
- **Blog-history / underground preservation context:** OCCII's “Filing the Underground” discusses the role of independent cassette blogs in surfacing marginal releases and the paradox between preservation and unauthorised redistribution: https://occii.org/filing-the-underground/
- **Blog triage rule:** only elevate a post into an environment lead when it supplies a specific recording, credible location/date, maker/performer and corroborating trail. A strange band name or an album's aesthetic is not enough. Search results in this pass included multiple false positives (“ASYLUM,” “DOG PRISON,” “POLITICAL ASYLUM,” “Released From The Hospital”); these were explicitly screened out.


## 21. Ohio Penitentiary 511 Jazz Ensemble — Ohio State Penitentiary, USA; *Hard Luck Soul* (1971)

- **VERIFIED recording / CONTEXT ONLY for obscurity.** Independent cassette blog Terminal Escape surfaced the session, then Jazzman Records' Bandcamp reissue and distributor notes provided a much stronger source trail. The 511 Jazz Ensemble consisted entirely of men serving time at Ohio State Penitentiary. The group was formed in 1971; its number refers to the prison's PO box. The ensemble played in the prison yard on holidays.
- The visiting Ohio State University band heard the inmates play, returned with microphones and studio equipment, and recorded the ensemble in the auditorium of the prison chapel. The session resulted in a privately pressed four-track LP, *Hard Luck Soul* (1971), later reissued by Jazzman in 2012. This is not the same thing as a prison band merely performing for visitors: the released record is the inmate ensemble's own performance, although the recording was facilitated by outside musicians and funders.
- Original track sequence: “Psych City” (10:53), “Mantra Dance” (4:57), “Counterry Bosa Davan” (11:44; spelling variants “Counterry Bosa Devan” and “Counterry Bosa Davan” in sources), “Java Man” (5:24). Logan Rollins composed/arranged the four pieces and played alto saxophone; Reynard Birtha played trumpet. Other credited players: Charles Peterson, Ricky Lewis, Dave Bennett, Richard Stokes, George Williams, Peter H. Gunn, Julius T. Johnson, John Grady, Richard Wise, Jesse Barrett and Sonny Ward.
- **Obscurity judgement:** the 1971 private pressing was extremely rare, but the 2012 Jazzman Holy Grail reissue has made it internationally visible and streamable. Retain as a benchmark/context case, not a newly discovered obscure release. The original label/pressing details, exact recording date, recording engineer and the musicians' full biographical details need further confirmation.
- **Ethical / rights note:** Jazzman Bandcamp states all rights reserved. Use authorised listening/reissue metadata and do not assume blog rips are reusable. The reissue account says Reynard Birtha never received payment and had never seen or owned a copy of the LP; this deserves careful attribution rather than repeating promotional romanticisation.
- Sources: Terminal Escape blog discovery (June 2019): https://terminalescape.blogspot.com/2019/06/ ; Jazzman official Bandcamp reissue and label account: https://jazzmanholygrailseries.bandcamp.com/album/hard-luck-soul ; Jazzman/Kudos release page: https://jazzman.kudosrecords.co.uk/release/jmancd052/ohio-penitentiary-511-ensemble-hard-luck-soul ; contemporary retailer metadata/tracklist: https://www.roughtrade.com/product/ohio-penitentiary-511-jazz-ensemble/hard-luck-soul

## 22. Uzuakoli Leprosy Centre — Abia State, Nigeria; Choir of the Leprosy Colony recordings of Ikoli Harcourt Whyte

- **VERIFIED posthumous choir recordings / PROMISING archive and recording trail.** Ikoli Harcourt Whyte (1905–1977) composed more than 200 Igbo-language hymns, developing his musical practice while a patient at the Methodist Leprosy Colony at Uzuakoli. He later conducted and taught choirs there. A Music In Africa history states that although Whyte did not record an album himself, the Choir of the Leprosy Colony, Uzuakoli recorded his compositions after his death in 1977; it also mentions a 1999 recording by the St Louis Missouri African Choir.
- John Manton's 2015 radio documentary, “Uzuakoli in Music and Medicine,” explicitly uses original vinyl recordings of Whyte's choir remastered for broadcast, contemporary arrangements by his biographer Achinivu Kanu Achinivu, oral testimony and field recordings of sung/spoken passages. This establishes that original choir recordings survive in some form and were available to the programme maker; it does not yet establish the original record's title, label, date, tracklist, recording location or whether all performers were former/current patients.
- **Next verification:** identify and locate the original vinyl edition(s) by the Choir of the Leprosy Colony, Uzuakoli; establish date, label, matrix/catalogue number, tracklist, performers and recording venue. Contact John Manton and/or Achinivu Kanu Achinivu for the original record's provenance and whether copies or transfers are held by the choir association, Methodist Church Nigeria, GLRA or the Uzuakoli centre. Distinguish the original colony choir from later school/church choirs and the 1999 St Louis recording.
- **Obscurity judgement:** the compositions are well known in Nigerian church music and have received recent heritage-preservation work; the original Uzuakoli choir recording may be less visible internationally and is the actual target. This is not a claim that the composer or his music are unknown.
- **Ethical/context note:** use Hansen's disease / leprosy centre as historically accurate context without reducing Whyte to illness. He chose to remain at Uzuakoli after being declared cured in 1949. Avoid sensational language about bodily impairment and preserve Igbo titles/orthography with knowledgeable review.
- Sources: Music In Africa history, including posthumous Uzuakoli choir recording and 1999 St Louis choir: https://musicinafrica.net/fr/node/13683 ; John Manton's documentary description and original-vinyl provenance: https://africanbiosciences.wordpress.com/publications/radio-uzuakoli-in-music-and-medicine/ ; Sasakawa Leprosy Initiative 2022 preservation project: https://sasakawaleprosyinitiative.org/latest-updates/initiative-news/4801/ ; Leprosy Mission biographical profile: https://www.lepra.org.uk/centenary/people/details/ikoli-harcourt-whyte


## 23. Uzuakoli / Harcourt Whyte discography clarification — Nigeria

A further search surfaced a possible commercial LP, *Harcourt Whyte And Choir*, listed as a 1975 Nigerian issue and as a 1977 Tabansi reissue, catalogue ARC 1059. A collectors' auction archive independently lists the 1975 issue and a second LP, *Harcourt Whyte & Choir Vol. 2*, ARC 1106 (1974). A low-quality download-index page supplies a 12-track list for *Harcourt Whyte And Choir*, credits Nnamdi Olebara as leader/vocalist and says it was produced at Arc Studio, Lagos; treat those details as provisional, not authoritative. HighlifeNG pages from 2021 identify Tabansi releases under the Harcourt Whyte and Choir name and mention Nnamdi Olebara and Item Choral Party, suggesting the catalogue may cover multiple choir projects.

Important distinction: Music In Africa says the Choir of the Leprosy Colony, Uzuakoli recorded Whyte's compositions after his death in 1977, while the 1975 commercial LP predates his death. Do not identify ARC 1059/ARC 1106 as the posthumous colony-choir recording without label scans, personnel credits, dates and provenance. John Manton's 2015 programme independently confirms that original vinyl of Harcourt Whyte's choir was used, but does not identify it in the page description. The 2014 digital compilation *The Original & Greatest Hits of Harcourt Whyte and Choir* is ℗ 2014 Tabansi Records, 22 tracks; this is a reissue/compilation lead, not proof of the original colony recording.

Status: PROMISING discographic lead; exact link to Uzuakoli patient choir NOT VERIFIED. Next steps: inspect physical label/cover images and matrix/catalogue details; compare ARC 1106 and ARC 1059 tracklists; establish Nnamdi Olebara and Item Choral Party affiliations; identify whether any session was recorded at Uzuakoli and whether choir members were residents/patients. Sources: https://www.popsike.com/php/quicksearch.php?bidsfrom=&bidsthru=&endfrom=&endthru=&eprice=&fcatno=&flabel=&incldescr=&layout=lv&pagenum=11&searchtext=+arc&sortord=&sprice= ; https://flac.vodila.net/51448/harcourt-whyte-and-choir-harcourt-whyte-and-choir-download-flac.html ; https://highlifeng.com/harcourt-whyte-obu-onye-kere-uwa-nkea/ ; https://highlifeng.com/harcourt-whyte-nna-chebe-nwa-gi/ ; https://music.apple.com/us/album/the-original-greatest-hits-of-harcourt-whyte-and-choir/828916188 ; https://musicinafrica.net/fr/node/13683 ; https://africanbiosciences.wordpress.com/publications/radio-uzuakoli-in-music-and-medicine/

## 24. Culion Leper Colony Band — Culion, Palawan, Philippines

Heritage documentation identifies a brass band organised by Fr. José Tarrago, initially training boys in the Angelitos dormitory who were affected by leprosy; a later heritage report calls it the Culion Leper Colony Band and describes it as composed of younger boys with leprosy, trained to replace members of the official colony band. A 2005 account says Tarrago organised a brass band that became the official colony band and gave regular concerts. A 2018 presentation says the band performed at colony occasions and celebrations and held bi-weekly concerts. This is strong evidence of patient musical activity, but no sound recording of this specific band has been located. Do not promote it to a recording candidate until audio, disc, cylinder, film soundtrack or broadcast evidence emerges.

Status: CONTEXT / RECORDING NOT FOUND. Search next: Culion Museum and Archives, National Archives of the Philippines, Jesuit archives, Philippine Bureau of Health/US colonial records, local oral histories and private family collections. Sources: https://www.mowcapunesco.org/wp-content/uploads/Culion-Leprosy-Archives-2018.pdf ; https://www.philstar.com/opinion/2005/10/10/301064/reflections-veranda/amp/ ; https://www.shf.or.jp/wp-content/uploads/2019/12/Philippines2018_web.pdf ; https://www.ethnomusicology.org/resource/resmgr/2008_annual_meeting/abstract_book_11.06.09.pdf


## 25. Harcourt Whyte: distinguish the Uzuakoli hospital choir from later touring/commercial choirs

Opened the International Leprosy Association's Harcourt Whyte entry and the original John Manton programme page. The ILA page says Whyte joined the Uzuakoli hospital choir and became its conductor; it specifically refers to two pieces sung by the “Choir of the Uzuakoli Leprosy Colony.” Manton's 2015 programme description confirms it uses “original vinyl as remastered recordings of Harcourt Whyte's choir,” alongside later arrangements by biographer Achinivu Kanu Achinivu, oral testimony and field recordings, but does not identify the vinyl title, label, performers or date. The programme itself is available via Mixcloud (link on page).
