Separately, HighlifeNG's 2021 Tabansi posts credit Nnamdi Olebara and Item Choral Party for songs branded “Harcourt Whyte and Choir.” This should be treated as a distinct commercial/touring choir identity unless primary evidence connects it to the Uzuakoli resident choir. A Nigerian profile quotes Achinivu describing himself as going to the colony to hear Whyte's choir and notes Whyte toured with his choir; this suggests the choir ecosystem extended beyond the patient community. Do not assume all performers on Tabansi LPs were patients or residents. The 1975 ARC 1059 LP and 1974 ARC 1106 Vol. 2 catalogue listings predate Whyte's death (May 1977); they cannot straightforwardly be the posthumous colony-choir recording described by Music In Africa.

Status: original recording of Uzuakoli colony choir is strongly attested by ILA and Manton, but the record itself remains unidentified. Research target: listen to/inspect Manton's Mixcloud programme and note the source vinyl segments; seek label/cover or archival copies and compare repertoire with the two colony-choir pieces. Do not download/reuse unofficial blog rips. Sources: https://leprosyhistory.org/reading-material/harcourt-whyte-broadcast/ ; https://africanbiosciences.wordpress.com/publications/radio-uzuakoli-in-music-and-medicine/ ; https://highlifeng.com/harcourt-whyte-obu-onye-kere-uwa-nkea/ ; https://highlifeng.com/harcourt-whyte-nna-chebe-nwa-gi/ ; https://blueprint.ng/the-nigerian-who-composed-hymns-from-a-leprosy-colony/ ; https://musicinafrica.net/node/13683

## 26. Culion band — dated image evidence and archive trail

Additional evidence: a Salesian archival publication reproduces a 1926 photograph captioned “With the band of the leper colony of Culion,” showing a band group with instruments. A University of Michigan history exhibit identifies a separate Dean C. Worcester photograph captioned “The leper band” (undated), from the Dean C. Worcester Photographic Collection, UMMA 58Q068, and describes the band as composed entirely of patients. A history of colonial medicine cites a related NARA photo reference RG 350-BS-1-4-177 (BS 995). These are visual/photographic records only, not sound evidence. They strengthen the historical record and give concrete archival identifiers for further provenance research.

Status remains CONTEXT / RECORDING NOT FOUND. Search NARA RG 350 Bureau of Science records for adjacent moving-image/sound materials, but do not infer audio from the still-photo series. Sources: https://db.archiviosalesiano.net/files/original/551954/ASC-LIB-VAR02-507.pdf ; https://philippines.michiganintheworld.history.lsa.umich.edu/s/exhibit/page/the-island-of-no-return ; https://dokumen.pub/colonial-pathologies-american-tropical-medicine-race-and-hygiene-in-the-philippines.html ; https://www.mowcapunesco.org/wp-content/uploads/Culion-Leprosy-Archives-2018.pdf


## 27. Uzuakoli: track-level discographic cross-check

The ILA page *Leprosy and Cultural Production* and its dedicated *Harcourt Whyte Broadcast* page identify two especially important compositions sung by the Choir of the Uzuakoli Leper Colony: “Atulegwu” and “Umu gi emebiwo uwa gi.” The 1975 commercial LP listing *Harcourt Whyte And Choir* (ARC 1059 reissue 1977) includes “Atulegwu” as A5 and a differently rendered “Ayi Emebi Wo Uwagi” as B5. A low-authority download index credits Nnamdi Olebara as leader/vocals and says produced at Arc Studio, Lagos. This is a plausible track-title match, but it does NOT establish that the LP is the posthumous Uzuakoli colony choir recording. Music In Africa says the Choir of the Leprosy Colony recording was made after Whyte's death in 1977, whereas the LP is dated 1975 (and reissued 1977). The LP may be an earlier or separate commercial choir recording, perhaps featuring a different ensemble. Do not merge these discographies.

The 1999 St. Louis African Chorus recording is separately catalogued as *AENaO*, with 14 songs including “Atulegwu,” “Chebe Mo Nna,” “Onyinye Chuku,” “Tikuenu Jehova,” “Nw' Oge Nta,” “Obu Chuku,” “Otuto Nke Chuku,” “Kp'Ekpere” and “Ihe Ndia Di Njo.” This gives a useful repertoire comparator but is not the Uzuakoli recording. A Nigerian 2025 download-blog post offers an MP3 called “Atulegwu” under Harcourt Whyte's name, but without credible provenance it is not evidence of the original LP/colony recording and must not be treated as cleared audio.

Status: PROMISING / source record still unidentified. Next: compare the audio in John Manton's 2015 broadcast against the 1975 ARC 1059 LP and the 1999 St. Louis *AENaO*; seek original sleeve/label scans and primary credits. Exact question: is the 1975 LP performed by the Uzuakoli patient choir, a later school/touring choir, or another ensemble? Sources: https://leprosyhistory.org/reading-material/leprosy-and-cultural-production/ ; https://leprosyhistory.org/reading-material/harcourt-whyte-broadcast/ ; https://wwws.musicinafrica.net/node/13683 ; https://flac.vodila.net/51448/harcourt-whyte-and-choir-harcourt-whyte-and-choir-download-flac.html ; https://singers.com/item/St__Louis_African_Chorus/AENaO/7143c/132004 ; https://loadedvibes.com.ng/2025/07/24/harcourt-whyte-atulegwu/

## 28. Culion: Smithsonian audiovisual record is silent, not sound

The Smithsonian Human Studies Film Archives catalogues *Roger Hilsman films of the Philippines* (HSFA.1981.02): five 35mm black-and-white silent films, circa 1929, 116 minutes total, including scenes of the Culion colony. The collection is open for research, but the archive says to contact it for access copies and that original audiovisual material may not be played; rights require repository contact. Because the films are explicitly silent, they cannot preserve the Culion band as a synchronised sound performance. They may nevertheless contain visual footage of the band or musical occasions. This is a potentially useful visual identification route only.

Status remains CONTEXT / no band recording found. Source: https://collections.si.edu/search/record/ead_collection:sova-hsfa-1981-02


## 29. Liberation — Massachusetts prison band, Norfolk, c. 1981

A particularly strong small-web discovery: Under the Radar Recording's archive page “Liberation” publishes five audio mixes and a first-person production account. The author says the session took place at “Massachusetts Correction Facilities in Norfolk, MA” around 1981; band was four incarcerated people (bass, drums, guitar, lead singer; all sang backing vocals); Andy Pratt produced. Crew had an eight-hour window to bring in and search equipment, assemble an 8-track 1/2-inch Tascam studio in a small hall with stage, record five songs (all first takes except two takes of “Jesus Is Love”), and finish before the musicians returned to their cells. The mixes were later found on a white, unlabelled cheap cassette used as quick reference mixes at Perfect Crime Studios, Watertown, MA. Track titles: “You've Turned My Life Around”; “Jesus Is Love”; “If You Need A Friend”; “If You Have Faith”; “Dr Jesus.” The page links MP3s for all five tracks.

This is an unusually good candidate: direct production testimony, identifiable recording date/location, all performers incarcerated, and apparently obscure/no commercial release identified. The author is likely a recording engineer associated with the session; the page does not name the four musicians or fully clarify the author's role. Avoid assigning a prison name more specifically than the source supports until corroborated (likely the Massachusetts Correctional Institution at Norfolk, but source says “Massachusetts Correction Facilities in Norfolk”). Andy Pratt is credited as producer by the archive page. The page says 8-hour session; source cassette is a quick-reference mix, not necessarily multitrack masters. Rights are not stated; availability of MP3 streams is not permission to reuse. Contact the page owner/producer for identification, provenance, consent and rights if the project later seeks publication.

Status: HIGH PRIORITY / recording and source account directly available; performer identities and rights unknown. Sources: https://undertheradarrecording.com/archive-recordings/liberation/ ; track audio links on that page: “You've Turned My Life Around” https://undertheradarrecording.com/wp-content/uploads/2013/03/Youve-Turned-My-Life-Around.mp3 ; “Jesus Is Love” https://undertheradarrecording.com/wp-content/uploads/2013/03/Jesus-Is-Love.mp3 ; “If You Need A Friend” https://undertheradarrecording.com/wp-content/uploads/2013/03/If-You-Need-A-Friend.mp3 ; “If You Have Faith” https://undertheradarrecording.com/wp-content/uploads/2013/03/If-You-Have-Faith.mp3 ; “Dr Jesus” https://undertheradarrecording.com/wp-content/uploads/2013/03/Dr-Jesus.mp3

## 30. Culion Radio Station — 1920 broadcast infrastructure, not yet a recording

The *Report of the Philippine Health Service for 1920* states that the Culion Radio Station was inaugurated on 19 November 1920, intended to connect the isolated colony with the rest of the archipelago and mitigate isolation. The report describes colony celebrations with concerts, theatre and literary programmes, but does not say that the band was broadcast or that broadcasts were recorded. A later heritage presentation documents a brass band that grew from the Angelitos dormitory and performed at colony occasions and regular concerts. This creates a possible avenue for broadcast-history research, but not evidence of an audio artefact. Early 1920s radio airchecks are rare generally; do not infer a surviving recording from station operation alone.

Status: CONTEXT / potential radio archive lead only; no recording found. Search Bureau of Posts/telecommunications files, Philippine Health Service annual reports, station logs, private receiving sets, American colonial radio press and contemporary newspapers. Sources: 1920 annual report, Culion section pp. 88–89: https://archive.org/details/acw9791.1920.001.umich.edu/page/88/mode/2up ; report text mirror https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/7/7d/Report_%28IA_acw9791.1920.001.umich.edu%29.pdf ; band history https://www.shf.or.jp/en/wp-content/uploads/sites/2/2019/12/Philippines2018_web.pdf


## 31. Liberation — facility attribution correction and contact route

A re-check of the original Under the Radar Recording page confirms it says “Massachusetts Correction Facilities in Norfolk, MA” (around 1981), while the site's parent Archive Recordings index labels the same entry “Mass. Corrections Facilities, Shirley, MA 1981?” The index explicitly adds a question mark. The facility attribution is therefore internally inconsistent. Do not present either Norfolk or Shirley as confirmed. Safest current metadata: Massachusetts prison facility, c. 1981; exact institution unresolved. The homepage identifies the site's operator/contact as Jeff Chitouras and publicly lists jeff@undertheradarrecording.com. This is a potential route to first-hand clarification, but no contact has been made. The site page itself says four inmates and names Andy Pratt as producer; it does not identify the musicians. Follow-up questions: which institution and exact date; was this a prison program or one-off visit; can Andy Pratt corroborate; do session paperwork, multitrack reels or original cassette survive; who owns the recording and what permissions/consent exist for archival publication; are former performers identifiable and do they wish to be named?

Status remains HIGH PRIORITY / five mixes and first-person account published; exact facility, date, personnel, ownership and consent unresolved. Source: https://undertheradarrecording.com/archive-recordings/liberation/ ; parent index https://undertheradarrecording.com/archive-recordings/ ; contact/homepage https://undertheradarrecording.com/


## Massachusetts prison recording *Liberation* — new Norfolk cultural-programme archival route (2026-11-15)
The recording engineer's primary account still explicitly says Massachusetts Correctional Facilities in Norfolk, around 1981; four incarcerated musicians (bass, drums, guitar, lead singer; all backing vocals); five songs; an eight-track half-inch Tascam session completed during an eight-hour prison visit; reference mixes survived on an unlabelled cassette. Titles: “You've Turned My Life Around,” “Jesus Is Love,” “If You Need A Friend,” “If You Have Faith,” “Dr Jesus.” Source: https://undertheradarrecording.com/archive-recordings/liberation/ . However, the site's archive index has reportedly also labelled the entry “Shirley, MA 1981?”, so exact facility remains unresolved; preserve the discrepancy rather than silently choosing one.

New archival route: Northeastern University's “We Raise Our Voices” page documents the Elma Lewis School of Fine Arts MCI Norfolk Prison Theatre Program, established 1970. The programme trained incarcerated men in theatre production, including music, and says participants created productions with responsibility for writing, acting, music and production. The associated Elma Lewis School of Fine Arts records (M043, 1954–1992) are held at Northeastern; its finding aid notes the MCI Norfolk programme. This is a plausible context/source for locating 1981 prison arts contacts or records, but does not yet connect *Liberation* to the programme. Sources: https://voices.library.northeastern.edu/theater-and-dance/mci-norfolk-prison-theatre-program/ ; https://archivesspace.library.northeastern.edu/repositories/2/resources/827 .

Other contemporaneous institutional leads: 1981 Department of Correction annual reports for both MCI Norfolk and MCI Shirley are listed in the State Library guide to Jack H. Backman Papers; the Massachusetts Archives holds state-institution records and gives a reference contact route. Sources: https://archives.lib.state.ma.us/server/api/core/bitstreams/9f02093a-a856-4406-9941-214fee6f2ef7/content ; https://www.sec.state.ma.us/divisions/archives/collections/state-institutions.htm . A UMass Boston finding aid lists a “Radio Free Rehabilitation Group” at MCI Norfolk in 1978, potentially relevant to prison communications/cultural activity, but not proof of a music connection: https://openarchives.umb.edu/digital/api/collection/p15774coll8/id/220/download .

Assessment: retain HIGH PRIORITY as an unusually obscure, first-hand-documented prison recording. Facility (Norfolk vs Shirley), exact date, identities, programme affiliation, surviving master/source-tape status, ownership, rights and consent remain unresolved. Next archival enquiry should ask Northeastern about M043 / MCI Norfolk programme files and whether any audio or 1980–82 programme documentation survives; compare with State Library 1981 reports. No contact made, no identities inferred, and no reuse permission assumed.


### Northeastern University M203 / M043 archival audit — *Liberation* and MCI Norfolk (2026-11-15)
A closer inspection of Northeastern's Larry Blumsack Papers (M203) finding aid narrows the value of this collection. It lists a specific VHS file in Box 8: “Elma Lewis Film Project — ‘We Hold These Truths’ ‘The Elma Lewis School’,” described at collection level as documenting the Elma Lewis School's Technical Theater Training Program work with Black inmates at MCI Norfolk in the early 1970s. This is a potentially valuable contextual film and may show the programme's methods, but it predates the circa-1981 *Liberation* session and is not evidence of that recording. M203 also has 93 cassettes of Blumsack's WADN radio show *Where It's Art*, explicitly dated 8 Dec 1993–12 Feb 1997; this is too late to be a direct source for the 1981 session absent an identified retrospective interview. Box 8 also contains a 1982 U-Matic item “New England Today: Blumsack Segment,” but no prison connection is described. Sources: https://archivesspace.library.northeastern.edu/repositories/2/resources/975 ; https://archivesspace.library.northeastern.edu/repositories/2/top_containers/17914 .

The Elma Lewis School of Fine Arts records (M043) document the MCI Norfolk Prison Theatre Program, established in 1970, including training in stage direction, stage design, sound, lighting, music and acting. The finding aid says most 1970s records survived but some 1980s records did not; student/personnel records in Boxes 3–11 are closed for 75 years. This limits direct access to likely 1981-era materials. Source: https://archivesspace.library.northeastern.edu/repositories/2/resources/827 .

A contemporaneous source reproduced in U.S. Congressional hearing material describes the programme's origin in 1969, training for 104 men in art, dance, Black history and economics, and inmate-created productions. The 1972 anthology *Who Took the Weight? Black Voices from Norfolk Prison* is another documentary source for the programme's creative output, but is a text anthology rather than an audio release. Sources: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CHRG-95shrg35318/pdf/CHRG-95shrg35318.pdf ; https://libraryguides.bennington.edu/blackstudies/blacklibrary .

Assessment update: Northeastern is useful context and a route to the earlier Norfolk programme, but the 93-cassette radio collection is not a promising direct route to *Liberation* because it dates 1993–97. Highest-value Northeastern object is the “We Hold These Truths” VHS in M203 Box 8; highest-value *Liberation* verification remains engineer/producer testimony and the unresolved Norfolk/Shirley conflict. No contact made; no permission inferred.


### RISK — Kentucky State Penitentiary, Eddyville, Kentucky, USA; original prison cassette recordings, c. 1981–84
A first-person band history on the group's own site describes RISK as four incarcerated men at Kentucky State Penitentiary (KSP), Eddyville. Members are identified only by first names/pseudonyms on the public pages: Dexter (lead guitar/lead vocals), Danny (guitar/vocals), Joe (bass/vocals), J. Wayne (drums/vocals). Three began composing/playing in the prison; Joe joined after transfer. The band rehearsed in a small prison band room and performed twice inside the institution (gym boxing-ring open house and visiting area); the site says they never performed outside prison. After approximately 24 months of work, they reportedly wrote/arranged over 100 songs and recorded about 50 on cassette over roughly four months in 1984, before Dexter's May 1984 parole. Joe left the prison with the cassette tapes, lyrics and copyright documents in November 1984. The cassettes were rediscovered after the band reunited in 2016–17; tapes were digitised and some early music made available online. The band site says the tapes had not been played for decades before rediscovery. Sources: https://www.riskarmy.com/ ; https://www.riskarmy.com/history .

Assessment: PROMISING / likely CONTEXT ONLY for obscurity, pending a release-by-release audit. The original prison tapes are a substantial, first-hand-documented corpus of inmate-created original rock songs, recorded inside a maximum-security prison and preserved by a member. However, RISK has since publicly marketed its “prison rock” story, reunited and released music online; do not present it as undiscovered. Discovery value may lie in original 1984 cassette recordings, full song corpus (c.50 tracks), unreleased songs/arrangements, exact recording chronology, and comparison with post-reunion rerecordings. Need establish whether the online audio is original prison tape or later re-recordings; track titles, tape count/sequence, recording equipment/engineer, exact dates, and permission/rights. Do not repeat sensationalised criminal narrative; focus on music, self-teaching, collective composition, recording conditions and custody of tapes. Public site uses first names/nicknames; do not infer legal identities.

### Smithsonian Anacostia Community Museum sound item — archival lead, not yet an independent candidate
Smithsonian catalogue identifies ACMA AV003511-2, “Musical Recordings: Choral Music, Religious Music, and Work Songs,” one 1/4-inch open-reel sound recording, circa 1980s, in Record Group AV09-023, *Museum Events, Programs, and Projects, 1967–1989*. Scope note names recordings performed by Robert Shaw, Grant Williams, Robert Shaw Chorale, Alan Lomax, Mississippi Chorus, South Carolina Chain Gang, Texas Chain Gang, and “Prisoners At the Mississippi and Louisiana State Penitentiaries.” The reel is undated and described as a multi-performer/multi-topic compilation; catalogue does not give item-level tracklist, source provenance, prison names, performer identities or establish that all prisoner music is original onsite recording. Use as a targeted archival enquiry, not a verified new recording by incarcerated musicians. Access requires appointment; some formats may be inaccessible. Sources: https://collections.si.edu/search/detail/ead_component%3Asova-acma-09-023-ref612 ; https://anacostia.si.edu/collection/archives/object/sova-acma-09-023-ref612 .


### Smithsonian Anacostia ACMA AV003511-2 — full finding-aid context and caution (2026-11-15)
Inspected the full 115-page finding aid, not just the standalone item record. ACMA AV003511-2 is one 1/4-inch open-reel compilation (technical identifier 003118), undated, circa 1980s, in AV09-023. Its scope note groups Robert Shaw, Grant Williams, Robert Shaw Chorale, Alan Lomax, Mississippi Chorus, South Carolina Chain Gang, Texas Chain Gang, and “Prisoners At the Mississippi and Louisiana State Penitentiaries.” It does not describe separate source tapes, track order, song titles, recording dates, exact facilities, or whether prison performances were recorded inside the institutions. The juxtaposition with Alan Lomax and chain-gang materials raises the possibility of a compilation assembled from prior recordings, not necessarily new fieldwork by the museum. Do not infer that all performances are prison-made or that “Mississippi/Louisiana State Penitentiaries” are precise facility names. Item requires appointment and may be inaccessible due to obsolete playback equipment. Contact listed in finding aid: acmarchives@si.edu, 202-633-4853. Source: https://sirismm.si.edu/EADpdfs/ACMA.09-023.pdf (pp. 51–52 / PDF lines around 2820–2856); standalone record https://anacostia.si.edu/collection/archives/object/sova-acma-09-023-ref612 .

Related adjacent reel ACMA AV003511-1 is two open-reel sound recordings of spirituals/gospel (including Odetta, Robert J. Bradley, Rev. J.M. Gates, and unidentified performers), but finding aid does not identify prison origins; not a prison candidate. ACMA AV003531 is another circa-1980s reel, but contains George Benson, Jean-François Paillard/Orchestre de Chambre, Elton John, etc.; unrelated. These neighbouring identifiers are not evidence of more prison material. Source same finding aid, pp. 4–5 and 51–52.

Assessment: Smithsonian AV003511-2 remains a valuable targeted archive enquiry but should be labelled CONTEXT / SOURCE AUDIT, not a candidate recording. Highest-value questions for archivist: is technical reel 003118 a compilation copied from commercial/LOC/field recordings? Which tracks correspond to the prison performers? Are source credits, leader notes, accession documentation, box lists, cue sheets or track timings extant? Can a preservation transfer be made for research access, and what rights/permissions govern quotation or publication?


### Upheaval — Waupun Correctional Institution, Wisconsin; date correction and audiovisual archive (2026-11-15)
IMPORTANT CORRECTION: Earlier blog/distributor copy says Upheaval recorded live sessions in 1979. A much fuller 2022 investigation by Max Wilson, based on interviews with last known surviving member Joe Hayes, archival film, contemporary newspaper clippings, Waupun World prison newspaper and Wisconsin Historical Society material, dates the original 45 “Paradise Lost” / “Now That You're Gone” to **26 August 1974**. It dates “Come On with the Come On” and “T.L.C.” to circa 1974, not 1979. A 2024 archival audiovisual conference paper says People's Video visited the then Wisconsin State Prison (now WCI) in **1975** and recorded the band; this footage shows rehearsals and “Love Station,” was digitised in 2022 by UW-Madison Communication Arts, and is held by Wisconsin Historical Society. Thus distinguish the 1974 single / c.1974 surviving tracks, the 1975 film, and later 2016/2021 reissues. The “1979” date in Symphonical's marketing copy and some blogs conflicts with this better-supported chronology; do not repeat it as fact.

Wilson's article adds a substantial music programme history: Dave Bultman became director in June 1973; nine-piece Upheaval rehearsed and performed at WCI, and was one of multiple inmate bands. The band included Joe Hayes, LaVelle Rudd, James Harris, James Lampkins, Eddie Quinn, Melvin Torrence, Ernie Warren, and others; membership changed over time. Hayes is described as last known surviving member in 2022. The group originated in a vocal group at Green Bay Correctional Institution in 1963, before the Waupun band formed. Prison newspaper *Waupun World* contains Eddie Quinn's “Music Scene” columns (including a Jan 1971 essay on soul music) and LaVelle Rudd's writing; likely useful for identifying other bands, repertoire and cultural context. Other groups named: QCR (Upheaval precursor), Yusef & The Fellows, King Thomas and the Blue Flames, The Spiritual Five (gospel), The Sudanese. These are secondary research leads, not all verified as recordings.

Wisconsin Historical Society audiovisual source: 1975 People’s Video half-inch open-reel video *People's Video - Upheaval*, approximately one hour, showing rehearsal, song “Love Station”; WHS collection. 2024 AMIA conference paper states digitised in 2022 by UW-Madison Department of Communication Arts. Investigative article embeds WHS Aviary footage, including “Broken Down Porch,” “Ready Train,” “Happy Hooker,” “Love Station.” Sources: https://quillette.com/2022/07/28/lost-soul-behind-bars/ (especially sections I–IV); https://amiaconference.net/wp-content/uploads/2024/12/ASN-2024.pdf (item 16); https://www.wisconsinhistory.org/Records/Article/CS16461 (Waupun music programme contextual article, if consulted).

Obscurity rating: CONTEXT ONLY for the released singles, now included on reissues/compilations and well-covered in collector circles; HIGH PRIORITY for unreleased repertoire, prison newspaper documentation, radio/TV broadcasts and the 1975 rehearsal film as a rare visual document of inmate-created music. The story has now been investigated at length, so do not present band itself as unknown. Avoid reproducing sensational details of members' convictions/escape; centre their music, education, songwriting and community. Rights for film/audio remain separate; do not assume reuse permission.

### RISK — Kentucky State Penitentiary (cross-check note)
Keep RISK as a separate 1984 cassette corpus lead. Do not conflate with Upheaval. Upheaval's strong audiovisual evidence and documented 1974–75 chronology makes it a more concrete comparative case, but also a more publicly documented one.


### Waupun prison music programme: earlier photographic record and newspaper access route (2026-11-15)
New Wisconsin Historical Society record: **Waupun State Prison Band**, photograph dated 1898, collection PH 2077, digital identifier W013Q00. Description: seven incarcerated men, three guitars, three violins and cello, posed outdoors in prison uniforms. This documents an organised inmate string band at Waupun nearly 75 years before the Dave Bultman programme and Upheaval. It is a photograph, not audio; use as institutional music-history context only. A second image reproduced in Max Wilson's 2022 investigation shows a jazz band including future Upheaval members performing at Wisconsin State Prison on Labor Day 1971; photo credited to Wisconsin Historical Society. Source: https://www.wisconsinhistory.org/record/image/W013Q00/waupun-state-prison-band ; https://quillette.com/2022/07/28/lost-soul-behind-bars/ .

Newspaper research route: City of Waupun says all local newspapers 1857–2009 are digitised on Newspapers.com but accessible only on computers or wireless devices at the public library. It also says the city holds some historical editions of newspapers written by prisoners and that Wisconsin Historical Society holds additional records; WHS contact askarchives@wisconsinhistory.org / 608-264-6460. This is the concrete path to *Waupun World* (Jan 1971 Eddie Quinn essay on soul; Quinn's “Music Scene” columns; LaVelle Rudd's “LaVelle Raps” beginning May 1971). Sources: https://www.cityofwaupunwi.gov/1735/Waupun-Historical-Newspapers-Online ; https://www.cityofwaupunwi.gov/1492/Waupun-Correctional-Institution-Prisoner .

Further claim requiring independent corroboration: Joe Hayes told Max Wilson that Upheaval performances were recorded live for local television and radio; article says broadcasts reached outside audiences, and Dave Bultman sent copies of some songs to local radio stations. No station, programme, date or extant broadcast master identified yet. Treat as oral-history lead, not proof that recordings survive. Source: https://quillette.com/2022/07/28/lost-soul-behind-bars/ .

Other bands remain discovery leads, not verified recording candidates: QCR (Eddie Quinn, Charles May, LaVelle Rudd); Yusef & The Fellows; King Thomas and the Blue Flames; The Spiritual Five; The Sudanese (with Willie “Sheik” Gray). The 2022 article gives a valuable primary-personnel / prison-press research trail but no surviving recording evidence for these groups. A 2024 University of Wisconsin podcast episode uses music by a contemporary band named Upheaval; this is not the 1970s band, and avoid name conflation. Source: https://www.lovewi.com/podcasts/season-2-episode-2/ .


## Waupun State Prison — longer institutional music history and newspaper route (new research, 2026-11-15)
Wisconsin DOC's official department history states that an inmate band was established at Wisconsin State Prison, Waupun, in 1908; it played on the recreation field and during meals and gave public concerts in the front yard. This is a useful institutional chronology, but not evidence that a recording survives. It links the 1898 WHS photograph of a seven-member string band to a documented, organised band programme a decade later. The 1898 image is PH 2077 / W013Q00; its description identifies guitars, violins and cello. Sources: https://doc.wi.gov/Pages/AboutDOC/DepartmentHistory.aspx ; https://www.wisconsinhistory.org/record/image/W013Q00/waupun-state-prison-band .

A more actionable paper trail has surfaced: Waupun Public Library's historical newspaper digitisation includes the *Prison Press*, *The Waupunite* and other short-lived local papers, with public viewing at the library through Newspapers.com (not open remote access according to the report). This sits alongside the known *Waupun World* trail: Eddie “Baby Q” Quinn's “Music Scene” pieces and LaVelle Rudd's “LaVelle Raps” column from 1971. The latter are likely to provide names, band rosters, repertoire, rehearsal/performance notices and clues to any recordings, but no new audio master has been identified in this pass. The city also confirms it holds some editions of prisoner-written newspapers and points researchers to the Wisconsin Historical Society. Sources: https://waupunpioneer.com/2024/05/21/waupun-public-librarys-collection-of-historical-waupun-newspapers-digitized/ ; https://quillette.com/2022/07/28/lost-soul-behind-bars/ ; https://www.cityofwaupunwi.gov/1492/Waupun-Correctional-Institution-Prisoner .

Research priority: request or inspect *Waupun World* and *Prison Press* issues around 1908, 1971–75, then search for music-programme annual reports, band rosters and references to radio/TV or in-prison recording. The 1970s groups QCR, Yusef & The Fellows, King Thomas and the Blue Flames, The Spiritual Five and The Sudanese remain leads only until an actual recording or reliable recording documentation is found.

## Waupun prison print culture: 1886–1946 and archival holdings (new research, 2026-11-15)
The Wisconsin DOC chronology dates the first issue of *The Waupun Prison Press* to 1886, and its 1908 inmate band chronology remains independently listed. A Wisconsin Historical Society prison-newspaper bibliography (compiled 2017) identifies a specific WHS-held issue of *Prison Press*, 5 Jan. 1886; it also lists *Prison Light* (Waupun; issues 16 Apr. and 7 May 1892), *Candle* (monthly, Nov. 1934–Jan. 1946; title varied as *G.E.C. News* in 1934–36), and *Waupun World* (1969?–Jan. 1983, with WHS-held issues in 1970–75 and July 1980–Jan. 1983). The bibliography says microfilm or digital copies may be available from WHS's Microfilm Lab; some titles are licensed commercially. This is a much broader archival route than the newspaper search previously recorded. Sources: https://doc.wi.gov/Pages/AboutDOC/DepartmentHistory.aspx ; https://cdn.wisconsinhistory.org/cdm/Prison%20Newspaper%20CollectionSep2017.pdf .

A Waupun Historical Society catalogue independently lists a *Prison Press* issue dated 23 Feb. 1886 and a general *Prison Press* book entry, suggesting local holdings beyond the single WHS issue in the 2017 bibliography. A 2011 French doctoral thesis on Wisconsin prisons quotes the 5 Jan. 1886 *Prison Press* and notes its early publication history; contemporary newspaper coverage in February 1886 also describes it as a weekly made by inmates after their ten-hour workday. A later report says the paper ended in June 1886, so the run may have been short. None of these sources yet confirms musical content. Sources: https://hub.catalogit.app/waupun-historical-society/folder/library ; https://theses.fr/2011AIX10058.pdf ; https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/9/95/The_New-York_Evangelist_1886-02-25-_Vol_57_Iss_8_%28IA_sim_evangelist-and-religious-review_1886-02-25_57_8%29.pdf ; https://kfai.org/minneculture-the-silent-citys-newspaper/ .

Priority: inspect *Candle/G.E.C. News* (1934–46) and *Waupun World* (1970–75) for band reports, music columns, inmate composers, instruments, concerts, radio/film recording references, and the named 1970s groups. Search *Prison Light* and the 1886 *Prison Press* for cultural life, but do not treat mention of a band or concert as evidence of a surviving recording. The WHS catalogue gives concrete issue-level targets and a reproduction route.

### Screened-out audio item: Buffalo State Hospital interviews (1962)
*The Language of Schizophrenia* is a privately pressed three-LP set of Dr. Hans Unger's interviews with patients at Buffalo State Hospital, recorded/released in 1962; the Ladd School Historical Society says all six sides have been digitised and presented with transcripts. This is spoken interview material, not music made by patients; it is not a candidate recording for the main music collection. It may be noted only as an ethically sensitive example of institutional audio, requiring careful consent/provenance scrutiny and avoidance of sensational framing. Source: https://www.theladdschool.com/collections/language-of-schizophrenia-buffalo-state-hospital/ .

## Soledad Correctional Training Facility (CTF) — Jack Bowers inmate-band recordings, 1983–1996 (new item-level lead, 2026-11-15)
The Library of Congress's Benjamin J. Harbert collection (AFC 2024/014, 2025 finding aid) contains a distinct Jack Bowers collection from Soledad Prison / Correctional Training Facility. Five cassette-liner photographs are preserved under “Collection artwork, 1983–1996,” alongside four named audio sets: **Rusty Smith (1983), 10 AIFF tracks** (“All I Wanna Do Is Make Good Music,” “Three Squares,” “Western Sunsets,” “Can't Take the Beer Out of Country Fried Music,” “Gonna Open Your Door (Say Adios For the Last Time),” “All Make Believe,” “The Speckled Bird,” “The Cowboy Song,” “God Needed an Angel,” “Never Could Dance (But I Can Sure Rock n' Roll)”); **Sacred Ryche (1986), 7 AIFF tracks** (“Heavy Metal Atmosphere,” “Lover's Delight,” “Guitar Solo,” “Patiently Devoted to Love,” “Seasons of the Witch,” “Why Don't We Say Goodbye?,” “Sound of a Broken Heart”); **Correctional Training Facility Recording Project (July 1992), 13 AIFF tracks** (“Youngster, Youngster,” “Guiding Light,” “Hasta Que Cierre el Salon,” “Down By the Riverside,” “Thinkin' uv U,” “I Am an Eagle,” “Day & Night,” “Jessica,” “Middle Class Brainwashed,” “The Memorie of Your Smile,” “My Feet Don't Touch the Ground,” “Drivin' Your Heart to Ruin,” “Bonus Jam”); **Anthology Correctional Training Facility (1996), 9 AIFF tracks** (“LA Samba,” “Recuerdos de la Alhambra,” “Rejas de Acero,” “Dreams of Arrowhead,” “Not Love,” “Invictus,” “Blurs,” “Affirmation,” “Cosa de la Vida”). A fifth item “Oscar's Tune” has two AIFF files (“Oscar's Tune,” extra “God Bless the Child”) but its precise date and facility attribution are not given in the item description; don't assume it belongs to one of the four dated sets without checking. Digital ID AFC2024014_dc001; subseries 7.1. The finding aid describes the larger collection as open to research and requests go through the Folklife Reading Room; third-party rights may still apply. This is a separate Soledad corpus from Harbert's own 2005–09 California Men's Colony and 2009 New Folsom fieldwork already listed elsewhere in this file. **Status: PROMISING / HIGH PRIORITY archive-audit lead**, not yet a fully verified standalone artist/release entry: exact identity of Rusty Smith and Sacred Ryche, inmate status, group membership, track order/source cassette, whether sets are original tapes or later digital transfers, and reuse permissions all need confirmation. Avoid conflating with the broader Arts in Corrections programme. Sources: https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/gdc/gdcfindingaidpdfs/af024007/af024007.pdf ; https://www.ala.org/srrt/events/abolitionist-visions-intersections .

Soledad programme context: a 2024 American Library Association event description by Jack Bowers and Reggie Austin says the Soledad inmate-band programme operated 1982–2005, supporting up to 20 bands at four rehearsal sites. This helps contextualise the breadth of the corpus but does not establish that every band or recording in the LOC subseries was part of a single programme or that all performers were incarcerated. The 1987 Bread & Roses event at Soledad is separately documented in Michael Kleff's audio finding aid (cassette C38A/C38B): it includes prison press officer Mary Hunter Shorter, Jack Bowers, inmates Vickers and Loren, visiting Blue Meanies and Kosono, and a 5:33 performance “Hard Body” by the Blue Meanies plus “La Bamba” by Kosono. Treat this as visiting-performance / mixed event documentation, not an inmate band recording. Sources: https://www.ala.org/srrt/events/abolitionist-visions-intersections ; https://www.michaelkleff.com/collections/Audio%20Material%20Finding%20Aid.html .

Next checks: request/access AFC2024/014 digital files and five cassette-liner images; compare artwork with four sets; establish whether the sets correspond to distinct groups/bands or compilation projects; search CTF newsletters, Jack Bowers / William James Association papers and former participants' testimony; establish performers and original tape dates. For the 1987 event, locate programme book and Bread & Roses newsletter no. 23 (Winter 1987), listed in Michael Kleff's related research-files finding aid.

### CELL(R) — *Every Day* — Leuven Central Prison, Belgium (2023)
- **Status:** VERIFIED recording / PROMISING. Obscurity: moderate; locally publicised and sold through partner organisations and Bandcamp, but still a small, place-specific prison-made album with limited wider circulation. Not an institutional showcase album by visiting artists.
- **Recording:** *Every Day*, released 13 November 2023; 15-track digital album and CD. Tracks in Bandcamp order: “A Better Way Than This”; “Heartstrings”; “Beyond Horizons Known”; “You're A Wonder”; “Inside of the Bars”; “Infamous”; “Don't Give Up”; “Four Seasons At One Time”; “Hotel Leuven”; “Solution”; “Every Day”; “Dawn”; “Sorry”; “You Got A Super Power”; “2018.”
- **Environment / provenance:** The album was recorded in the music cellar / rehearsal studio of Leuven Central Prison. The partner organisation Moderator vzw says eight incarcerated people (seven musicians and one artist) formed CELL(R) after a band-coaching trajectory; songs were rehearsed and recorded inside. De Rode Antraciet confirms all songs were written, sung and played by eight incarcerated contributors (one songwriter/musician, four musicians, two guest musicians and one artist). A prison staff member, Anne Verbraeken, contributes backing vocals; volunteer Gerry Koolen brought recording equipment into the prison, recorded, mixed and mastered. Artwork was made by incarcerated artist Mamboleo. Lyrics and music are credited on Bandcamp to Marcus Matthews; other credited performers use first names or aliases (Johan, Pierre X, Johnni K Heart, Big Mama, Maxx Banco, Bossi). Do not try to identify these aliases or disclose legal identities.
- **Credits nuance:** The institutional source describes eight incarcerated contributors and the Bandcamp description says all songs were written, sung and played by eight inmates, but detailed credits identify Marcus Matthews as writer/composer and lead vocalist on all tracks except track 10, while staff member Anne and other collaborators appear in backing/guest credits. Preserve this distinction rather than implying every incarcerated participant wrote every song. The CD sale proceeds went to Moderator vzw, chosen by the incarcerated participants; the CD was also made available within and outside the prison.
- **Why it fits:** clear resident-created and resident-performed album; original songs respond to confinement and life inside, including “Hotel Leuven,” “Inside of the Bars,” “Every Day,” “Infamous,” and “Sorry”; official partner organisation provides unusually strong provenance and participant-role detail.
- **Cautions / next steps:** The release was publicly promoted in Belgium and has local media/video coverage, so do not overstate obscurity. Confirm current CD availability, physical catalogue details / label or self-release imprint, full liner notes and the artists' preferred public credits. Use only official authorised listening/purchase routes; Bandcamp states all rights reserved. Do not reproduce lyrics or artwork without permission.
- **Sources:** Moderator vzw project announcement (contemporary partner account): https://moderator.be/gedetineerden-uit-leuven-centraal-lanceren-eigen-cd-voor-het-goede-doel/ ; De Rode Antraciet album page: https://derodeantraciet.be/over-ons/onze-publicaties/ ; official Bandcamp with tracklist and credits: https://cellr.bandcamp.com/album/every-day ; De Rode Antraciet 2023 annual report: https://www.derodeantraciet.be/wp-content/uploads/2024/03/03-2024_DeRodeAntraciet-Jaarverslag2023_WEB.pdf ; local launch report: https://www.leuvenactueel.be/cultuur/cultuur/28291/gedetineerden-uit-leuven-centraal-lanceren-eigen-cd-voor-het-goede-doel .

### Research update — Soledad and Waupun source-audit, 2026-11-15

**Soledad / Jack Bowers: strengthen the archival route, not the performer identifications.** The Library of Congress finding aid places the Rusty Smith (1983), Sacred Ryche (1986), CTF Recording Project (July 1992), Anthology CTF (1996), and undated “Oscar's Tune” files together in Jack Bowers's Soledad Prison subseries (AFC 2024/014, digital ID afc2024014_dc001). It also lists five cassette-liner photographs dated 1983–1996 and a separate Jack Bowers interview on MiniDV MV031 (2003–2004). The latter is a promising route for identifying programme participants and explaining the recording workflow, but the catalogue alone does not connect the interview to any one tape or resolve the performers' identities. A 2024 ALA programme description dates the Soledad inmate-band programme to 1982–2005 and says it supported up to 20 bands at four rehearsal sites; this establishes programme context, not that every cassette in Bowers's collection was a band-programme release. Request the cassette-liner photographs and MV031 through the LOC Folklife Reading Room before assigning people or provenance beyond the finding aid.

The Rusty Smith title overlap remains unresolved: a separately catalogued 1984 Santa Cruz cassette by “Rusty Smith and the Saddletramps” shares several titles with the LOC 1983 Soledad set, but the copyright record names George Lee Davidson as claimant and credits him with the performance and recording. Nothing found establishes that this is the same Rusty Smith or that the Santa Cruz cassette derives from the prison tape. Keep these as distinct records pending direct comparison and primary documentation.

**Waupun: specify the newspaper holdings and screen out visiting performances.** The Wisconsin Historical Society's 2017 prison-newspaper bibliography gives an item-level run of *Waupun World*: 20 Feb., 20 Apr. and 20 Nov. 1970; 20 Jan.–20 Nov. 1971; 1 Sep. 1972; 15 Oct. and 20 Dec. 1973; 20 Jan.–20 Nov. 1974; 20 Jan.–20 June and 20 Nov. 1975; and July 1980–Jan. 1983. This sharpens the next archival request: prioritise the 20 Jan. 1971 issue (Eddie Quinn's essay on soul is cited by Max Wilson), then 1971–75 issues for Quinn's “Music Scene,” LaVelle Rudd's “LaVelle Raps,” band rosters and performance/recording notices. The same bibliography lists WHS holdings of *Prison Press* (5 Jan. 1886), *Prison Light* (16 Apr. and 7 May 1892), and *Candle* / *G.E.C. News* (Nov. 1934–Jan. 1946), offering a longer institutional print-culture route.

A separate 15 Oct. 1971 *Daily Cardinal* issue includes a report on two blues concerts at Waupun by a visiting Madison-based act associated with the Luther Allison Blues circle. This is **not** evidence of inmate-made music; retain it only as context for visiting performances and for the newspaper's contemporary references to *Waupun World*. Do not turn it into a candidate recording. Waupun Public Library says the local newspaper digitisation is available for in-library viewing; BadgerLink access is available for Waupun newspapers through 1928. The city also confirms it holds some prisoner-written newspaper issues and directs researchers to the Wisconsin Historical Society.

**Disposition:** no new qualifying recording was verified in this pass. Keep Soledad as a high-priority archive request and Waupun as a newspaper-led discovery route; do not promote the additional Waupun bands to recording candidates without audio or primary recording documentation.

Sources:
- LOC Harbert finding aid, Soledad subseries and Bowers interview: https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/gdc/gdcfindingaidpdfs/af024007/af024007.pdf
- ALA description of Soledad programme: https://www.ala.org/srrt/events/abolitionist-visions-intersections
- Copyright record for 1984 Santa Cruz Rusty Smith/Saddletramps cassette: https://www.copyrightencyclopedia.com/all-i-wanna-do-is-make-love-to-you-the-receiving-end-more/
- WHS prison newspaper bibliography: https://cdn.wisconsinhistory.org/cdm/Prison%20Newspaper%20CollectionSep2017.pdf
- 15 Oct. 1971 *Daily Cardinal*: https://asset.library.wisc.edu/1711.dl/RCTBDBIRPTG6686/E/file-35077.pdf
- Waupun newspaper access: https://www.cityofwaupunwi.gov/1735/Waupun-Historical-Newspapers-Online ; https://waupunpioneer.com/2024/05/21/waupun-public-librarys-collection-of-historical-waupun-newspapers-digitized/


### Harry Urata — *holehole bushi* field recordings — Japanese sugar-plantation workers, Hawai‘i (1960–1980; CD 2000)

- **Status:** VERIFIED archival recording corpus / PROMISING for item-level discovery; the wider tradition is now more visible following a 2013 book and 2025 National Recording Registry selection, so avoid presenting the collection itself as unknown. The strongest discovery value is the relatively unexamined individual field tapes, singers and variants.
- **Environment / tradition:** *Holehole bushi* is a Japanese-language folk-song tradition originating among Japanese immigrants working on Hawai‘i's sugar plantations in the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries. The songs were sung during plantation work and preserve workers' experiences. Harry Urata (1917–2009) was interned at Honouliuli during WWII; after his release in 1945 he undertook a decades-long effort to recover the repertoire, travelling among the Hawaiian islands from 1960 and recording former plantation workers through the 1980s. Do not confuse Urata's wartime internment with the plantation origin of the songs or imply that these particular recordings were made in the internment camp.
- **Surviving audio:** Smithsonian Ralph Rinzler Folklife Archives and Collections, *Harry Urata audio recordings*, collection CFCH.URAT, dated 1960–2000 (bulk 1960–1980): 16 open-reel tapes, four cassettes, one commercially released CD, one DVD and nine lyric transparencies. The 20 field/oral-history recordings are fully digitised; listening copies are available on request. Item-level clues include Tape 1 (1975); Tapes 3 and 5 (24 Dec. 1965); Tape 8 (22 Apr. 1975); Tape 9 (16 Aug. 1960); Tapes 11–12 labelled “KPUA” / “KPUA Sato” (KPUA was a Hilo radio station); Tape 15 labelled with holehole bushi singers Kikue Kurashige, Matsumoto Koike and Shige Yameda; Tape 16 labelled “Hole Hole Bushi / Hore Hore Bushi — 3 versions”; Tape 17 cassette labelled “Plantation Woman — Bango [Banjo?] 1885 by Jim Albertini”; and Tapes 19–20, interviews with Mrs. Tei Saito on 10 March 1980. A commercial CD, *Hole Hole Bushi* (M&H Hawaii, 2000; item MHCD2000), is also in the collection.
- **Why it fits:** unusually direct recordings of older workers preserving a musical tradition formed in a racialised, exploitative plantation labour environment. The collection holds named singers, multiple variants and oral-history context across several decades, rather than only a polished later reconstruction.
- **Obscurity assessment:** PROMISING, not HIGH PRIORITY as a whole. The Smithsonian selected the collection for the 2025 National Recording Registry and Franklin Odo's *Voices from the Canefields* (2013) has made the tradition accessible. However, the item-level tape contents, singer biographies, local recording locations, exact track order and relation of the field tapes to the 2000 CD remain open research questions. Target overlooked individual performances and tapes rather than re-presenting the general tradition.
- **Cautions / next steps:** Do not infer that every item is music: several are oral-history recordings, and tape-box notes require Japanese-language reading and direct access-copy review. Establish which named singers were former plantation workers and where/when each song was recorded. Clarify the “1885” inscription on Tape 17; it may refer to a song's date/origin, not the recording date. Compare the 2000 CD with the field tapes. The archive states that permission to publish must be requested and that copyright may be held by others; access is not reuse permission. Preserve community-preferred spellings and pronunciation of Japanese/Hawaiian names and terms.
- **Sources:** Smithsonian finding aid CFCH.URAT (item-level inventory and access conditions): https://sirismm.si.edu/EADpdfs/CFCH.URAT.pdf ; Smithsonian collection overview: https://folklife.si.edu/archives-and-resources/harry-urata-audio-recordings ; Smithsonian National Recording Registry announcement: https://folklife.si.edu/news-and-events/urata-national-recording-registry ; Smithsonian catalogue record for 2000 CD: https://collections.si.edu/search/results.htm?q=set_name%3A%22Harry+Urata+audio+recordings%22 ; Franklin Odo, *Voices from the Canefields: Folksongs from Japanese Immigrant Workers in Hawai‘i* (Oxford University Press, 2013).


### Harry Urata follow-up: item-level catalogue records (2026-11-15)

The Smithsonian's public item records now give stable catalogue targets for the strongest tape leads:
- **Tape 15 / Urata_01_0015:** undated 5-inch quarter-inch open reel, labelled “Hole Hole Bushi” with Kikue Kurashige, Matsumoto, Koike, others and Shige Yameda; Japanese/English; listening copy available. Smithsonian item record: https://collections.si.edu/search/results.htm?q=%22Harry+Urata%2C+1917-2009%22 (item Urata_01_0015).
- **Tape 16 / Urata_01_0016:** undated 5-inch quarter-inch open reel, labelled “Hole Hole Bushi / Hore Hore Bushi — 3 versions”; full-track mono, 7.5 ips; listening copy available. Finding-aid item details: https://sirismm.si.edu/EADpdfs/CFCH.URAT.pdf .
- **Tape 17 / Urata_01_0017:** undated cassette labelled “Plantation Woman — Bango [Banjo?] 1885 by Jim Albertini”; Smithsonian item page explicitly confirms one cassette and that a listening copy is available: https://www.si.edu/object/archives/components/sova-cfch-urat-ref522 .
- **CD MHCD2000:** Smithsonian catalogues the 2000 commercial CD *Hole Hole Bushi*, produced by Harry M. Urata for M&H Hawaii; catalogue search: https://collections.si.edu/search/results.htm?q=set_name%3A%22Harry+Urata+audio+recordings%22 .

These catalogue pages strengthen the existence and accessibility evidence but do not supply audio content, complete performer biographies, recording locations or exact song titles. Do not infer that “1885” is a recording date; the cassette itself is undated. Do not expand the identity of any named singer using unrelated genealogy or obituary matches absent a reliable connection to the tape.


### Follow-up — Upheaval surviving-song corpus and Uzuakoli discography clue (2026-11-15)

**Upheaval / Waupun:** Max Wilson's fuller 2022 account says that, in addition to the released/reissued tracks already listed, Joe Hayes had “a CD's worth” of Upheaval recordings, including “Come On with the Come On” and “Vampire Lady.” The article also reports that LaVelle Rudd had reels and music tapes, bequeathed them to his girlfriend before his death; their present whereabouts remain unverified. It describes cassette recordings circulating among members as damaged/poor-quality and insufficient for reissue. This expands the *known reported corpus*, but does not establish a clean master, exact tracklist, carrier genealogy or which items are original prison performances versus later transfers/rerecordings. Add “Vampire Lady” to the search terms for the Hayes-held CD/tape material; do not treat the reported CD as a commercial release or assert that its contents are all prison-era masters. Source: Max Wilson, “Lost Soul Behind Bars,” original Quillette article / author's republication: https://quillette.com/2022/07/28/lost-soul-behind-bars/ ; https://maxwellwilson.substack.com/p/lost-soul-behind-bars .

**Uzuakoli / Harcourt Whyte:** a 2015 Nairaland forum discussion claims that two albums existed by the Choir of the Uzuakoli Leper Colony and names Nnamdi Olebara as conductor. This is a low-confidence discovery clue only: it supplies no label, catalogue number, sleeve image, date, tracklist or source citation. It may refer to the ARC 1106 (1974) and ARC 1059 (1975/1977 reissue) records already noted, but the chronology and Music In Africa's statement that the colony choir recorded after Whyte's 1977 death remain unresolved. Do not upgrade the identification or cite the forum as proof. Search the exact phrase “Choir of the Uzuakoli Leper Colony” with Nnamdi Olebara, and ask John Manton / Achinivu whether the vinyl used in the 2015 broadcast is one of these commercial LPs or a separate posthumous issue. Source (forum, discovery only): https://www.nairaland.com/2335651/remembers-ikoli-harcourt-whyte-leper-boy . The 2017 BBC profile independently confirms that Whyte formed a choir of people living with leprosy, that it toured, and that the choir performed at the centre; it does not identify the recordings or confirm the forum's album claim: https://www.bbc.com/news/world-africa-42111196 (mirror: https://bbcinsight.pages.dev/news/world-africa-42111196).


## International small-web sweep — Argentina prison recordings (2026-11-15)

### Rimas de Alto Calibre — Unidad Penal 48, San Martín, Buenos Aires, Argentina (2010–12)
- **Status:** VERIFIED prison-made album / CONTEXT ONLY for obscurity priority; retain as a regional comparison, not a new priority discovery.
- **Recording:** *Rimas de Alto Calibre*, recorded inside Unidad Penal 48 of the San Martín prison complex, José León Suárez. Sessions began in November 2010 with the mobile La Burra Records studio brought into the prison. The Universidad Nacional de San Martín (UNSAM) says the album was composed and recorded entirely inside the prison and was the first album made in an Argentine prison. The group emerged from CUSAM's popular-verse/music workshop, begun in 2009; the university's 2012 report describes ten tracks spanning hip-hop, cumbia, ska, rock and heavy metal. Página/12's December 2011 report describes the finished album and gives examples including “Hacia la libertad” and “La Guajira.” A 2013 report says 11 songs; a CUSAM thesis describes a 12-track disc. Preserve this track-count discrepancy until the actual release is inspected.
- **Makers / collaboration:** the group comprised incarcerated students and some formerly incarcerated participants; the number varies by date/source (UNSAM's 2012 preview says 15 musicians; the official concert report describes 12 group members; contemporaneous reporting gives individual first names and roles). Outside musicians also contributed to the project/recording, including Sergio Dawi, Alejo Ferrero, Andrea Prodan and Miss Bolivia; the mobile studio was provided by Juan Pablo de Mendonca / La Burra Records. It is not an inmate-only recording in the strict sense, even though the songs were created and recorded in prison by the group.
- **Why context only:** it received university promotion, public concerts, press coverage, a documentary (“Desde adentro suenan… Rimas de alto calibre,” 2014), and collaboration with established musicians. The 2012 launch was a substantial local public event. It is valuable and comparatively little-known outside Argentina, but not obscure enough to prioritise ahead of private, unissued or poorly catalogued recordings.
- **Cautions / next steps:** if included in a regional context index, verify the physical/digital release object, full tracklist, catalogue/label/imprint, exact credited roles and current authorised listening route. Preserve public aliases and do not expand identities beyond source permissions. Distinguish incarcerated contributors from released alumni and visiting/professional guests. Do not infer that every track was written/performed solely by incarcerated people.
- **Sources:** UNSAM official announcement (3 July 2012; 10 songs, recording entirely in Unidad 48, mobile studio): https://noticias.unsam.edu.ar/2012/07/03/rimas-de-alto-calibre-en-el-bauen/ ; UNSAM official launch report (5 July 2012; 12 members, credits and collaborators): https://noticias.unsam.edu.ar/2012/07/05/rimas-de-alto-calibre-toco-en-el-bauen/ ; Página/12 contemporaneous report (13 Dec. 2011; group, workshop, songs and recording): https://www.pagina12.com.ar/diario/sociedad/3-183208-2011-12-13.html ; Página/12 2013 report (11 songs and mobile studio): https://www.pagina12.com.ar/diario/suplementos/radar/17-8680-2013-03-10.html ; CUSAM thesis: https://www.unsam.edu.ar/ciep/wp-content/uploads/2013/06/TESIS_AZENAS_VANESSA_TATIANA.pdf ; UNSAM documentary announcement (2014): https://noticias.unsam.edu.ar/2014/05/27/estreno-del-documental-rimas-de-alto-calibre-en-el-campus/ .

### *Rock en la cárcel* — Carabanchel Prison, Madrid, Spain (1993)
- **Status:** VERIFIED inmate-participation album / CONTEXT ONLY; screened as a visibility benchmark, not a priority lead.
- **Recording/release:** 1993 album *El rock en la cárcel* / *Rock en la cárcel*, made with ten or eleven incarcerated participants at Carabanchel Prison. A June 1993 *El País* report describes eleven prisoners recording an 11-song album inside the prison, with help from established musicians including Manolo Tena, Raimundo Amador, Ariel Rot, Rosendo and Pepe Habichuela. A November 1993 *El País* launch report says the released album was performed by ten Carabanchel inmates, artistic director Manolo Tena, and had an initial pressing of 20,000 copies; proceeds were to go to drug-rehabilitation centres.
- **Provenance nuance:** the prison administration initiated the project; well-known outside musicians assisted. Some participants were on conditional release and performed at the launch. It is therefore an institutional collaborative project, not a hidden inmate-only tape. Track and performer counts differ between the June and November reports; the actual album sleeve/liner notes should govern.
- **Why context only:** a 20,000-copy initial pressing and prominent media/artist involvement make this a public rehabilitation/charity project rather than an overlooked private recording. It is a useful comparator for distinguishing resident composition/recording from outside collaboration and institutional programming.
- **Sources:** *El País*, “Canciones desde la celda” (14 June 1993): https://elpais.com/diario/1993/06/14/ultima/740008801_850215.html ; *El País*, “Se presenta un disco grabado por internos de la cárcel de Carabanchel” (11 Nov. 1993): https://elpais.com/diario/1993/11/11/cultura/752972413_850215.html .


### Impacto Real — *Mundos dos Muros* / *Mundo dos Muros* — Presídio Santa Augusta, Criciúma, Santa Catarina, Brazil (2011)
- **Status:** VERIFIED recording / PROMISING; a small, locally documented prison-made CD with limited current discoverability. It merits an item-level check, but the original audio and exact release metadata remain uninspected.
- **Recording:** a 9-track CD titled *Mundos dos Muros* in the November 2011 Portuguese reports; one independent blog headline/body renders the title *Mundo dos Muros* (singular). The group Impacto Real consisted of three incarcerated men at Presídio Santa Augusta, Criciúma, identified publicly only by initials I.A.M., A.R.A. and D.V.M. (ages 27, 26 and 28 in the 2011 account). The CD was created under Projeto Comunicasom, a partnership involving Justiça Federal, UNESC, the state prison administration (DEAP) and Presídio Santa Augusta.
- **Environment / recording process:** reports say the trio formed inside the prison, began writing lyrics and composing after one member started playing guitar, and converted a room in the prison into a studio. The work involved 15 days of workshops and more than 60 hours of recording, resulting in nine tracks. Lyrics address the prison system, separation/longing, prejudice, visiting days, drugs and opportunities. The trio performed publicly at UNESC on 10 November 2011; the recording itself is reported as made inside the prison.
- **Why it fits:** a little-circulated, local Brazilian prison rap/gospel recording with original songs by three self-described novice musicians, preserved only in a thin trail of 2011 small blogs and local reporting. Reports say some tracks were available on the original Projeto Comunicasom blog, but that blog is currently inaccessible through the available web route; no audio was auditioned.
- **Cautions / next steps:** preserve the three members' initials; do not attempt to identify them. Establish the exact cover title (singular/plural), CD imprint/catalogue/pressing, full tracklist, recording credits and whether the nine tracks remain in the project's blog/archive. Verify current project/archive custodianship through UNESC or the Federal Justice partnership. Check participant consent and rights before linking, quoting or reproducing audio. Do not assume that the original blog's streaming/download link grants reuse permission.
- **Sources:** contemporaneous Folha Gospel report, explicitly credited to Gospel Prime with information from local paper A Tribuna (16 Nov. 2011): https://folhagospel.com/trio-de-detentos-grava-cd-gospel-dentro-da-cadeia/ ; Blog do Salvo, contemporaneous local-blog post (15 Nov. 2011): https://blogdosalvo.blogspot.com/2011/11/grupo-grava-primeiro-cd-dentro-de.html ; another contemporaneous blog copy (17 Nov. 2011): https://jluancaesar.blogspot.com/2011/11/jovens-detentos-gravam-cd-gospel-dentro.html ; UNESC 2012 annual report confirming the Comunicasom programme: https://www.unesc.net/files/editor/files/relatorio_2012.pdf ; UNESC repository thesis on Comunicasom's radio/education work: https://repositorio.unesc.net/items/d8e18128-0403-4a59-9b48-6e5dcb2e4dc7 .

### Screened pending recording-location proof — Danila Alves / *A Voz da Liberdade*, Presídio Feminino do Distrito Federal, Brazil (2011)
A 6 October 2011 report says incarcerated singer Danila Alves was due to record an eight-song CD, *A Voz da Liberdade*, with five songs composed by her; a December 2011 official DF penitentiary-council minute confirms the CD launch at the women's prison. A 2012 community-organisation blog also reports attending the launch. However, the available sources do **not** establish where the CD was recorded. The initial report says she would record the CD on 13 October but does not say the session took place inside the prison; a launch at the prison is not proof of an in-prison recording. Keep as CONTEXT / recording location unverified, not a main discography entry until the studio/session provenance is established. Preserve the artist's public name as sourced; do not infer further identity details. Sources: https://www.reporternews.com.br/noticia/73764/Descoberta_em_concurso_na_cadeia__presidiaria_do_DF_compoe_e_lanca_CD ; https://www.sinj.df.gov.br/sinj/BaixarArquivoDiario.aspx?id_file=1801b400-92fd-3d76-84f7-4e2e594a2ffc ; https://capdf.blogspot.com/2012/01/ .


### Sufferras Crew — CAI La Reforma, Alajuela, Costa Rica (c. 2012)
- **Status:** PARTIALLY VERIFIED recording lead / PROMISING for item-level discovery; the group and in-prison recording opportunity are documented, but the specific recording object remains unidentified.
- **Environment / group:** a 4 May 2012 post on Costa Rican reggae blog Rootstico says five young men serving sentences at La Reforma met inside the prison and formed Sufferras Crew. It names their public stage names as Black Diamond, Demente, Dictionary, Kriminal and Natural Youth. The post says E.G.V. Records, Cultura Roots and DJ Zion helped them record inside La Reforma; because the five were not held together, they could only coordinate lyrics at the moment of recording. The blog also links a download file, but does not identify its filename, release title, tracklist, format or whether it is the prison-session audio.
- **Independent context:** a 23 May 2013 *Diario Extra* article reports that Gilberto Harris and fellow prisoners formed a reggae group called Sufferras inside La Reforma and were seeking support to continue. It does not map Harris to any of the five stage names, so do not make that identification. A later local music platform search surfaces numerous Sufferras tracks/videos, but these later releases do not prove they were recorded in prison.
- **Why it fits:** rare local-language small-web trace of a Costa Rican prison group whose members recorded inside a facility with outside label/producer assistance. The specific prison recordings may be poorly catalogued and the original blog download link is a valuable discovery route.
- **Obscurity assessment:** PROMISING, not yet HIGH PRIORITY. Sufferras later gained some local reggae visibility and multiple songs circulate online; the target is the small, specific in-prison recording corpus, not the whole later discography.
- **Cautions / next steps:** identify the file linked in the 2012 Rootstico post through the blog author/producer rather than assuming it is authorised or still available; ask E.G.V. Records, Cultura Roots or DJ Zion for session dates, equipment, tracklist and original masters. Verify which tracks were actually recorded inside La Reforma and whether later tracks were made after release. Preserve public stage names; do not map them to legal identities without consent. No audio auditioned and no contact made.
- **Sources:** Rootstico, “Sufferras crew” (4 May 2012; five stage names and explicit in-prison recording claim): https://rootstico.blogspot.com/2012/05/sufferas-crew.html ; *Diario Extra*, “Reos impartirán justicia” (23 May 2013; inmate Gilberto Harris says he and others formed reggae group Sufferras at La Reforma): https://www.diarioextra.com/noticia/reos-impartiran-justicia/ ; local video/song search result for later Sufferras circulation (not evidence of prison recording): https://sonichits.com/video/The_Sufferras/The_Sufferras_%02_No_es_facil_%28_Bomb_A_Drop_Riddim_%29 .

### Chacabuco concentration camp — clandestine cassette / *Chacabuco* LP (Chile, 1973–74; issued in exile)
- **Status:** VERIFIED clandestine camp recording / CONTEXT ONLY for obscurity; retain as an important provenance benchmark, not a priority discovery.
- **Recording:** a cassette recording made clandestinely in Chacabuco camp in northern Chile during 1973–74, involving prisoners and the camp choir. A 2019 Chilean discography blog says the cassette was recorded by Alberto Corvalán Castillo with help from fellow prisoners; a recorder was supplied by an officer/chaplain, and the cassette was smuggled out by Ángel Parra. The first LP publication occurred in Italy in 1974–75 under the title *Chacabuco* (the blog gives VPA 8344 / I Dischi Dello Zodiaco / 1977 in its page title; reconcile original release date and catalogue chronology). The recording includes comments by Corvalán, the Coro de Chacabuco under Iván Quezada, and other prisoner performances. The same blog distinguishes a later technical rerecording of Parra's own songs from the rawer clandestine cassette.
- **Related repertoire:** “Cuecas chacabucanas” were composed in the camp between November 1973 and February 1974 by Víctor Canto and Luis Cifuentes of Los de Chacabuco; these were later issued on *Pisagua + Chacabuco* (2003). A 2018 blog post documents a later reconstruction/recording of “La Cueca del Filistoque” by MACOMBA; that reconstruction is not the original camp performance.
- **Why context only:** Angel Parra and the camp recording have been documented and reissued; this is a known political-exile record rather than a hidden cassette. It is useful as a methodological example of distinguishing the clandestine source cassette, first LP publication, Parra's later rerecordings and 2003 reissue.
- **Cautions / next steps:** verify the first Italian LP edition and catalogue number from sleeve/label images; compare it against the original cassette and 2003 reissue; identify which tracks are original camp performances versus later rerecordings. Respect the political-prisoner context and avoid flattening all camp music into a single “soundtrack of suffering.” Do not reuse blog-ripped audio.
- **Sources:** Discoteca Nacional Chile, “Ángel Parra: Chacabuco. VPA 8344. I Dischi Dello Zodiaco. 1977. Italia” (first-hand recollection with caveats): https://discotecanacionalchile.blogspot.com/2019/04/angel-parra-chacabuco-vpa-8344-i-dischi.html ; Chilean historical blog on camp music and clandestine recordings: https://terrorismodeestadoenchile.blogspot.com/2013/09/08/ ; 2018 note on “La Cueca del Filistoque” later reconstruction: https://culturaacompanada.blogspot.com/2018/08/museo-de-la-memoria-estrenara-cueca.html ; archive/blog overview of the 2003 *Pisagua + Chacabuco* reissue: https://folcloreyculturachilena.blogspot.com/2017/04/memoria-del-cantar-popular-musica.html .


### Small-web sweep: additional screens and false positives (2026-11-15)
- **Es lo que hay — Unidad 30, General Alvear, Argentina (2008):** an independent blog reports a band formed by incarcerated students and rehearsing inside the prison, but says the planned first album was to be recorded at an outside studio in Luján in February 2008. No completed prison-made recording is established. CONTEXT ONLY / not a candidate unless a later in-prison master is found. Source: https://accionmutantefm.blogspot.com/2008/01/un-grupo-de-presos-est-punto-de-grabar.html
- **Ivan Kuchin — *Vozvrashchenie domoi* / “Возвращение домой” (Russia, c.1985–87):** Russian small-web searches surfaced biographies claiming that Kuchin recorded songs while imprisoned and that a 1987 tape was confiscated but circulated through police contacts. Later studio albums *Novinki lagernoi liriki* and *Letyat goda* were released by Marathon in the 1990s. This is not a priority: Kuchin became a well-known chanson performer, the original prison-tape chronology is inconsistent across secondary biographies, and the later releases are not proof that the surviving commercial recordings were made in prison. CONTEXT ONLY; do not confuse “camp/prison lyric” as a genre/marketing phrase with recording location. Sources: https://musstrateg.blogspot.com/2019/ ; https://russianshanson.info/catalog/196/albums/8060 ; https://www.shanson-plus.ru/forums/index.php?topic=54738.300
- **Atrofia Cerebral — *Matanza Extrema* (Peru):** cassette-blog material links the cover art to the 1986 prison massacre, but this is a grind/noise cassette whose cover references a prison atrocity; no evidence that the music was made or recorded by prisoners. FALSE POSITIVE for this project's admission criteria. Source: https://buhrecords.blogspot.com/2016/
- **“Studio du Temps,” Saint-Maur Prison, France:** prisoners spent decades digitising INA sound archives under Nicolas Frize's programme. This is significant cultural labour inside a prison but the digitised recordings are pre-existing archive materials, not music made by the incarcerated workers. CONTEXT ONLY, not a recording by prisoners. Source: https://radiofanch.blogspot.com/2026/04/


### African small-web sweep — screens, not new candidates (2026-11-15)
- **Zomba Prison Project / Malawi:** the small-blog search re-surfaced the 2013 inmate recording sessions and Grammy-nominated *I Have No Everything Here*. This is already covered in the calibration/exclusions section; it is highly publicised and should remain CONTEXT ONLY, not a fresh discovery.
- **TID / *Prison Voice* (Tanzania, 2009):** the album title and songs refer to the artist's experience serving a one-year sentence, but the source describes it as a post-release album launch; it does not establish that the tracks were recorded in prison. CONTEXT ONLY; do not admit on title/theme alone. Source: https://makavulive.blogspot.com/2009/10/tid-kutambulisha-album-yake-prison.html
- **Gemann / *14* (Ghana, c.2009):** a blog says the gospel singer was recording an album named for the 14 years he spent in prison. This is a post-release autobiographical project, not evidence of in-prison recording. CONTEXT ONLY. Source: https://beckykwei.blogspot.com/2009/05/
- **Lamboginny / Kirikiri concerts (Nigeria, 2014):** a blog interview describes a series of visiting-musician shows for inmates, not music composed or recorded by inmates. CONTEXT ONLY. Source: https://adadike.blogspot.com/2014/07/why-i-sensitize-prison-inmates-with.html
- **Yaw Sarpong prison music-centre proposal (Ghana, 2017):** planned music education and a future album launch were reported, but no identified inmate-made recording was found. Do not treat the planned project as an extant recording. Source: https://cypressgh.blogspot.com/2017/08/yaw-sarpong-to-build-music-centres-for.html


### Sufferras Crew follow-up — “La Vida En El Penal” and dead source download (2026-11-15)
- A specific track has now been identified: **“La Vida En El Penal (Automatic Riddim)”**, attributed to The Sufferras / Sufferras Crew. A YouTube upload by Videos Luis was published 7 March 2010 (3:33; search result reported 999,592 views at retrieval). A later official Costa Rican reggae portal's riddim pack tracklist also lists “The Sufferras Crew – La Vida En El Penal (Automatic Riddim).”
- This is a stronger item-level lead than the unnamed 2012 download. The 2012 Rootstico post says the five-member group had recorded inside La Reforma; the 2013 *Diario Extra* report confirms the group formed and made music inside the prison. However, neither source explicitly states that **this particular track** was recorded there. The 2010 YouTube upload date is a publication date, not a session date.
- The MediaFire link in Rootstico's 2012 post (ID `gydgyyk1e2g`) currently returns 404. Do not substitute a rip from YouTube, SoundCloud or third-party riddim packs for the original prison source. The Rootstico page embeds two other YouTube videos, but the available search route throttled the embedded pages and their identity has not been resolved.
- **Next verification:** ask the original blog author or producer/label contacts (E.G.V. Records, Cultura Roots, DJ Zion) for the original 2010 session documentation and the 2012 file identity; establish whether “La Vida En El Penal” was recorded inside La Reforma, by which members, and whether it is the same recording as the later riddim-pack version. No contact has been made.
- Sources: YouTube upload by Videos Luis (7 Mar. 2010): https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cfV_pCwWTBw ; ReggaeTico official riddim pack listing: https://reggaetico.com/reggaetico-riddims-pack-tracklist/ ; Rootstico 2012 post / now-dead MediaFire link: https://rootstico.blogspot.com/2012/05/sufferas-crew.html ; *Diario Extra* (23 May 2013): https://www.diarioextra.com/noticia/reos-impartiran-justicia/ .


## New environment and archive leads — residential schools, refugee settlements and liberation-front studios (2026-11-15)

### Cecilia Jeffrey and Birtle Residential School Choir — Presbyterian Church in Canada Archives, Canada (undated cassette; digitised 2011)
- **Status:** PARTIALLY VERIFIED archival sound object / PROMISING research lead; do not yet treat as a fully contextualised main-discography recording.
- **Object:** Presbyterian Church in Canada Archives, Audio-Visual Material file **2011-7001**, titled “Cecilia Jeffrey Residential School and Birtle Residential School Choir.” The official finding aid says the tape contains 30 songs, has no indication of when or where the songs were recorded, and was digitised by Rev. Stephen Kendall in early 2011. The digitisation comprises two master WAV files (one per side) and 30 individual CDA files, stored on two CDs.
- **Environment:** Cecilia Jeffrey Residential School operated near Shoal Lake, Ontario, from 1902 to 1976; Birtle Residential School operated in Manitoba. The archive guide describes both as Presbyterian-operated schools until transfer to federal control in 1969. The tape title associates the choir with both schools, but does not establish that the recording was made at either school, during their operating years, or by students rather than staff, alumni or a later commemorative choir.
- **Why it merits investigation:** this is an extant, item-level 30-song recording preserved in an institutional archive, and the finding aid provides an exact file number and digitisation format. It is a rare concrete audio lead within residential-school archives, where music activity is often documented only through photographs, programmes and written reports.
- **Obscurity / disposition:** PROMISING archive lead. The archive object is not publicly identified as a commercial release, but no evidence yet establishes whether it is a school-era recording or a later compilation. Do not claim that all 30 songs were performed by children who attended either school.
- **Ethics / next steps:** this is a profoundly sensitive Indigenous residential-school context. Do not seek to identify child performers from voices or archival records; do not publish lyrics, audio, names or personal details without appropriate permissions and consultation with affected Indigenous communities/survivors and the archive.
