Establish the tape's donor/custodian, date, venue, choir membership, purpose and recording chain through the archive's non-restricted catalogue/context. The finding aid states that copyrighted material may not be published without copyright-holder permission. No listening or archive contact has occurred.
- **Sources:** Presbyterian Church in Canada Archives, *Guide to Records Relating to the Residential Schools*, Audio-Visual Material file 2011-7001 (pp. 98 and 137): https://presbyterianarchives.ca/wp-content/uploads/2022/08/FA-Residential-Schools-Thematic-Guide-Public-version.pdf ; archive exhibit “Cecilia Jeffrey School” (school history and contextual evidence of musical life): https://presbyterianarchives.ca/2018/08/17/cecilia-jeffrey-school/ ; National Centre for Truth and Reconciliation school record (Cecilia Jeffrey, Kenora, 1902–1976): https://nctr.ca/residential-schools/ontario/cecilia-jeffrey-shoal-lake/ .

### Eritrean People’s Liberation Front / Dmtsi Hafash travelling studio and master-cassette archive — Eritrea, 1970s–1991
- **Status:** VERIFIED archive and recording practice / CONTEXT ONLY at collection level; a strong new environment/archive route, not yet a priority obscure-recording entry.
- **Recording infrastructure:** Dmtsi Hafash (“Voice of the Masses”), the EPLF radio station, began broadcasting in 1979. A 2026 open-access study based on oral histories with studio manager Yosief Kidanemariam and archivist Daret Yosief Elyas documents central and travelling recording sessions, reel-to-reel and cassette recording, master-cassette numbering by brigade/cultural troupe and date, typed lyric books, diaspora duplication/distribution, and a post-independence digitisation project. The Ministry of Information in Asmara reportedly retains the original cassettes and accompanying lyric books.
- **Field environments:** sessions took place at remote front-line locations, including Arag and Anberbeb; later, a mobile team carried microphones, amplifiers and recorders to comrades in dispersed units. Recordings were scheduled at night to reduce the risk of detection by aircraft. One complete show was recorded in a field-hospital unit with EPLF comrades and injured patients present. The article does not provide the hospital recording's accession, date, full tracklist or exact performer roster.
- **Named repertoire / artists:** the study discusses Estifanos “Wedi Zemach” Zemach's “Bdho” (recorded c. mid-1980s), “Salina” by Wedi Tukul, Uqbagabr Gebretensae's “Filmawit Ade,” and Elsa Kidane's “Weledi.” These are useful search keys, not proof that each named song belongs to the unidentified field-hospital show.
- **Editorial disposition:** the wider EPLF corpus is politically curated and intentionally circulated as part of a national liberation project; several songs became well known. Do not label it outsider music or automatically prioritise the collection. The unidentified frontline/hospital field recordings may have separate discovery value if exact masters and performers can be identified.
- **Ethics / next steps:** contextualise the EPLF's political programme, multilingual representation and editorial censorship; do not treat the archive as politically neutral or assume a single uncontested Eritrean national narrative. Distinguish fighters' performances, civilian contributions, hospital patients' participation, studio recordings, field recordings, radio broadcasts and later re-recordings. The original master archive is held by the Ministry of Information; public access and reuse terms are unclear. No archive contact, listening or rights request has occurred.
- **Sources:** Raymok M. Ketema, “Music circulation, war and the diaspora: a history of the travelling music studio during the Eritrean liberation struggle (1970s–1990s),” *Africa* 96(1), published online 6 April 2026 (open access): https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/africa/article/music-circulation-war-and-the-diaspora-a-history-of-the-travelling-music-studio-during-the-Eritrean-liberation-struggle-1970s1990s/47CA7BDB16C23B8920810EC6D1EFA6A7 ; PDF: https://www.cambridge.org/core/services/aop-cambridge-core/content/view/47CA7BDB16C23B8920810EC6D1EFA6A7/S0001972025101733a.pdf/music_circulation_war_and_the_diaspora_a_history_of_the_travelling_music_studio_during_the_Eritrean_liberation_struggle_1970s1990s.pdf .

### Screened refugee-settlement recording projects — Za’atari, Jordan and Calais, France (2015–2026)
These are extant recordings made in refugee settlements, but the known projects are too publicly promoted or collaborative to be priorities under the obscurity threshold. They are retained as context and as models for finding more independent resident-made material.

- **Za’atari, Jordan — *Songs from Zaatari* / Recording Earth (2015):** Recording Earth documents a November 2015 project interviewing and filming Syrian refugee musicians, with a rough-cut film and associated music/recording material. Its project page says rights to the film must be obtained from Oliver Burton. Oxford researcher Graihagh Cordwell's 2024 thesis describes Za’atari residents, including women, composing songs about displacement, war and camp life; however, the thesis abstract alone does not identify a particular surviving recording. The later Zaatari Music Program established an in-camp studio in 2023; its *Amal / Hope* album (2026) was recorded at the camp with teachers and students, with six tracks including songs credited to camp-based composers/performers and traditional material. This is an organised education programme and professionally mixed/mastered in Spain, not an obscure independent tape. **Context only.**
  - Sources: Recording Earth project page: https://www.recordingearth.com/portfolio/songs-from-zaatari-refugee-music-project ; Cordwell, *Once upon a time in Za’atari refugee camp: music and Syrian displacement in Jordan* (Oxford DPhil, 2024): https://ora.ox.ac.uk/objects/uuid%3Af76cadb1-5dcb-4a9f-a7ec-9457787ede6c ; Playing for Change Foundation, *Amal / Hope* (tracklist and credits): https://playingforchange.org/amal/ ; Jordan News Agency Petra, 18 June 2026: https://petra.gov.jo/dynpage/index.php/en/news/zaatari-refugee-children-release-hope-album .
- **Calais “Jungle,” France — *The Calais Sessions* (2016):** 13-track album recorded in a makeshift camp studio, with refugee residents alongside UK volunteer musicians. Bandcamp credits engineer Damien Barriere-Constantin and says sales supported refugee musicians and Citizens UK; individual track pages distinguish some camp church singers and collaborations. Al Jazeera reports that the project involved 20 refugee performers; Bandcamp Daily selected the album in its 2016 best-albums list. It is a valuable example of resident agency and collaborative recording, but **CONTEXT ONLY** for obscurity. Do not describe all album tracks as refugee-made: some are visiting musicians' contributions or arrangements.
  - Sources: official Bandcamp album/tracklist and recording context: https://thecalaissessions.bandcamp.com/album/the-calais-sessions-2 ; Al Jazeera, 21 Aug. 2016: https://www.aljazeera.com/features/2016/8/21/the-calais-sessions-and-the-music-of-kasper ; Bandcamp Daily year-end list: https://daily.bandcamp.com/best-of-2016/bandcamp-best-albums-of-2016-100-81 .


### Leprosy Mission Canada / Effect:Hope — multinational audiovisual archive and location-specific audio-tape route (collection-level; item contents unverified)
- **Status:** CONTEXT / ARCHIVE ROUTE ONLY. Do not count the listed tapes as patient-made music recordings without item-level descriptions.
- The International Leprosy Association's archive directory describes the TLM Canada (now Effect:Hope) collection as holding extensive audiovisual material from leprosy mission work. It lists audio tapes made “on” Purulia and Chandraghona (India/Bangladesh region), Bhutan, Irian Jaya, Papua New Guinea and Thailand; numerous video tapes; eleven 16mm film reels; and a cassette/pamphlet item “More Than a Prophet” associated with 1978 slides.
- The listing does not provide individual tape identifiers, dates, tracklists or sound descriptions. “Made on” a leprosy settlement does not prove the recordings contain music, were made by residents, or even feature residents rather than missionary staff. The “More Than a Prophet” cassette may be religious teaching material; no evidence yet links it to a patient choir or resident music.
- **Why retain:** this is a multinational archive route across several under-researched leprosy institutions and settlements, potentially useful for locating local music, worship, community events or patient-made material. It may also preserve only missionary documentation, so the distinction must be tested.
- **Next:** locate a detailed audiovisual inventory and establish the source of each tape, recorder, date, location, participants and sound contents. Search the related TLM International and country-branch archives, plus the named local hospitals/settlements. Do not describe any as a music recording until the catalogue or audio establishes that. No contact or listening has occurred.
- **Sources:** International Leprosy Association, TLM Canada / Effect:Hope archive description: https://leprosyhistory.org/archive/archive714/ ; related TLM International archive route: https://leprosyhistory.org/archive/archive718/ ; related Angus Library holdings on Chandraghona, Cuttack and Chingleput: https://leprosyhistory.org/archive/archive698/ .

### Screened remote-environment archive false positives
- **Great Lakes Lighthouse Keepers oral-history cassette (Michigan, Aug. 1986):** Smithsonian catalogue identifies one compact cassette excerpt from the Great Lakes Lighthouse Keepers Association oral-history tape collection, FP-1987-CT-0024-2. The catalogue does not describe it as a keeper-made music recording; it is an oral-history excerpt. Context/archive route only, not a music candidate. Source: https://collections.si.edu/search/results.htm?fq=object_type%3A%22Sound+recordings%22&q=set_name%3A%22Smithsonian+Folklife+Festival+records%3A+1987+Festival+of+American+Folklife%22 .
- **Casey Antarctic station (1989 cassette):** an Australian Antarctic Program account says a cassette of expeditioners' favourite music was found in a 1989 time capsule at Casey station. This is a compilation of music the crew listened to, not evidence of music made or recorded by station residents. Context only / false positive for the main discography. Source: https://www.antarctica.gov.au/news/stations/casey/2016/this-week-at-casey-2-september-2016/ .
- **Percy Riley USSR coal-mining delegation tape (Nov. 1983):** University of Sheffield catalogue identifies a 90-minute cassette about coal mining and workers' conditions; the last five minutes contain recorded music. This appears to be music included on a visitor's documentary tape, not music made by miners. Context only unless the source of the music is separately identified. Source: https://archives.shef.ac.uk/repositories/3/archival_objects/56923 .


## West Papua / Papua New Guinea refugee and exile music — new research (2026-11-15)

### “Papua Barat” — unidentified West Papuan song recorded in a refugee camp, Papua New Guinea (date unknown)
- **Status:** VERIFIED recording existence by programme/archive description / HIGH PRIORITY item-level lead. The track itself is not separately issued or fully catalogued.
- **Object:** Fred Gales's Concertzender programme *De Klankbron 130: Op expeditie naar West Papua* (rebroadcast of the revised 2026 programme) lists track 16, **“Papua Barat”**, duration **4:15**. The playlist explicitly says it was recorded in a refugee camp in Papua New Guinea on a cassette titled “refugee camp”; the recordings are held by a number of Papua refugees and exiles in the Netherlands. The programme describes the song as critical of the situation in West Papua.
- **Unknowns:** performer(s), language, exact refugee camp, recording date, original cassette owner, recording engineer, whether the extant programme audio is sourced from the original cassette or a later transfer, and whether the cassette contains other tracks. Do not assume Blackwater or East Awin: the camp is not identified.
- **Why it fits:** an unpublished song recorded in a refugee camp, preserved through refugee/exile personal collections rather than a commercial label or formal public archive. This is a direct fit with the refugee-settlement and exile pathways and potentially one of the most valuable recent discoveries.
- **Next:** identify the “refugee camp” cassette among PACE / Fred Gales / West Papuan exile collections; ask for a tape-box image, side contents, original owner and transfer chain; establish performer identity only with consent. Compare the 4:15 programme track with the original cassette without assuming the radio broadcast grants reuse permission. No listening or contact occurred in this research pass.
- **Sources:** Concertzender playlist, track 16 (4:15 and cassette description): https://www.concertzender.nl/programma/907825/ ; mirrored/related current programme listing: https://www.concertzender.nl/programma_woj/916291/ ; PACE Papua Collecties / Fred Gales collection overview: https://www.papuacollecties.nl/ ; ANU Press, *Permissive Residents*, on West Papuan refugee camps and music: https://press-files.anu.edu.au/downloads/press/p77101/mobile/ch02.html .

### Sampari — West Papuan refugee musicians, Blackwater camp, Vanimo, Papua New Guinea; later Netherlands exile (1984 onward)
- **Status:** PARTIALLY VERIFIED / PROMISING refugee-made recording corpus. The group’s formation and recording activity in exile are documented; surviving PACE items are identified, but their exact relation to the 1984 Blackwater recordings is not yet established.
- **Formation and context:** ANU Press research reports that West Papuan musicians formed **Sampari** (“Morning Star” in Biak) at Blackwater refugee camp, Vanimo, in August 1984, after fleeing Jayapura. From their place of exile they arranged and recorded “songs of the struggle,” including “Blue 7 White 6,” a reference to the stripes of the West Papuan flag. The group performed at East Awin in 1989; members later dispersed to PNG and the Netherlands. The source does not identify a surviving master of “Blue 7 White 6.”
- **Surviving item A — self-published CD-R “Traditional Papua Wor songs” / “Traditionele Papua Wor liederen” (PACE Band 169):** a 24-track Imation CD-R with a jewel case and sleeve naming all 24 tracks. PACE describes Sampari as a music-and-dance group of Papuans in exile in the Netherlands for many years; the group self-published the CD and sold it at performances. The songs are mainly in Biak with some Bahasa words, in a traditional Wor style. Track 24 is “Papua Merdeka.” Recording date, recording venue, pressing quantity, member credits and whether this is a direct digital copy of a master are not stated. Do not assume the CD-R was recorded at Blackwater camp.
- **Surviving item B — “Sampari Aneka Lagu” cassette (PACE Band 189):** a TDK D60 cassette with handwritten sleeve by W. Blom. Side A contains a VPRO *De Wandelende Tak* radio broadcast with a live Sampari performance recorded at “The End of the World,” Amsterdam, on **26 June 1986** (28:24). Side B (31:21) contains “Aneka Lagu,” described as string-band/kroncong-style material that seems to come from an album; its performer and source are not confirmed. PACE offers access to the B side by request. This item is a clearly dated exile performance, not the Blackwater-camp master.
- **Why it fits:** the group's recordings emerged from a refugee community displaced by political violence and maintained cultural practice and political expression in exile. The self-published CD-R is a particularly promising low-circulation object; the 1986 live broadcast is a valuable provenance anchor.
- **Cautions / next steps:** distinguish Sampari from Mambesak, the later Netherlands-based Mambesak group, and other similarly named diaspora ensembles. Preserve local language and spelling; verify track titles and translations with community members. Determine whether “Blue 7 White 6” or other Blackwater songs survive on an unindexed cassette. No listening, contact or reuse permission occurred.
- **Sources:** ANU Press, *Permissive Residents*, chapter 2 (formation at Blackwater, 1984; songs of struggle; East Awin performances): https://press-files.anu.edu.au/downloads/press/p77101/mobile/ch02.html ; PACE Papua Collecties, Music page, Band 169 and Band 189: https://www.papuacollecties.nl/music-groups/ ; Concertzender playlist, Sampari / “Yoruso” and refugee-camp track: https://www.concertzender.nl/programma/907825/ ; PACE/Rijksmuseum collection overview: https://www.papuacollecties.nl/rijksmuseum-voor-volkenkunde/ .

### Arnold Ap / Mambesak — original reels, radio programmes and the five-volume cassette series (West Papua, 1977–1984)
- **Status:** VERIFIED archive corpus / CONTEXT ONLY for the commercially circulated five-volume cassette series; PROMISING for the less-circulated original reels, prison-made recordings and surviving radio broadcasts.
- **Historical context:** Mambesak was founded in 1978 by Arnold Ap, Sam Kapissa, Yowi Kafiar and Marthinny Sawaki. It collected, arranged and performed songs in multiple Papuan languages and regional styles, and became extremely popular in West Papua. ANU Press scholarship describes the five volumes of folksongs from nine regions recorded between 1978 and 1983 and marketed throughout the province; the lyrics were transcribed in a 1980 songbook. After Ap's arrest and death in 1984, the University stopped selling the cassettes; the music became taboo in official settings but was widely copied by refugees and activists. The published series is not “unknown” locally and should not be oversold as outsider music.
- **Original Freerk Kamma / PACE tapes:** PACE documents three original reels recorded by Arnold Ap and sent to Freerk Kamma:
  - **Band 34, “Arnold Ap 1”:** box dates 9 Nov. 1977 and 6 Apr. 1979; 9.5 and 4.75 cm/sec; Arnold Ap introduction/interview, string-band songs probably Mambesak, regional song examples, a radio broadcast with presenters and Mambesak songs, tifa songs and commentary. Side A 49:18; Side B 86:31.
  - **Band 35, “Arnold Ap 2 side A”:** reel with Ap commentary, Wor/Koreri songs, tifa accompaniment, regional songs and a Mambesak song from Sentani; side B is described as classical music and not listenable. Side A 97:34; Side B 74:35.
  - **Band 36, “Arnold Ap III Wor”:** Wor songs, Ap commentary, two named Mambesak songs “Wambeso” and “Emambo,” further Mambesak songs; English pop songs at the end may have been recorded by members of the Kamma family. Side A 77:28; Side B 4:51.
  - **Band 130, “Ap wor 1”:** a later 2005 digitisation of Band 34 using another tape recorder; duplicate transfer, not an additional recording.
  - **Band 141, “Cassette F”:** a Sony HF60 copy of an Arnold Ap tape, now in the PKN collection at Utrecht Archive; side A and B include Mambesak songs, Ap commentary, radio presenters and a conch-shell trumpet. Side A 31:21; Side B 30:48.
- **Five-volume series:** PACE identifies Band 208 (Mambesak 1) and Band 209 (Mambesak 5) as homemade copies; Bands 186, 187 and 188 (Mambesak 2–4) as original university-published cassettes. The original series was issued by Lembaga Antropologi, Universitas Cenderawasih, as *Seri Lagu Rakyat Iriani*. PACE gives tracklists and carrier condition, including track-order discrepancies and unlisted tracks. The 1986 Concertzender programme describes the series as discontinued after Ap's death and circulated through refugees and activists.
- **Further archival route:** the PACE description of the Rijksmuseum voor Volkenkunde audio collection lists cassettes copied from Gerson Kaigere, including Mambesak I–IV, Kurana Mambesak Group vols. 2–3, Sandia Group vols. 1–2, Apuse Group Biak vols. 1 and 3, Tekad Group Jayapura vol. 1, Winggemi, Manbefor Irian, Kamasan and Rumgiri groups. Other original cassettes catalogued by PACE include Yanero Group gospel/string-band cassettes (Bands 174–175), Apuse Group (Band 177), Tune Manaa vols. 2–3 (Bands 180–181), Kurana Mambesak Group vol. 1 (Band 185), and other regional string-band recordings. These establish a much wider local cassette scene, but dates, locations, roles and social provenance need item-level verification.
- **Next:** prioritise original unpublished Arnold Ap reels and refugee-held copies of his radio programmes, then map the broader group-cassette scene with community and language-specific sources. The ANU chapter notes that Ap continued recording in prison, including visits by a Mambesak member; no surviving prison-tape accession has yet been identified. Do not conflate that statement with the three reels described by PACE, whose dates and provenance differ.
- **Sources:** PACE, Arnold Ap item-level archive: https://www.papuacollecties.nl/arnold-ap/ ; PACE, Music / group cassettes: https://www.papuacollecties.nl/music-groups/ ; PACE, Rijksmuseum voor Volkenkunde holdings: https://www.papuacollecties.nl/rijksmuseum-voor-volkenkunde/ ; ANU Press, *Permissive Residents*, chapter 2: https://press-files.anu.edu.au/downloads/press/p77101/mobile/ch02.html ; PACE newsletter no. 39 (May 2025), on missing radio broadcasts and surviving Arnold Ap tapes: https://www.papuaerfgoed.org/sites/default/files/2025-06/Pace%20Nieuwsbrief%20proef%202025%201%2039%20nieuw.pdf ; Concertzender, updated 2026 West Papua programme: https://www.concertzender.nl/programma/907825/ .


### Arnold Ap — “Mystery of Life” / “Misteri Hidup” / “Misteri Kehidupan” — Jayapura detention, West Papua (1984)
- **Status:** VERIFIED prison-made recording by multiple published accounts / HIGH PRIORITY. The original cassette's current custodian and access status remain unverified.
- **Recording:** Arnold Ap, musician, anthropologist and Mambesak founder, recorded his final song in his prison cell shortly before his death in April 1984. Alex Rayfiel's 2007 *Inside Indonesia* account says Ap sat beside a portable tape recorder with his guitar, recorded “The Mystery of Life,” wrapped the cassette in an envelope with a message of consolation and sent it to his wife, who had fled to a refugee camp in Papua New Guinea. The song's closing refrain expresses a desire for freedom. A 2018 interview with his wife Corry Ap says a relative brought a cassette recorder and guitar to the jail, Ap wrote his final song and entrusted the cassette to be delivered to her. An ANU Press chapter independently confirms Ap continued composing and recording from prison with a guitar, recorder and cassettes, and that a fellow Mambesak member sometimes visited to sing and make recordings.
- **Title / language:** Sources use English title “The Mystery of Life” and Indonesian/Biak forms “Misteri Hidup” / “Misteri Kehidupan.” A 2001 ethnographic chapter quotes the Biak text beginning “Hidup ini suatu misteri” and gives an Indonesian translation. Confirm the original Biak title and exact wording with family/community sources; do not treat modern Indonesian translations as original titles.
- **Important distinction:** later performances and studio re-recordings are not the prison master. Eyuser's 2020 album *Didedikasikan* includes a 3:40 track “Misteri Kehidupan,” but this is a later tribute/re-recording, not the 1984 prison cassette. The 2009 David Bridie studio recordings used in the *Strange Birds in Paradise* project are also later recordings. Do not substitute them for the original.
- **Related but distinct prison song:** ANU Press scholarship also quotes another Biak-language song translated as “I am sailing away,” describing Ap's yearning for his ancestral land and his people's suffering. The chapter places this in its discussion of his prison compositions, but it does not establish that this is the same composition as “Mystery of Life” or identify a surviving recording. Keep as a separate composition lead, not a track on the known cassette.
- **Why it fits:** an original song recorded by a political prisoner in his cell, on a portable recorder, then smuggled out to a family member in exile. This is a rare and unusually well-documented intersection of prison recording, political repression and refugee circulation.
- **Obscurity assessment:** HIGH PRIORITY for locating the original cassette and its transfer history. Ap and Mambesak are iconic in West Papua, so do not market the musician as obscure; the particular prison recording remains a rare archival object.
- **Next:** identify whether the original cassette survives with Corry Ap / the family, the PACE collection, or another exile archive; establish the recording date, original carrier, message/envelope, transfer generation, full duration and any additional prison recordings. Ask permission and follow family/community preferences before publishing or linking audio. No contact or listening occurred.
- **Sources:** Alex Rayfiel, “Singing for life,” *Inside Indonesia*, 26 July 2007: https://www.insideindonesia.org/editions/edition-7829/singing-for-life ; Danilyn Rutherford, “Culture as the conscious object of performance,” ANU Press, *Permissive Residents*: https://press-files.anu.edu.au/downloads/press/p77101/mobile/ch02.html ; “West Papuans in exile: A conversation with Corry Ap,” Free West Papua Campaign, 25 Dec. 2018: https://www.freewestpapua.org/2018/12/25/corryap/ ; “The Murder of Arnold Ap and Eduard Mofu,” TAPOL / *West Papua: The Obliteration of a People*: https://www.freewestpapua.org/wp-content/uploads/2019/10/budiardjo_1983_obliteration.pdf ; Eyuser, *Didedikasikan* (2020; later tribute/re-recording): https://eyuser.bandcamp.com/album/didedikasikan ; *Strange Birds in Paradise* production blog (2009 re-recordings): https://strangebirdsdocop.wordpress.com/tag/david-bridie/ .


### East Awin refugee-settlement song recordings — Diana Glazebrook field tapes, Papua New Guinea (1998–1999)
- **Status:** PARTIALLY VERIFIED field-recording corpus / HIGH PRIORITY archive-tracing lead. The researcher explicitly confirms that she tape-recorded songs at East Awin, but no tape accession, tracklist, custodian or access route has been located.
- **Direct evidence of recordings:** In *Permissive Residents: West Papuan Refugees Living in Papua New Guinea* (ANU E Press, 2008), Diana Glazebrook states: “At East Awin, I tape-recorded song, but never speech.” Her fieldwork dates are March–September 1998 and February–September 1999. She explains that she avoided recording speech because participants were deciding whether to return to West Papua and she did not want to retain potentially dangerous testimony about their political lives. This is an important privacy rationale: do not seek or publish speech recordings, and do not infer that song recordings are unrestricted.
- **Songs described in the book (not yet crosswalked to tapes):**
  - Dani “songs of sadness” (*lagu kesedihan*), six discrete songs sung in the period before burial and forty days after burial; lyrics invoke distant Wamena, family, hunger and return. Chapter 11 says one song was composed in Indonesian by Dani children whose parents fled the Baliem Valley in 1977.
  - Muyu funeral lamentation (*tamagop*) “You are strong, I am strong,” composed by a Muyu woman and sung at funerals; it encodes the Indonesian military and OPM as “you” and “I” and recalls flight in 1984. Chapter 4.
  - Yonggom-language “Cassava Song,” arranged by two Muyu teachers at Iowara and performed during a bishop's visit in August 1999; it expresses the desire to return to the homeland where sago grows. Chapter 7.
  - “It's said you want to leave,” a song about the departure of another refugee and the loss of everyday social life at East Awin. Chapter 5.
  - Other songs and laments are described in the book, but should not be presumed to be on Glazebrook's tapes.
- **Why it fits:** resident-composed and resident-performed songs emerged from a long-term refugee settlement and express displacement, memory, grief and political uncertainty. The existence of a researcher-held sound corpus is confirmed, while the individual tracks remain unlocated and largely unpublished.
- **Next:** determine whether Glazebrook's field tapes survive, where they are held, whether any song recordings can be described without exposing participants, and which songs in the book correspond to actual audio. Any inquiry should respect her stated reason for not recording speech and should seek community/survivor guidance before listening, copying or publishing. Do not seek to identify unnamed songwriters or performers. No contact or listening occurred.
- **Sources:** Diana Glazebrook, *Permissive Residents: West Papuan Refugees Living in Papua New Guinea* (ANU E Press, 2008), especially Chapter 4 (Muyu tamagop), Chapter 5 (“It's said you want to leave”), Chapter 7 (“Cassava Song”), Chapter 11 (Dani songs of sadness), and the methodological note on tape-recording songs but not speech: https://press-files.anu.edu.au/downloads/press/p77101/mobile/ch02.html ; full PDF: https://www.wpik.org/Src/permissive_residents.pdf ; chapter 4: https://press-files.anu.edu.au/downloads/press/p77101/mobile/ch04.html ; chapter 5: https://press-files.anu.edu.au/downloads/press/p77101/mobile/ch05.html ; chapter 7: https://press-files.anu.edu.au/downloads/press/p77101/mobile/ch07.html ; chapter 11: https://press-files.anu.edu.au/downloads/press/p77101/mobile/ch11.html .


## Southeast Asian refugee-camp music — Cambodian, Karen and Karenni recordings (2026-11-15)

### Kieng Yuthhan / “Blue Basket” (“Sronos Kantong Khiev”) — SITE 2 refugee camp, Thailand (November 1986)
- **Status:** VERIFIED refugee-camp cassette recording / CONTEXT ONLY for obscurity; original full cassette-album remains a PROMISING recovery target.
- **Recording:** Cambodian singer and guitarist Kieng Yuthhan, born 1953 near Battambang, reached SITE 2 refugee camp in Thailand after the Khmer Rouge period. In November 1986 he and his group recorded “Blue Basket” (Khmer title transcribed as “Sronos Kantong Khiev,” with spelling variants) in a makeshift bamboo studio they built inside the camp. They used a cassette recorder, instruments borrowed from others, and two microphones loaned by local monks. The tapes were sent to Cambodian companies in the United States for duplication and distribution. Mark Gergis met and interviewed Kieng in Cambodia in 2013 and published the account on his Cambodian Cassette Archives blog in 2016.
- **Recording / release distinction:** the song is a rendition of a traditional folk tune in the Rom Saravan style, not necessarily an original composition. It was included as track 1 on *Cambodian Cassette Archives: Khmer Folk and Pop Music Vol. 1* (2004 CD; 2016 reissue on Sublime Frequencies). This later compilation means the known track is no longer obscure among cassette/world-music collectors. The original Kieng cassette-album and any additional unreissued tracks have not been identified.
- **Why retain:** a rare, well-documented example of refugee musicians creating their own studio infrastructure inside a camp and circulating recordings through diaspora companies. The original cassette album may contain more overlooked material than the reissued track.
- **Next:** locate the original Kieng cassette, cover, title, full tracklist and US duplication label; determine whether other artists in SITE 2 made cassette albums with similar production methods. Preserve the singer's preferred Khmer name/spelling and verify the group's members. No contact or audio audition occurred.
- **Sources:** Mark Gergis, Cambodian Cassette Archives, “Introducing: Kieng Yuthhan — The Singer Behind Blue Basket” (3 Apr. 2016): https://cambodiancassettearchives.blogspot.com/2016/04/cambodian-cassette-archives-khmer-folk.html ; Sublime Frequencies official Bandcamp reissue: https://sublime-frequencies.bandcamp.com/album/cambodian-cassette-archives-khmer-folk-pop-music-vol-1 ; release details and tracklist: https://www.forcedexposure.com/Catalog/va-cambodian-cassette-archives-khmer-folk-and-pop-music-vol-1-cd/SF.1011CD.html .

### Karen refugee cassette culture and Mae La studios — Thai–Myanmar border (1990s–2020s)
- **Status:** VERIFIED recording ecosystem / PROMISING archive route for early cassette albums; CONTEXT ONLY for the best-known 2017–2020 digital tracks.
- **1990s cassette production:** Manoch Chummuangpak's 2015 conference paper, based on field engagement with Karen refugee communities on the Thai–Burma border and in Melbourne, states that several Karen music albums on audio cassette had been produced on the Thai border since the 1990s. Early border singers/musicians included Hsar Gay Doh Htee, Danny, Kaw Law La and Tha Kolo; some maintained links with the Rangoon music industry and many later resettled in third countries. Some projects were supported by Karen political/religious organisations and others self-funded. Exact album titles, labels, dates, tracklists and camp locations remain to be identified.
- **Mae La studio infrastructure:** Charlotte Hill's 2021 Goldsmiths thesis documents three recording studios in Mae La. Saw Ka Lu and his musician brother began recording music in 2008 with a borrowed microphone and accessories from a local church, an old mixer and a bamboo-walled room; the studio later became soundproof and served both camp residents and diaspora collaborators. The thesis says 60% of the studio's clients lived inside Mae La (2018 account); the remaining clients included Thai-Karen villagers and diaspora musicians. Music was distributed via YouTube, Spotify and cross-border networks.
- **Specific resident-made recordings (public context, not obscure priorities):**
  - Pu Dah, “Refugee” (2020), by an encamped Karen singer who recorded in Saw Ka Lu's studio.
  - Hser, “We Need Unity” (2020), a self-composed song about Karen unity and freedom.
  - Tempered Family, “Revolution” (2020), a group recording with over 310,000 views by April 2021.
  - TNKG, “Who are you?” (2017), a camp-filmed rap/pop video with over 1.8 million views by April 2021.
  - NUCELER and OBED 7, “Life in MRM refugee camp” (2020), written/produced by Saw Na, a camp-born Karen refugee, describing the constraints of life at Mae Ra Moe.
- **Other recording route:** Hill's thesis describes Kaw Koh recording Christian music and messages in one of Mae La's studios and distributing them to other camps and villages using MegaVoice devices. This may include devotional songs but the thesis does not give a discrete tracklist. Treat as a route, not an identified music album.
- **Obscurity assessment:** the contemporary songs above are publicly visible and should be CONTEXT ONLY. The 1990s cassette albums and lesser-known camp-produced gospel/pop recordings are the promising targets. Do not treat the whole Karen scene as an undifferentiated political soundtrack; include everyday romance, devotional music, traditional instruments and internal debates about identity.
- **Ethics / next:** obtain exact cassette titles and labels from Karen musicians/collectors; distinguish studio owner, songwriter, singer, producer and funder; preserve preferred Karen language/romanisation and do not identify anonymous artists. No contact or listening occurred.
- **Sources:** Manoch Chummuangpak, “The Sound of Loss and Hope: Pop Music of Karen Refugees from Burma/Myanmar” (2015 conference paper): https://www.burmalibrary.org/en/the-sound-of-loss-and-hope-pop-music-of-karen-refugees-from-burmamyanmar ; Charlotte Hill, *Mediating Encamped Identities and Belongings: An Ethnography of Everyday Karen Life in Mae La Refugee Camp* (Goldsmiths PhD, 2021): https://research.gold.ac.uk/id/eprint/30385/8/13678779211027179.pdf ; full thesis: https://research.gold.ac.uk/31475/1/MCCS_thesis_HillC_2022.pdf ; Hill, “Poetic resistance: Karen long-distance nationalism, rap music, and YouTube” (2021): https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1177/13678779211027179 .

### Karenni — Nai Soi refugee camp field recordings (Thailand, April 1993; issued 1994)
- **Status:** VERIFIED camp performance recordings / CONTEXT ONLY for obscurity; important track-level archive reference.
- **Release:** *Karenni: Music from the Border Areas of Thailand and Burma*, PAN Records 2040CD (1994), 21 tracks, 75 minutes. Tracks 3–5 are credited to “Singers from Nai Soi refugee camp”: “Ami (Welcome Song)” (13:45), “Makoto” (5:10) and “The Journey” (5:03 on the commercial release). A Concertzender programme gives “The Journey” as 4:55 and identifies ten named singers/performers, including vocals and flutes, recorded by Fred Gales at Nai Soi refugee camp in April 1993. Track 6 “Everywhere I Meet Sorrow” (Lah Nai, with Maw Pein on guitar) and track 7 “In a Foreign Country I Remember” (Lah Nai) are further possible displacement-themed repertoire, but the available sources do not establish that these were recorded in the camp or composed there.
- **Why retain:** an item-level, dated field recording of Karenni refugee performers, with named singers and a specific camp location. The album was commercially issued and is available on streaming platforms, so it is not a hidden release; the individual performers and context may still be overlooked.
- **Next:** consult the 1994 CD booklet for full recording notes and exact performers; reconcile track duration and whether “The Journey” is the same recording as the 4:55 Concertzender version; establish whether Lah Nai's songs are refugee compositions or traditional repertoire.
- **Sources:** Concertzender / Fred Gales, “The Journey” recorded at Nai Soi in April 1993: https://www.concertzender.nl/programma/de_klankbron_760270/ ; Apple Music tracklist and performer credits: https://music.apple.com/us/album/karenni-music-from-the-border-areas-of-thailand-and-burma/350539435 ; Qobuz track details: https://www.qobuz.com/us-en/album/karenni-music-from-the-border-areas-of-thailand-and-burma-various-karenni-musicians-from-kayah-state/0713958204023 ; PAN Records catalogue context: https://www.edmu.fr/2014/08/pan-record-discographie.html .

### Palestinian Oral History Archive — refugee songs and folktales, Lebanon (1994–2009 field collections; digitised from cassettes)
- **Status:** CONTEXT / archive route only. The archive is principally oral history, not a music collection.
- **Holdings:** American University of Beirut's Palestinian Oral History Archive (POHA) consolidates over 800 audio/video testimonies, around 1,000 hours total, from first-generation Palestinians and other Palestinian communities in Lebanon. Its “Folktales” series includes 172 hours of audio interviews containing folktales, traditional songs, proverbs and poems; “Uprooting” includes 136 hours of audio recordings; “Ayn al-Hilwat” includes 36 hours of video interviews with women from Ein el-Helwe camp. Original analog cassette material was digitised. The archive provides a searchable platform and contact address.
- **Caution:** songs embedded in oral-history interviews are not necessarily stand-alone music performances or recordings made by camp residents. Preserve the distinction between song as testimony/repertoire, a complete musical performance, and a commercial recording. The archive is community-rooted and rights/access conditions should be checked before use.
- **Next:** search the public catalogue for song/folktale records with audio; identify any discrete performances recorded in refugee camps, and check whether performers were refugees and whether they consented to public access. No audio audition or archive request occurred.
- **Sources:** AUB Libraries, “About the Palestinian Oral History Archive”: https://libraries.aub.edu.lb/poha/Content/about .


## Further refugee-exile cassette and studio routes — Afghanistan and Liberia (2026-11-15)

### Afghan refugee musicians and cassette production — Peshawar, Pakistan (1985 onward)
- **Status:** VERIFIED exile-cassette scene / PROMISING item-level leads; the best-known institutional projects are CONTEXT ONLY.
- **1985 Haikal cassette:** John Baily's first-hand account in *War, Exile and the Music of Afghanistan* describes a cassette he bought in Peshawar in 1985 by **Haikal**, an Afghan Pashto singer formerly heard on Radio Afghanistan and then based in Peshawar. The tape contains a 40-minute epic about a particular mujahideen group operating in northern Afghanistan, accompanied by harmonium ('armonia), rubab and tabla; the tabla player imitated gunfire. Baily later identified Sattar Khan as the tabla player. The title, label, full artist identity, cassette cover and current master location are not given. The tape was sold in Peshawar's cassette bazaar and apparently bought by some fighters and taken back to Afghanistan.
- **Second unidentified war cassette:** Baily's 1985 study guide describes another cassette of solo Pashto singing with continuous gun-battle sounds mixed in. It may or may not be the Haikal tape; keep separate until identified.
- **Political context:** Baily reports that music was prohibited in many Afghan refugee camps under Islamist party/mullah authority; refugees could own radios for news but not music, and refugee musicians living in camps sometimes hid their identities. Many professional musicians therefore lived and worked in Peshawar outside camp control, where a thriving cassette industry circulated mostly love songs and a smaller number of resistance epics. Do not claim that the Haikal cassette was recorded inside a camp.
- **2001 Afghan Music Preservation project:** a U.S. State Department Ambassador's Fund grant supported six album-length recordings of Afghan musicians then residing in Pakistani refugee camps, recorded by Peshawar's Irfan Cultural Center / Development and Humanitarian Services for Afghanistan. The project aimed to document music for weddings and other life events; one completed CD contained ten folk tracks. The recordings were to be archived at the U.S. Library of Congress and broadcast by Voice of America. A 2024 U.S. State Department Cultural Heritage Center page confirms that six recordings were made and archived at LOC. No LOC item numbers, disc titles, tracklists or individual performers have been found in the public search; the actual session locations (inside camps or at Peshawar studios) are not specified. Treat as PROMISING archive route, not six fully verified camp-recorded albums.
- **Current cassette archive:** the Afghan Cassette Archive is an independent community preservation project working with Mohammad Hassan “Kaka Zmarai,” an Afghan refugee collector in Peshawar who has safeguarded thousands of tapes. The archive says the collection includes refugee recordings, radio-derived material, commercial cassettes and music produced within refugee communities; its site currently displays zero digitised tapes online, so this is a living archive route rather than an online catalogue.
- **Obscurity assessment:** the Afghan music scene in Peshawar and its resistance cassette culture are known to specialists, but the specific Haikal cassette, anonymous battle-sound tape, and unlocated 2001 LOC discs remain under-catalogued. PROMISING, pending original tape identification and item-level access.
- **Next:** identify the Haikal cassette title/label and current owner through John Baily's fieldwork records, Sattar Khan's network, and the Kaka Zmarai collection; request the LOC catalogue/search by grant/project name and VOA broadcast logs; establish which 2001 recordings were made in camps versus Peshawar studios. Do not assume all refugee musicians were camp residents or that all music was politically oppositional. No contact or listening occurred.
- **Sources:** John Baily, *War, Exile and the Music of Afghanistan* (2015), pp. 55–57 and 82–83 (first-hand 1985 cassette account): https://dokumen.pub/war-exile-and-the-music-of-afghanistan-the-ethnographers-tale-1nbsped-9781472415820-2015000814-9781315466934.html ; Baily, “Can You Stop the Birds Singing?” (2001): https://www.freemuse.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/01/Afghanistansats.pdf ; State Department, *Ambassador's Fund for Cultural Preservation 2001 Annual Report*: https://eca.state.gov/files/bureau/2001afcpannual.pdf ; State Department Cultural Heritage Center, “Honoring Music”: https://artsandculture.google.com/story/honoring-music-cultural-heritage-center-us-department-of-state/pAXRfQnU0j9cQQ ; Afghan Cassette Archive: https://afghancassettearchive.org/ ; John Baily, *Amir: An Afghan Refugee Musician's Life in Peshawar* (1985/86 film): https://films.der.org/film/metadata-amir-an-afghan-refugee-musician-s-life/e0f1ff76-dbc8-4005-b360-4a869896601a .

### Buduburam Liberian refugee camp music — “Giving Voice to Hope” and resident studio recordings (Ghana, 2007–2012)
- **Status:** VERIFIED refugee-made recording ecosystem / CONTEXT ONLY for the 2009 compilation; PROMISING for individual camp-studio masters and unreleased works.
- **Camp music production:** Buduburam, west of Accra, developed a flourishing refugee music scene. Michael Frishkopf's account describes popular musicians **S-Man / Samuel Taylor** and **Shadow / Samuel Morgan** producing music in their own studios inside the camp. The scene included hip-hop, reggae, gospel, traditional and pop groups; songs addressed war, abuse of power, peace, unity, everyday life and the experience of encampment. Shadow was a self-taught composer, rapper, audio editor and producer; his studio also became a meeting place for young people.
- **Compilation:** *Giving Voice to Hope: Music of Liberian Refugees* (University of Alberta, 2009) is a 16-track CD (68:48) featuring 16 groups who had resided as refugees in Buduburam. The CD's 16 demos were recorded across studios in Accra, Kasoa and Buduburam, so not every track can be assumed to have been recorded inside the camp. A 2009 University of Alberta guide describes the project as a cooperative initiative with the Center for Youth Empowerment; the 2011 guide says camp musicians collaborated to produce the CD. A 2014 review identifies individual songs and artists, including:
  - S-Man / Samuel Taylor — “Power”
  - Soul Whisperers — “Take Away”
  - Universal Vision — “About Time”
  - Shadow / Samuel Morgan — “Peace Must Prevail”
  - Wesley Toh and Alvin Page (“Big Seen”) — “Stop the Violence”
  - Calabash Unite Us All — “We are all Liberian”
  - Morris Haynes — “No More War”
  - Helbert Collins — “Shine for Jesus”
  - Constance Bowier — “Gbai Kai Yaa”
  - Representorz (Joseph Teah, Dave Nyema, Milton Farley) — “Je t'adore”
  - Blessed Brothers — “CYE Theme Song”
  - Alaric Crump — “Give a Little Love”
  The review does not provide track-by-track studio locations, and the list above should not be mistaken for a complete tracklist.
- **Circulation / obscurity:** the project was promoted by the University of Alberta and sold nearly 1,000 copies; royalties were returned to the musicians and the CYE NGO. It is a visible collaborative project, so the compilation itself is CONTEXT ONLY. Its value to the compendium is that it points to a much larger local recording ecosystem and original camp-studio recordings not necessarily included on the CD.
- **Next:** locate the 2009 CD booklet and project database; map each track to its studio, performer, songwriter and producer; identify S-Man's and Shadow's own camp-studio catalogues, original demos/cassettes and other locally circulated releases. Respect artist ownership and do not assume project participation grants permission to reuse masters.
- **Sources:** University of Alberta 2009 programme guide: https://www.ualberta.ca/en/international/media-library/gep/archive/programs/iweek2009programguide.pdf ; 2011 programme guide: https://www.ualberta.ca/en/international/media-library/gep/archive/programs/iweek2011programguide.pdf ; Michael Frishkopf, “Music for Global Human Development: Participatory Action Research for Health and Wellbeing” (Buduburam studio and 16-group CD): https://www.academia.edu/117245925/Music_for_Global_Human_Development_Participatory_Action_Research_for_Health_and_Wellbeing ; Michael Thorn, review of *Giving Voice to Hope* (2014, track examples): https://www.academia.edu/84817228/Giving_Voice_to_Hope_Music_of_Liberian_Refugees ; Samuel Morgan / Michael Frishkopf, “Shadow and music in the Buduburam Liberian refugee camp of Ghana” (video): https://vimeo.com/20009721 ; Society for Ethnomusicology social justice resources: https://www.ethnomusicology.org/page/Resources_Social/Music-and-Social-Justice-Resources-Project.htm .


## Palestinian and Somali refugee-camp music — Dheisheh, Dadaab and Lebanon (2026-11-15)

### Ettijah / Shoruq Rap Girls Group — Dheisheh refugee camp, Bethlehem, Palestine (2013–2020)
- **Status:** VERIFIED original refugee-girl rap recordings / PROMISING group-level discography; the album is publicly documented but still little-known outside specialist and community networks.
- **Group:** Ettijah (“direction”) began in 2013 through Shoruq, a Palestinian NGO based in Dheisheh refugee camp. The first generation consisted of girls who started rap workshops at approximately 10–12 years old; Shoruq's later description lists eight girls aged 12–15 in the group. They write and produce songs about daily life as refugee girls under occupation, women's rights and social restrictions. Preserve the group name; do not republish names or identifying details of minors without current consent.
- **Recordings:** Shoruq says the group released its first seven-song rap album, *The Journey*, in 2017. Track themes/titles: “My Homeland,” “Freedom,” “Dream,” “Women,” “Freedom of Expression,” “Displacement,” and “Child Soldiers.” Some music was produced by a musician from Morocco; Shoruq's media centre made a video for “My Homeland.” In 2020 the group released “Ana Qawiya” (“I am strong”), produced by Zaid Hilal. The group also performed “Bala Hudood” and “Watani” at a public show; these may be separate from the seven-track album.
- **Why it fits:** original songs written and performed by young women living in a refugee camp, addressing their daily lives, restrictions and rights. The album is a small, locally rooted project rather than a mainstream commercial release.
- **Cautions / next:** determine which recordings were made in Dheisheh and which were produced elsewhere; establish the original release carrier, full credits, producer roles and whether any demos or unreleased tracks survive. The original group members were minors at formation; use the NGO's public contact and community consent rather than attempting to identify individual singers. No audio audition or contact occurred.
- **Sources:** Shoruq official group and discography page: https://www.shoruq.org/SubPrg.aspx?Id=37 ; Shoruq English mirror: https://shoruq.engpal.com/en/Programes.aspx?id=2 ; Al Jazeera report on the group's formation and early workshop (2017): https://www.aljazeera.com/features/2017/1/20/palestinian-hip-hop-group-uses-music-as-a-weapon ; 2014 MECA project report on girls writing and recording songs: https://www.globalgiving.org/projects/music-and-songs-from-dheisheh-camp-to-the-world/reports/?subid=47563 .

### Palestine Street — Dheisheh refugee camp, Bethlehem, Palestine (2004/2007 onward)
- **Status:** PARTIALLY VERIFIED original camp rap recordings / PROMISING archive lead. Specific song titles are known, but no complete discography or original master inventory has been located.
- **Group / environment:** Palestine Street began as a five-member hip-hop group formed by young people in Dheisheh; sources differ on the start year (2004 in a community song index, 2007 in a 2013 interview). The group initially practised in an abandoned building in the camp and later worked from a small studio there. They wrote songs about daily life, occupation, displacement and the politics of NGO intervention.
- **Identified recordings:** Al Jazeera's 2017 report names “Qafes el-Deffe” (“West Bank Cage”), for which the group was preparing a video shoot in the camp, and “An Institutional Wedding,” a song by Mohammed Azmi critiquing NGO nonviolence projects. A 2013 Musicians Without Borders video documents Palestine Street rapping original texts written and signed by members of the Palestinian deaf community; the video was produced by Tamara Abu Laban from Dheisheh. A 2013 report says the group recorded four songs with children in the camp about camp life and the Separation Wall.
- **Obscurity / next:** the group has received regional/international press and performed in Europe, so the group itself is not unknown. The early demos, camp-studio masters and four children's songs may be less visible. Identify original releases, track titles, recording dates and studio location; do not assume all tracks were made in the camp. Protect child participants and collaborators from the deaf community; no identity expansion or reuse without consent.
- **Sources:** Al Jazeera, “Palestinian hip-hop group uses music as a weapon” (20 Jan. 2017): https://www.aljazeera.com/features/2017/1/20/palestinian-hip-hop-group-uses-music-as-a-weapon ; Musicians without Borders, original Deaf-community song/video (25 Apr. 2013): https://www.musicianswithoutborders.org/2013/04/25/celebrating-arab-deaf-awareness-week/ ; Antiwar Songs group page (community-submitted biographical note; discovery only): https://www.antiwarsongs.org/artista.php?id=19658&lang=en ; 2013 interview with Palestine Street: https://synchronicitymagazine.com/2013/03/2836/ .

### Dadaab Allstars — “Dear Mr. Peace” — Dadaab refugee camps, Kenya (2012)
- **Status:** VERIFIED resident-made camp music video/song / CONTEXT ONLY for obscurity; useful as a documented collaborative camp recording.
- **Recording:** FilmAid's Dadaab Stories project launched “Dear Mr. Peace” on Peace Day in September 2012. The song was created in seven days by the Dadaab Allstars, a temporary collaboration of solo artists from across the camps. A 2013 *Inside Story* report describes three young male singers, women dancing in both contemporary and traditional dress, and lyrics in French, Swahili and English. The project was built around refugee-produced media and involved camp residents as field producers and contributors.
- **Why context only:** the song was publicised through FilmAid and a major participatory-media project. It is not a hidden recording, but it is a concrete resident-made composition and a route into the wider Dadaab Stories multimedia archive.
- **Next:** recover the original video/audio page and credits; identify the participating artists only from their own public credits; establish whether studio masters or individual demo versions survive. Do not assume every Dadaab Stories music item was composed by camp residents.
- **Sources:** FilmAid FY2013 annual report, “A Music Video: Dear Mr. Peace”: https://libcloud.s3.amazonaws.com/129/39/5/1640/FA_FY2013_Annual_Report_SCREEN.pdf ; Clar Ni Chonghaile, “Tales of the unexpected,” *Inside Story* (2 May 2013): https://insidestory.org.au/tales-of-the-unexpected/ ; Dadaab Stories official platform: https://www.dadaabstories.org/ .

### Refugeenius — refugee artists from Dadaab, Kakuma and Nairobi, Kenya (2016)
- **Status:** VERIFIED collaborative album / CONTEXT ONLY. The album is public and promoted; the unreleased demos and artist-owned masters are more promising.
- **Recording / release:** Octopizzo's *Refugeenius* (Papichulo Music, 8 Dec. 2016) contains 18 tracks (67 minutes) featuring refugee artists from South Sudan, Somalia, DRC, Burundi, Uganda and Ethiopia, alongside Octopizzo. The project began under UNHCR's Artists for Refugees initiative in 2015 and was implemented with the Danish Refugee Council. UNHCR says more than 30 refugee musicians were trained in 2016; the compilation featured 18 songs and was launched in Nairobi in December. The Octopizzo Foundation reports that 48 commercially viable songs were produced in five-day workshops and that mastered tracks were given to artists for sale, licensing and performance; nine videos were produced. Therefore the album is only a subset of the project's music, and some tracks were made by urban refugees or in Nairobi rather than inside camps.
- **Why retain:** the 48-song production archive may contain 30 unreleased tracks, and the artists retain their own masters. This is a concrete route to under-documented refugee-authored songs, though the released compilation is not obscure.
- **Next:** map all 48 tracks and artists to camp/urban status, recording studio, date and current custodian; seek artists' own release pages and original demos. Do not attribute the whole project to Dadaab alone.
- **Sources:** UNHCR Kenya, Artists for Refugees Project (18 May 2016): https://www.unhcr.org/ke/news/artists-refugees-project ; UNHCR Kenya, “Music careers enhanced through Artists for Refugees Project”: https://www.unhcr.org/ke/news/music-careers-enhanced-through-artists-refugees-project ; Octopizzo Foundation, Artists for Refugees: https://octopizzofoundation.org/our-social-impact/artistes-for-refugees/ ; Spotify tracklist: https://open.spotify.com/album/2JYKop5h07NVJYEQQAPCpZ ; Apple Music release: https://music.apple.com/us/album/refugeenius/1325460813 .

### Refugee Cultural Heritage Archive — Palestinian and Syrian refugee camps in Lebanon (2019–2020)
- **Status:** CONTEXT / community-curated audiovisual archive route; individual music items not yet identified.
- AUB University Libraries announced in February 2020 a digital collection of 220 records (at launch) containing sound recordings, video and photographs documenting Palestinian and Syrian cultural heritage. The items were researched, selected and collected by residents of refugee camps in Lebanon. The archive specifically includes intangible heritage such as popular songs, dances and folktales; its stated aim is to expand across more camps.
- **Caution:** resident-curated collection does not mean every sound recording is resident-created music; some may be heritage recordings from the homeland or material recorded by visiting researchers. Identify the recording maker, performance location, participants and date for each item.
- **Next:** browse the sound-recording subset and search for camp-based song performances, local bands and cassette transfers; compare with POHA but keep the two AUB archives distinct. No audio audition or archive request occurred.
- **Sources:** AUB University Libraries announcement (18 Feb. 2020): https://www.aub.edu.lb/Libraries/News/Pages/RefugeeCulturalHeritage-DigitalArchive.aspx ; AUB Institute for Public Policy, POHA / archive context: https://www.aub.edu.lb/ifi/Pages/poha.aspx .

### Dadaab Stories — community media archive (Kenya, 2011 onward)
- **Status:** CONTEXT / discovery conduit, not a single recording.
- FilmAid's Dadaab Stories is a participatory multimedia platform containing videos, poetry, music and journalism created by camp residents and professional collaborators. It includes music and dance material and the “Dear Mr. Peace” video, and describes the camp's everyday music culture, including Somali pop at tea stalls and Gambella wedding dance music. It is a useful route for finding resident-made recordings and video performances, but content must be assessed item by item.
- **Sources:** official Dadaab Stories site: https://www.dadaabstories.org/ ; MIT Docubase overview: https://docubase.mit.edu/project/dadaab-stories/ ; Communication Arts project review: https://www.commarts.com/webpicks/dadaab-stories .


### Sierra Leone's Refugee All Stars — Sembakounya refugee camp, Guinea (1997 onward; later commercial recordings)
- **Status:** CONTEXT ONLY; well-publicised band and documentary. Important comparison for camp-formed music, but not a priority obscure release.
- **Formation:** the group formed in Kalia Refugee Camp, Guinea, and expanded after members were relocated to Sembakounya Refugee Camp. Members played in camp to process displacement and support fellow refugees. The band later came to UNHCR's attention, received instruments and sound equipment, and was featured in an internationally distributed documentary.
- **Recording distinction:** the band's later commercial album *Living Like a Refugee* (2006) and subsequent recordings were made after the group left the camps / in Freetown and should not be labelled as camp-recorded masters. The documentary contains footage of camp performances, but no separate, item-level original camp cassette has been identified in this pass.
- **Why retain:** it is a benchmark for how a refugee-camp group can become internationally visible, and a reminder to distinguish camp formation/performance from later studio recording.
- **Sources:** UNHCR, “Cinderellas of Reggae: The incredible story of the Refugee All Stars of Sierra Leone” (2007): https://www.unhcr.org/news/stories/cinderellas-reggae-incredible-story-refugee-all-stars-sierra-leone ; UNHCR 2011 refugee-camp arts review (group formation and later recording/tour): https://www.unhcr.org/sites/default/files/legacy-pdf/4def858a9.pdf ; official band biography: https://www.refugeeallstars.org/about/about-the-band/ .


## Sahrawi refugee-camp music and archives — Tindouf region, south-west Algeria (1970s–present)

### Portraits of Saharawi Music — British Library / Sahrawi Ministry of Culture field recordings (2013–2014)
- **Status:** VERIFIED 133-item recording archive / PROMISING item-level research corpus. The collection was made in refugee camps and deposited with the British Library; copies were also left in local institutions.
- **Collection:** Violeta Ruano Posada's six-month fieldwork in the Sahrawi refugee camps (September 2013–April 2014) produced 133 music items, according to the Music In Africa archive profile. The project was developed with the British Library Sound Archive and Sahrawi Ministry of Culture. It includes traditional drum-and-voice music, medeh, revolutionary/nidal songs and modern band music, sung in Hassaniya. The project sought to preserve a threatened oral repertoire and left local copies in the National Archive, National Library and National Music School in the camps.
- **Specific recordings with item-level context:**
  - **Salwan — “Bismilah”**: a medeh group led by Addala Doula, with Faknash Abeid on tbal; recorded in Faknash's house in Smara refugee camp. A field-video and description survive on Vimeo; this is a specific resident-performed camp recording, not a commercial album.
  - **ElHafed — “Your Objective”**: ElHafed is a non-professional composer with a repertoire of more than 100 self-written songs, employed at the local hospital and performing at national events. The song was first performed at a 2012 demonstration in Dakhla; the 2013 recording was made in Dakhla camp as part of the British Library project.
  - **Tarba Bueibu — “Saharawi Revolution”**: a revolutionary song recorded during a session at her home in Al Aaiun refugee camp; a version was issued on the British Library/Songlines *West Africa in the British Library* CD (December 2015). This version is more publicly circulated and is CONTEXT ONLY for obscurity.
  - **Tarba Bueibu — traditional and early nidal songs**: a video documents three short examples (one traditional *lashuar* love song and two early national/revolutionary songs dedicated to Basiri and El Uali). This is a field-recorded performance, but the video page does not give a full tracklist or exact session date.
- **Obscurity assessment:** the archive itself is a formal, collaborative preservation project, but its 133 items are not all commercially released. The two specific camp recordings “Bismilah” and “Your Objective” are PROMISING item-level entries; “Saharawi Revolution” is CONTEXT ONLY due to its Songlines compilation release. Do not treat the whole collection as obscure or as outsider music: it is a living national music culture shaped by exile and the independence struggle.
- **Ethics / next:** the archive was intentionally curated with the Sahrawi Ministry of Culture and the researcher as an activist project. Its repertoire-selection process and political aims should be documented; it is not a neutral sample of all music in the camps. Consult the British Library item catalogue for exact shelfmarks, dates, participants and rights; retain local copies and community access. No listening or archive contact occurred.
- **Sources:** Music In Africa archive profile (133 items): https://musicinafrica.net/directory-professional/violeta-ruano-portraits-saharawi-music-british-library/ ; British Library project overview: https://blogs-archive.bl.uk/music/2014/05/saharawi-music-in-the-refugee-camps-in-sw-algeria.html ; Ruano, “Portraits of Saharawi Music: When Cultural Preservation Meets Political Activism” (2016): https://journal.ru.ac.za/index.php/africanmusic/article/view/2040 ; Salwan / “Bismilah” recording: https://vimeo.com/93966624 ; ElHafed / “Your Objective” recording: https://vimeo.com/83449182 ; Tarba Bueibu / “Saharawi Revolution”: https://www.linkedin.com/pulse/portraits-saharawi-music-songlines-mag-violeta-ruano ; Tarba traditional/early national songs: https://vimeo.com/channels/652535 .

### Nubenegra / *Sahrauis: The Music of the Western Sahara* — camp recording sessions and unissued pre-mix (January 1998)
- **Status:** VERIFIED camp recording project / CONTEXT ONLY for the published box set; HIGH PRIORITY for the unreleased third pre-mix disc and raw field takes.
- **Recording:** Spanish label Nubenegra's Manuel Domínguez and a production team travelled to Sahrawi refugee camps in Algeria in October 1997 and January/February 1998. The January 1998 sessions recorded singers and musicians across Rabouni, Dakhla, Smara and Auserd/Aswerd. The published box set *Sahrauis: The Music of the Western Sahara* (1998) contains three discs: *A pesar de las heridas* (women's songs), *Sáhara, tierra mía* (more contemporary repertoire) and *Polisario Vencerá* (a reissue of the 1982 Shaheed El Uali album, recorded in Barcelona, not in the camps).
- **Unissued material:** producer Manuel Domínguez's 1998 first-person production diary says the team prepared **three pre-mix CDs of everything recorded**: one more traditional, one more contemporary, and a third containing “discarded” songs, modes/rhythms and themes considered of lesser importance. The first two were developed into the released albums; the third is not identified as a commercial release. Some selected tracks were later augmented with guitar, keyboards, accordion, bass or saxophone in Spanish studios. This creates a valuable distinction between the original field recordings, the pre-mix of rejected material, and the commercially mastered/overdubbed album tracks.
- **Obscurity / next:** the 1998 box set is well documented and circulated in world-music networks, so CONTEXT ONLY. The unreleased third pre-mix CD and original field recordings are HIGH PRIORITY archival targets. Seek the producer's archive, raw recording logs and the complete tracklist of all three pre-mix CDs; compare the raw camp sessions with the two released discs. No contact or listening occurred.
- **Sources:** Manuel Domínguez, first-person production diary in Nubenegra's 1998 newsletter, “De cómo la campaña”: https://nubenegra.com/pdf/LVI_1998_2.pdf ; Vivian Solana, “The Strategy of Style: Music, Struggle, and the Aesthetics of Sahrawi Nationalism in Exile” (2015): https://escholarship.org/uc/item/4hm2f4pf ; AECID catalogue with 3-CD tracklist and catalogue data: https://www.aecid.es/documents/d/guest/adq202506bi?download=false ; World Music Central project overview: https://worldmusiccentral.org/sahrauis-music-of-the-western-sahara/ .

### Radio Nacional Saharaui / Ministry of Information archive — Rabouni, Algeria (1976–present)
- **Status:** VERIFIED large broadcast/archive collection / HIGH PRIORITY archive route; item-level music provenance not yet established.
- **Holdings:** a 2017 thesis on Radio Nacional Saharaui reports approximately 2,500 audio reels covering four decades of radio broadcasts, now preserved in digital format. Contents include speeches, conferences, political and cultural programmes, Sahrawi music, anniversary reports and war coverage. The archive is located in the Ministry of Information complex at Rabouni, near Tindouf; it has been described as open to the public since 2006 but historically under-resourced and still requiring cataloguing/digitisation.
- **2024 preservation survey:** UCLA's Modern Endangered Archives Program survey (MEAP-6-0133) describes the Ministry of Information archive as dating from 1975 to the present and containing the archives of “Free Sahara” newspaper and “Voice of the Free Sahara” radio. It includes interviews, performances such as dances and festivals, and materials on Sahrawi history, culture and education. Digitisation/open access is contingent on Ministry review; some items have political sensitivities.
- **Next:** obtain a reel-level inventory for music and cultural broadcasts; identify camp-based bands, women's medeh groups, local and children's bands, field recordings and original radio performances. Distinguish studio recordings, live broadcasts, news clips, interviews and music played on air. No archive visit or contact occurred.
- **Sources:** Malainine Salama Naiem, *Cuarenta años en las ondas: Radio Nacional Saharaui* (2017 thesis): https://addi.ehu.es/bitstream/handle/10810/21745/TESIS_NAIEM_MALAININE%20SALAMA.pdf?isAllowed=y&sequence=1 ; UCLA MEAP Ministry of Information survey (19 Oct. 2024): https://static.library.ucla.edu/craftassetsprod/MEAP/MEAP-documents/MEAP-Survey-Ministry-of-Information.pdf .

### Badawi Sahrawi Music Archives — diaspora-run cassette preservation and label (ongoing)
- **Status:** VERIFIED community archive / discovery conduit, not one recording.
- DJ Bedouin Sahrawi, a refugee raised in Spain and now based in the Canary Islands, has collected Sahrawi music for decades and is digitising material for release through the Badawi label. The archive describes older political folk music dating to the 1970s and contemporary releases, with some recordings available online. It is the first Sahrawi-run label/archive and an important route to local cassette collections that may not be in formal catalogues.
- **Caution:** individual releases must be checked for recording date and place. For example, El Wali's *Tiris* (originally a small Oxfam Belgium CD, recorded in Belgium in 1994 while the group was on tour; later reissued in 2019) reflects the popular style of the refugee camps but is not a camp recording. Do not label it as such.
- **Sources:** Sandblast Arts, Badawi Sahrawi Music Archives profile: https://sandblast-arts.org/badawi-saharawi-music-archive ; El Wali, *Tiris* (2019 reissue and provenance): https://elwali.bandcamp.com/album/tiris ; Sahel Sounds track note: https://sahelsoundscompilations.bandcamp.com/track/long-live-the-sahrawi-army .

### Sahrawi camp music institutions and bands — context for future item-level search (1970s onward)
- The Sahrawi exile administration built an organised music system: regional and national bands, adult and children's groups in wilayas/dairas, music competitions, cultural festivals, and musical groups at military bases. The national band Shaheed El Uali (later El Uali) was formed in 1976 and represented the liberation movement; its 1982 album *Polisario Vencerá* was recorded in Barcelona during an international tour, not in the refugee camps. The first cassette/LP recordings of revolutionary songs were also circulated through underground or smuggled cassette networks.
- This is a political national culture, not outsider music. The compendium's value lies in specific rare camp performances, unissued recordings, women's and children's groups, regional ensembles and local radio material, not in presenting the whole Sahrawi national music tradition as obscure.
- **Sources:** Violeta Ruano, “Shaheed El Uali Band and Beyond: 40 Years of Resistance Music in Western Sahara” (2016): https://ethnomusicologyreview.ucla.edu/content/shaheed-el-uali-band-and-beyond-40-years-resistance-music-western-sahara ; Vivian Solana, “The Strategy of Style” (2015): https://escholarship.org/uc/item/4hm2f4pf ; Luis Giménez Amorós, “Azawan: Precolonial Musical Culture and Sahrawi Nationalism in the Refugee Camps of the Hamada Desert in Algeria”: https://journal.ru.ac.za/index.php/africanmusic/article/download/1225/652/654 .


## Northern Greek refugee-camp sound recordings — Oreokastro, Softex and Thessaloniki (2016)

### “Refugees Got Talent” — Oreokastro camp, Thessaloniki, Greece (11 August 2016)
- **Status:** VERIFIED resident-run camp performance archive / CONTEXT ONLY at project level; individual original songs remain PROMISING item-level leads.
- **Project:** Refugee TV, an internet-based channel run by four Syrian refugees, organised a DIY talent contest inside Oreokastro refugee camp. The organisers explain that authorities restricted filming and photography in camps, so the event was conceived as a way to record and share residents' talents. The first event was video-recorded; a final show was live-streamed, and voting took place on Facebook. Performers presented a range of genres and instruments.
- **Specific performance lead:** Ioannis Christidis's MMRC podcast identifies a rap performance by two young women, Sedra and Sara, at the 11 August 2016 contest; the podcast includes an audio extract from a video uploaded to Refugees TV's Facebook page. A 2017 Al Jazeera documentary, *Refugees Got Talent*, documents the organisers and performances in Oreokastro.
- **Cautions:** performers may have been minors; do not republish names, faces, lyrics or personal histories without current consent. The event was resident-organised but not all songs were necessarily original compositions. Separate original songs from covers, dabke/pop playback, dance and sketches.
- **Next:** recover the original Refugees TV video and full audio, identify the rap song's title/credits only through the artists' public consent, and determine whether other performances were original refugee compositions. No audio audition or contact occurred.
- **Sources:** MMRC podcast with timestamped audio-sample list: https://www.musicandminorities.org/research/projects/music-in-the-experience-of-forced-migration/podcast/ ; Al Jazeera documentary (26 Mar. 2017): https://www.aljazeera.com/video/witness/2017/3/26/refugees-got-talent ; Refugee TV interview and camp recording conditions: https://aoratespoleis.files.wordpress.com/2019/05/contested-borderscapes-4.pdf .

### Unnamed Syrian refugee songs / protest recordings — Thessaloniki, Greece (July–October 2016)
- **Status:** PARTIALLY VERIFIED field-recording corpus / PROMISING archive route; original performers are unnamed in the public research source.
- **Recordings:** Christidis's MMRC podcast documents:
  - “Janna, Janna,” chanted as a slogan by young refugees during a protest, Thessaloniki, 21 October 2016 (field recording).
  - “Safarna A'la Euroba” (“We travelled to Europe”), written by Syrian pop singer Nidal Karam (then living in the Netherlands), widely circulated online in 2015–16; one young Syrian refugee sang it during a protest outside a detention centre on 20 July 2016. The protest performance is a field recording, but the composition is not a camp-origin song.
  - An unnamed young refugee's own song about the Syrian revolution, sung to fellow protesters on 21 July 2016; the podcast includes two extracts from videos uploaded to the Help Refugees Facebook page. This is a particularly promising original-composition lead, but no title, artist or recording location within the camp is provided.
  - The adhan broadcast over a speaker at Softex camp, 9 August 2016. This is religious call to prayer, not a music recording for the main discography.
- **Archive:** the researcher says the field recordings form part of a personal audiovisual archive, alongside online videos uploaded by refugee/activist pages. The source does not establish public access to the original files beyond the podcast samples.
- **Why retain:** these recordings document resident-authored music and chants during forced encampment and border protests, and show how Facebook/live-streaming became part of refugee self-documentation. Most are context-only because they are field recordings of public performances or pre-existing songs; the unnamed original song is the stronger lead.
- **Next:** identify the original Help Refugees and Refugee TV video posts, retrieve only through public authorised routes, and preserve anonymity unless the performers themselves have chosen public credit. Distinguish original compositions from songs downloaded/listened to by residents. No audio audition or contact occurred.
- **Sources:** MMRC podcast and audio-sample descriptions (samples 1, 6–12): https://www.musicandminorities.org/research/projects/music-in-the-experience-of-forced-migration/podcast/ ; MMRC initial research findings: https://www.musicandminorities.org/research/projects/music-in-the-experience-of-forced-migration/initial-research-findings/ ; Christidis et al., *Internment Refugee Camps: Historical and Contemporary Perspectives* (2023): https://www.ssoar.info/ssoar/bitstream/handle/document/83678/ssoar-2023-Anderl_et_al-Internment_Refugee_Camps.pdf?isAllowed=y&lnkname=ssoar-2023-Anderl_et_al-Internment_Refugee_Camps.pdf .


## Kakuma and Tibetan exile music — additional refugee-settlement research (2026-11-15)

### Kakuma Sound — *Africa yetu* — Kakuma Refugee Camp, Turkana, Kenya (released 1 October 2025)
- **Status:** VERIFIED resident-led camp recording / PROMISING. A small, self-released five-track digital album, with track-level performers and recording dates still uncredited.
- **Release:** *Africa yetu* by Kakuma Sound, released on Bandcamp 1 October 2025. Five short tracks: “Africa yetu” (1:30), “Africa tupendane” (1:10), “Mali ya Ngoma nini ?” (0:46), “Mkongolele Mama ya Uma” (0:48) and “Yasa” (2:15). The artist describes the album as raw field recordings made in Kakuma refugee camp, blending traditional rhythms, chants, voices and everyday sounds. It is available as a $4 digital album in 24-bit/48 kHz; all rights reserved.
- **Recording ecosystem:** Kakuma Sound was founded in 2018 by Kenyan producer Treynor Tumwa and historian Mark LeVine. Its current organisation has a resident-led Community-Based Organization at its core; the project says all instruments and recordings remain the property of Kakuma residents through the CBO. It reports 700+ musicians across 40 groups (2023). The first camp recording studio opened in 2022 alongside the first Kakuma Sound Cultural Festival; World Refugee Day performances have taken place since 2015, and the project runs Kakuma Got Talent. Branches have opened in Kyaka and Kyagwali camps (Uganda) and Mahama camp (Rwanda).
- **Why it fits:** a recent, small-scale, resident-owned release documenting an improvised multi-national music culture in a long-term refugee settlement. It is more than a charity compilation: the project explicitly locates ownership with camp residents.
- **Cautions / next:** identify the performers, nationalities/languages, exact recording dates, field-recording vs composed-song distinction, and whether the five tracks are excerpts from longer sessions. Preserve the CBO's ownership and the Bandcamp all-rights-reserved terms. Do not assume the project’s NGO status means recordings are open for reuse. No audio audition or contact occurred.
