- Other useful holdings context: TLM International records include Purulia institutional files (1908–1987), Chandraghona (1921–1987) and Bhutan (1964–72 onward), while the Angus Library at Regent's Park College holds BMS Chandraghona records and a typed narrative of the colony's origins. These are paper/context collections, not identified sound holdings: https://leprosyhistory.org/archive/archive718/ ; https://leprosyhistory.org/archive/archive698/ .
- **Next audit request:** ask Effect:Hope for the audiovisual finding aid and item-level inventory for all six audio-tape locations; seek tape identifiers, date, title/subject, language, recordist, whether music is present, format/condition, restrictions, and any preservation transfer. Separately request identification of the 1978 “More Than a Prophet” cassette. Do not request blanket digitisation before identifying content and community/consent context.

## Japan — Aoi-tori Gakudan: touring history added (2026-11-15)

- A Japanese institutional history says the Nagashima Aiseien Blind Association's Aoi-tori harmonica band made annual performances outside the sanatorium from 1967 through 1976, totalling 15 outings, including six performances in Osaka and events in Tokyo, Nagoya and Kyoto. This adds a likely source route: local newspaper coverage, event programmes, amateur film, radio/TV reports and host-venue records in those cities. It remains evidence of performances, not proof that audio survives. https://ritsumei.repo.nii.ac.jp/record/10057/files/k_1115.pdf .
- Keep the National Hansen's Disease Museum's May 1974 performance photograph and 1970 Zhao Gen-zai photographs as visual evidence. No period sound recording identified yet.


## Japan — Aoi-tori Gakudan: confirmed historic concert recordings and current preservation project (2026-11-15)

- **Major upgrade from “archive hunt” to “surviving audio evidence.”** Leprosy.jp's 2017 Japanese oral-history feature reports that former Aoi-tori vocalist Matsuo Sasaki (佐々木松雄) played the interviewer recordings from concerts. One is **「鶴島哀歌」 (Tsurushima Aika)**, composed by Koichi Kondo (近藤宏一), with a spoken/sung treatment developed at Sasaki's request and a solo vocal part assigned to Sasaki. The article also says the interviewer heard a concert recording with Sasaki's lead voice and a women's chorus from the nursing school. It does not publish the audio or give a tape date/format/catalogue number. Sasaki died in 2019, so he is a historical witness/source custodian, not a current contact. Source: https://leprosy.jp/people/plus08/ ; obituary/context: https://engekikyoikuron.hatenablog.com/entry/2025/10/16/193908 .
- The 1968-06-24 external concert at Osaka Kosei Kaikan Cultural Hall, “らいを聴く夕べ” (“An Evening to Listen to Leprosy”), is documented by the surviving 20-page programme as including **「鶴島哀歌」 — solo vocal, Matsuo Sasaki**. Sasaki was a graduate of Shinryoda School and joined the ensemble for the event from Tohoku Shinseien. This establishes a dated public performance and programme credit; it does not yet prove the surviving tape heard by the 2017 interviewer is that specific 1968 performance. Source and transcription of the programme: https://engekikyoikuron.hatenablog.com/entry/2025/10/16/193908 ; corroborating event chronology: https://researchmap.jp/miya_shita/published_papers/17113817/attachment_file.pdf .
- A 2025 lecture by composer Umitaro Abe reports that nearly all Aoi-tori concerts were recorded. He says event organisers showed him source audio and materials; his research involved Kondo's belongings, concert programmes and repertoire. He intended to curate a selection of historic recordings for public listening and release an audio collection under the project “近藤宏一の過去と現在” (“Koichi Kondo: Past and Present”). This is the most promising present-day preservation/access route. The lecture report does not confirm that the collection was subsequently released. https://kouhou.niigatakenjinkai.com/?p=17685 .
- A 2026 Nagi Museum of Contemporary Art event page says Abe and Takeo Toyama have worked since 2017 to research Kondo's scores and recordings, make recordings toward audio publication, and preserve the music. This distinguishes their new recordings/interpretations from historical source tapes. The page documents a 21 Feb 2026 concert and identifies the continuing project, but does not link a public historical recording collection: https://www.town.nagi.okayama.jp/moca/news/20260207.html .
- A 2017 Leprosy.jp article says Sasaki died in 2019, and 2024 correspondence from Akira Yabe records that Sasaki's funeral included the 1968 “らいを聴く夕べ” programme and the lyrics of “鶴島哀歌.” Yabe had chaired the FIWC Kansai committee that organised the 1968 event. This makes Yabe's historical documentation and the FIWC / Musubi no Ie archive potential paper/provenance routes, but not automatically custodians of the audio. https://engekikyoikuron.hatenablog.com/entry/ba481c9d62012b9df985a9e532af4748 .
- A 2025 lecture transcript says the original tapes were shown to Abe by an event organiser; the identity of that organiser and current physical/digital custodian is not named. A 2021 exhibition retrospective also says the 2019 “青い鳥のハモニカ” project had access to archival materials, but the pandemic prevented public playback of original recordings at that exhibition. https://www.arsvi.com/2020/202109tm.htm .
- **Provenance and classification:** treat the original Aoi-tori performance tapes as a distinct archive from Abe/Toyama's 2017–2026 reinterpretation/recording project and from the 2019 memorial concert by professional musicians. “Tsurushima Aika” is a Kondo original; the 1968 programme lists Sasaki as soloist. Do not label a specific extant tape as the 1968 performance until the tape is matched to the programme.
- **Current status:** confirmed reported survival of multiple concert recordings; near-complete recording practice claimed in 2025; no public historic audio file or item-level catalogue yet located. No Grok request: first establish a public authorised release or a clearly documented access route and rights.
- **Next exact route:** follow Abe Umitaro's “青い鳥のハモニカ” / “近藤宏一の過去と現在” project and its 2025–26 audio-collection status; identify the event organiser who supplied source audio; query Nagashima Aiseien History Museum, National Hansen's Disease Museum, FIWC Kansai / Musubi no Ie, and the 1968 event's surviving programme archive for reel/cassette inventories, transfer history and rights/consent. Protect surviving participants' and families' wishes; avoid contacting bereaved families or publishing private audio without consent.


## Japan — Aoi-tori Gakudan: two dated historic tapes documented in 1963 and 1967 (2026-11-15)

- **New primary-source trail via Mami Tanaka's 2016 Ritsumeikan doctoral thesis**, *A Study of Mieko Kamiya's “Practice”: Focusing on Her Work as a Psychiatrist in the National Sanatorium Nagashima-Aiseien in the 1960s*, pp. 164–165. Full thesis: https://ritsumei.repo.nii.ac.jp/record/10057/files/k_1115.pdf .
- **Tape A — 1963 soundscape / Aoi-tori recording:** the thesis quotes Mieko Kamiya's account of taking an Aoi-tori tape to Carville Sanatorium in the United States during her 25–30 July 1963 visit. It was played for John Wick and senior staff. Kamiya recognised the sounds of waves around Nagashima and the sanatorium's “Megumi no Kane” bell. She says patients had carried a recorder around for days in the sun to make the tape, and separately praises the Aoi-tori members' strenuous rehearsals. The passage indicates a composite sound document containing Nagashima ambience and Aoi-tori music/performance context, not necessarily a clean concert recording. It is not known whether the tape was retained at Carville, returned to Japan, or survives in a current archive. Do not label this as a concert master without listening or item documentation.
- **Tape B — 12 May 1967 Ibaraki Hospital concert:** after Aoi-tori's first concert outside Nagashima, at Ibaraki Hospital, Osaka Prefecture, Kondo sent Kamiya a tape recording of the concert. Her letter dated 21 May 1967 (quoted in Tanaka's thesis as previously unpublished correspondence) says she listened to the tape from beginning to end, planned to return it, and could not make a copy on her home recorder; she offered to make a copy at the sanatorium's Lighthouse when she next visited. This establishes that the event was recorded and that a copy at the Lighthouse was contemplated, but does not prove that a second copy was made or that either tape survives. The letter is addressed to Kondo and confirms his role as sender; the original tape may have been returned to him.
- **Separate Sasaki-held recordings (reported in 2017):** a Leprosy.jp oral-history feature says Matsuo Sasaki played the interviewer a recording of “鶴島哀歌” and a separate concert recording with nursing-school women's chorus. The 1968-06-24 Osaka Kosei Kaikan programme lists “鶴島哀歌” as a Sasaki solo, but the recording he played has not been matched to that programme. Keep these distinct from the 1963 Carville tape and 1967 Ibaraki tape.
- **Institutional lead:** Tanaka's thesis also documents the Lighthouse tape-library context at Nagashima. In 1976 correspondence, Kamiya refers to the newly completed Lighthouse and its “tape library” (テープ・ライブラリー). This suggests a resident-managed tape collection may be a plausible copy/custody route, but it does not establish that either Aoi-tori tape was deposited there. Source: same Ritsumeikan thesis, pp. 168–170.
- **Kamiya archive lead:** a 2024 published catalogue covers the Nagashima Aiseien “Kamiya Library” (神谷書庫), including approximately 8,500 periodical holdings and 4,000 books/documents, plus Kamiya's donated personal books. It is not described as an audio archive, but may help trace original letters and related paper provenance. Catalogue details: https://libro-koseisha.co.jp/leprosy/9784774408392/ .
- **2025–26 preservation project:** Umitaro Abe's 2025 lecture says event organisers showed him historic source audio and materials; he had found that almost all concerts were recorded. The 2026 Nagi Museum page confirms his continuing research into Kondo's scores and recordings and work toward audio publication. This may be the strongest present-day route to the source tapes, but no public historical audio collection has been verified. https://kouhou.niigatakenjinkai.com/?p=17685 ; https://www.town.nagi.okayama.jp/moca/news/20260207.html .
- **Priority and next actions:** Aoi-tori remains the top recording lead. Trace Tanaka's footnote/source trail for Kamiya's 21 May 1967 letter and the 1963 Carville visit; identify the US Carville archive/recording holdings and whether the tape remained there; establish whether the Lighthouse tape library survives in the Nagashima Aiseien History Museum or residents' records; ask Abe/project organisers about item-level inventory, dates, format, digitisation and access/rights. Respect community and family consent; no direct private contact or public reuse without permission.
- **Classification:** These are documented surviving-at-the-time tapes, not yet confirmed extant accessible masters. No Grok request until authorised public release or documented access and rights.


## Aoi-tori / Carville — named US archival cross-checks for the 1963 tape (2026-11-15)

- The 2016 Tanaka thesis's “John Wick” at Carville appears likely to be **Dr. Edgar B. Johnwick**, who is identified by the U.S. National Library of Medicine as Carville's chief and whose daybooks cover 1956–1965. This is a plausible person-level match, not yet an independently confirmed identity for the 1963 meeting. NLM collection MS C 374 comprises three boxes of daybooks, is listed as unrestricted, and is available onsite in the History of Medicine Division; the finding aid is marked “Unverified Partial Draft.” The daybooks are a direct contemporaneous source to check for Kamiya's 25–30 July 1963 visit and the playback of the Nagashima tape. https://findingaids.nlm.nih.gov/repositories/ammp/resources/johnwick374 .
- The Daughters of Charity Archives' 2023 Carville finding aid includes an audiovisual subseries RG 11-2-8-4, Box 54, Folder 2, titled **“16 mm reel to reel tape of patients, etc.”** The format/title are unusual and require clarification with archivists; the finding aid does not connect this item to the Japanese Aoi-tori tape. It is a possible secondary inventory lead only, not evidence that the 1963 tape is in Carville's holdings. The same subseries lists unrelated Carville organ music and patient cantatas, which must not be conflated with Aoi-tori. https://docarchivesblog.org/wp-content/uploads/2023/02/11-2-carville-la-national-hansens-disease-center-finding-aid.pdf .
- The Carville finding aid states some restrictions apply, audiovisual digital alternatives should be used where they exist, and publication of archival text/image/video/audio requires permission from the Daughters of Charity Communications Director. Contact archive staff for access forms and item-level clarification. The NLM daybooks are unrestricted for research access, but NLM does not own copyright and access does not confer publication rights.
- Official National Hansen's Disease Museum description says its Carville collections include Daughters of Charity and USPHS material, Carville culture/activities and patient stories; this is a useful institutional orientation but does not establish custody of the 1963 Nagashima tape: https://www.hrsa.gov/hansens-disease/museum .
- **Next exact checks:** search/request Dr. Edgar B. Johnwick's July 1963 daybook entries and any references to Dr. Mieko Kamiya / Nagashima / Japanese tape / Aiseien; ask Daughters of Charity Archives whether Box 54 Folder 2's reel-to-reel item is audio, film sound, or a different object and whether any 1963 Japanese visitor/tape is documented; ask Carville Museum staff whether any Japanese audio item was transferred to the current museum. Do not assume any of these records is the Aoi-tori tape.


### Clarification to Aoi-tori tape labels (2026-11-15)

Earlier working labels “Tape A/B/C/D” in the appended audit notes were navigation shorthand, not four verified catalogue objects. The defensible object distinctions are: (1) the 1963 Carville soundscape/music tape; (2) the 12 May 1967 Ibaraki Hospital concert tape sent to Kamiya; and (3) the recording(s) Matsuo Sasaki played the Leprosy.jp interviewer in the 2017 oral-history interview, including “鶴島哀歌” and a separate concert with nursing-school women's chorus. The Sasaki-held “鶴島哀歌” recording is not proven to be the 24 June 1968 performance listed in the programme. Do not treat the 1968 programme itself as a fourth tape or assume any of these three bodies of evidence refer to the same physical recording.


## Japan / Carville follow-up — public-release audit and archive scope clarified (2026-11-15)

- Checked Umitaro Abe's own current NEWS archive (https://www.umitaroabe.com/news/; page crawled 2026-11-15). It contains 2026 announcements through October but no indexed mention of “青い鳥”, “青い鳥のハモニカ” or “近藤宏一”, and no identifiable historic audio collection or release. This is a bounded negative result from the visible news page, not proof no release exists elsewhere. The 2026 Nagi Museum page still describes research into Kondo's scores and recordings and work toward recording/publication; no public historical-audio link appears there either. Sources: https://www.umitaroabe.com/news/ ; https://www.town.nagi.okayama.jp/moca/news/20260207.html .
- NHDM's 2023 “青い鳥のハモニカ” event page links a YouTube record of the concert. Its description identifies Abe, Takeo Toyama and Itoko Toma performing/reading Kondo's poetry and music; this is a modern tribute/interpretation, not a historical Aoi-tori recording. NHDM event contact listed: event@nhdm.jp. https://www.nhdm.jp/en/events/list/4899/ .
- Carville repository scope cross-check: NLM Directory of History of Medicine describes the National Hansen's Disease Programs Museum holdings as Daughters of Charity correspondence, publications, photographs, clippings and diaries, and USPHS materials including Medical Officer in Charge daybooks 1921–1960; it does not list the 1963 Nagashima tape or a general audio collection. It permits advance-registered research and some staff copying by approval. Museum research contact: NHDPmuseum@hrsa.gov; 225-642-1950. https://hmddirectory.nlm.nih.gov/hmddir/print_collection/223 .
- NLM's Edgar B. Johnwick Daybooks (MS C 374, 1956–1965) are described as daily records of happenings at the Carville USPHS Hospital. This is a documentary cross-check for Kamiya's visit, not an audio holding. NLM also lists unprocessed oral-history collections from 1995–97 and documentary raw footage from 2004–05, neither likely to be the 1963 tape. https://findingaids.nlm.nih.gov/agents/corporate_entities/39 ; https://findingaids.nlm.nih.gov/repositories/resources?field%5B%5D=title&filter_fields%5B%5D=subjects&filter_values%5B%5D=Leprosy&from_year%5B%5D=&limit=resource&op%5B%5D=&q%5B%5D=%2A&sort=title_sort+asc&to_year%5B%5D= .
- Daughters of Charity Provincial Archives' public ILA entry says RG 11-2 documents everyday Carville life through correspondence, publications, photographs and clippings, with extensive Sisters' correspondence and diaries; it does not itself describe the Aoi-tori tape. Archives email archives@doc.org. A separate 2023 public exhibit streams two Sister interviews from cassette, showing the archive does preserve some digitised sound, but these are not the Japanese tape. https://leprosyhistory.org/archive/archive853/ ; https://docapsl.omeka.net/exhibits/show/the-daughters--history-in-heal/the-national-hansen-s-disease- .
- **Revised finding:** the 1963 Carville tape's historical playback is documented, but no public catalogue or extant audio match has been located in Carville's published finding aids. The most useful question to the Carville Museum / Daughters Archives is not simply “do you have Aoi-tori?” but whether the July 1963 Kamiya visit appears in Johnwick's daybook or the Sisters' annals/visitor files, whether any Japanese tape or accompanying letter was retained, and whether an accession/transfer record exists.


## Nigeria — Uzuakoli: original vinyl source confirmed in a broadcast, with source layers distinguished (2026-11-15)

- John Manton's 2015 Cambridge / Anthropologies of African Biosciences programme page explicitly says *Uzuakoli in Music and Medicine* assembles **found and field recordings, including original vinyl as remastered recordings of Harcourt Whyte's choir**, alongside contemporary recordings arranged by Whyte's scholarly biographer Achinivu Kanu Achinivu, oral-history testimony, and field recordings of sung/spoken passages of Whyte's music. This independently confirms that original Uzuakoli choir vinyl existed and was available to the production by 2015; it does not identify pressing, label, catalogue number, date, owner or whether the entire record was transferred. Streaming programme link is on the page via Mixcloud: https://africanbiosciences.wordpress.com/publications/radio-uzuakoli-in-music-and-medicine/ .
- The programme page identifies John Manton as author, Anthropologies of African Biosciences, University of Cambridge, co-produced with The Arts & Culture Unit, engineered by Vivien Jones; originally broadcast June 2015 on Resonance 104.4fm. This provides a much more exact route than generic discography searching: production records, original source tape log, permission correspondence, and any retained vinyl transfer may identify the record owner and metadata.
- Music In Africa separately says Whyte himself did not record an album; the Choir of the Leprosy Colony, Uzuakoli recorded his compositions after his death in 1977, while a separate St. Louis Missouri African Choir recording was made in 1999. Keep these as separate recording entities; the 2015 programme's "original vinyl" is the posthumous Uzuakoli choir source, not Whyte's own performance.
- The Sasakawa Health Foundation documents a 2021–23 local preservation project by the Achinivu-Harcourt Whyte Choral Association and German Leprosy and TB Relief Association, resulting in two 50-song volumes of translated/transcribed scores. This is a current community-linked source network and notation project, not the historical vinyl or an audio reissue. https://www.shf.or.jp/en/information/21652 .
- ILA's *Leprosy and Cultural Production* identifies “Atulegwu” and “Umu gi emebiwo uwa gi” as notable Uzuakoli Choir of the Leper Colony performances, and gives the latter's title meaning; treat as song-level search terms, not verified disc-side assignments. https://leprosyhistory.org/reading-material/leprosy-and-cultural-production/ .
- **Next exact actions:** inspect the 2015 Mixcloud programme's spoken credits and sound-source sequence; search Manton's production notes and The Arts & Culture Unit archive for a vinyl source log, permission correspondence and remaster files; ask Achinivu-Harcourt Whyte Choral Association / Prof. Achinivu whether the association, a Methodist church, family, or another collector holds the original disc; request label, matrix/catalogue, tracklist, recording date, performers, language, condition and rights. No public direct vinyl/download URL or rights-cleared reissue verified; no Grok request.


## Japan — Aoi-tori: correction to the 1967 Lighthouse duplication hypothesis (2026-11-15)

- Re-read the full relevant passage in Tanaka's thesis, pp. 164–166. Kamiya's 21 May 1967 letter says she had listened to Kondo's 12 May Ibaraki Hospital concert tape from beginning to end. Kondo had apparently asked her to make a recording/copy when returning it. She could not record on her home machine; she offered to make a copy at the Lighthouse if needed. Crucially, the letter's closing says she planned to bring the original tape back to the Lighthouse the following Tuesday and leave it with someone there.
- **Correction:** the source establishes a proposed return of the original to the Lighthouse and a conditional offer to copy it there; it does not establish that a copy was actually made or that the original was deposited in a permanent tape library. Do not describe a Lighthouse duplicate as more than a possibility. The thesis elsewhere mentions the Lighthouse as the Blind Association meeting place and a 1975 letter about a new Lighthouse building; it does not provide an audio inventory.
- This strengthens the Lighthouse as a physical hand-off / witness trail for the 1967 original, not as a confirmed archive accession. Search surviving Blind Association records, Lighthouse management/committee papers, resident tape collections and Nagashima Aiseien History Museum provenance records.
- Exact source: Tanaka, *A Study of Mieko Kamiya's “Practice”*, pp. 164–166, especially pp. 165–166: https://ritsumei.repo.nii.ac.jp/record/10057/files/k_1115.pdf .


- **Public broadcast route located:** Resonance FM's Mixcloud page hosts the complete 1:00:01 *Modulations – Uzuakoli in Music and Medicine* broadcast, dated 12 June 2015. It is a public streaming documentary assembled from multiple sources, not an isolated vinyl-side release or downloadable original master. Use it as a listening and source-identification route; do not extract/re-upload. https://www.mixcloud.com/Resonance/modulations-12th-june-2015/ .


## Nigeria / Uzuakoli — original choir LP catalogue leads and chronology conflict (2026-11-15)

- A specialist record-market index (Popsike archive) lists two Nigerian LPs credited **Harcourt Whyte & Choir**: *Harcourt Whyte And Choir*, **ARC 1059**, listed 1975; and *Harcourt Whyte & Choir Vol 2*, **ARC 1106**, listed 1974. This is a discographic lead only; no label scan, matrix, sleeve or primary catalogue has yet corroborated either date or the connection to the posthumous Uzuakoli choir. https://www.popsike.com/php/quicksearch.php?bidsfrom=&bidsthru=&endfrom=&endthru=&eprice=&fcatno=&flabel=&incldescr=&layout=lv&pagenum=11&searchtext=+arc .
- An unlicensed file-sharing page reproduces a purported 12-track list for *Harcourt Whyte And Choir* and identifies production at Arc Studio, Lagos, “by courtesy of the Writers Workshop of East Central State of Nigeria,” with **Nnamdi Olebara** as leader/vocals. Treat these details as provisional metadata only, not an authorised listening/download source: A-side “Otuto Nke Chukwu”; “Epitaph Nwokorobia”; “Tikuenu Jehova”; “Ekpere Ogbenye”; “Atulegwu”; “Nwanyi Aga”; “Onyinye Chukwu”; B-side “Chebe Mnna”; “Nga Abu Udele”; “Obu Chukwu”; “Agadi Nwanyi”; “Ayi Emebi Wo Uwagi.” Its own versions note labels the ARC 1059 Tabansi issue a 1977 reissue, while the page's headline metadata says 1975. Do not reproduce or acquire the rip. https://flac.vodila.net/51448/harcourt-whyte-and-choir-harcourt-whyte-and-choir-download-flac.html .
- **Important chronology conflict:** Music In Africa says the Choir of the Leprosy Colony, Uzuakoli recorded Whyte's compositions after his death in 1977; the Popsike index dates ARC 1059 to 1975 and ARC 1106 to 1974, while the secondary file-sharing page describes ARC 1059 as a 1977 Tabansi reissue. It is possible that the 1974/75 entries refer to a different/earlier ensemble or that seller/index dates are wrong. No catalogue-number-to-2015-source-master match is established. Keep the two LPs as separate provisional discographic leads until sleeve labels, matrices and personnel confirm their identity.
- A later community-network route is now documented: the **Achinivu-Harcourt Whyte Choral Association, Arochukwu Incorporated** partnered with the German Leprosy and TB Relief Association and Sasakawa Health Foundation to publish two volumes of *Messages for Life and Living* (100 songs, 2022–23). The 2023 report announced a commemorative choir concert for 26 November 2023 via Aromouthpiece TV's YouTube live channel. This is a modern community performance, not the historic Uzuakoli choir recording; its recording may preserve current association activity and provide a legitimate local contact route. ILEP's Nigeria page lists the Association as a partner but does not provide its direct contact. https://www.shf.or.jp/en/information/21652 ; https://sasakawaleprosyinitiative.org/latest-updates/initiative-news/4801/ ; https://ilepfederation.org/nigeria/ .
- A 2013 memorial concert report independently identifies **Nnamdi Olebara** as an iconic broadcaster and performer at an “Evening with Harcourt Whyte”; the programme included five choirs and the Achinivu-led association. This supports Olebara as a significant research node, but does not independently verify his credit on ARC 1059. https://www.newsexpressngr.com/news/1954/surprise-surprise-charly-boy-performs-with-mass-choir-at-harcourt-whytes-memorial .
- **Next checks:** obtain label/sleeve photographs and matrix details for ARC 1059 and ARC 1106 from physical collectors, Nigerian discographic researchers and Tabansi/ARC catalogues; identify whether 1974/75 are recording, release or seller-entered dates; ask John Manton for the 2015 source log and whether either catalogue number is audible/credited in his source paperwork; check whether the 2023 Aromouthpiece TV concert remains archived and whether the association has its own authorised recordings. No Grok request: no authorised direct purchase/download URL or source-master match verified.


### Uzuakoli LP identity — label/imprint distinction and source limits (2026-11-15)

- The current Popsike search result identifies both ARC 1059 and ARC 1106 under the discogs-style label entity **ARC (21)**, not Tabansi: ARC 1106 / *Vol 2* is indexed 1974; ARC 1059 / *Harcourt Whyte And Choir* is indexed 1975. The separate unlicensed metadata page's “Other versions” table calls a **Tabansi** ARC 1059 copy a 1977 reissue. Therefore do not describe Tabansi as the original label on the basis of that page; provisional lineage is ARC issue (1974/75 market dates) → alleged Tabansi 1977 reissue, subject to physical label verification. https://www.popsike.com/php/quicksearch.php?bidsfrom=&bidsthru=&endfrom=&endthru=&eprice=&fcatno=&flabel=&incldescr=&layout=lv&pagenum=11&searchtext=+arc ; https://flac.vodila.net/51448/harcourt-whyte-and-choir-harcourt-whyte-and-choir-download-flac.html .
- The same unlicensed metadata page's tracklist for ARC 1059 (12 titles) and Nnamdi Olebara “Leader, Vocals” credit remain provisional. A 2013 memorial-concert report independently confirms Olebara performed at a Harcourt Whyte memorial, but does not validate his 1970s LP credit. Do not infer that the choir is the Uzuakoli patient choir or that Olebara's role was on the original pressing without sleeve/label evidence. https://www.newsexpressngr.com/news/1954/surprise-surprise-charly-boy-performs-with-mass-choir-at-harcourt-whytes-memorial .
- The chronology can be framed more precisely but is not resolved: ARC market dates 1974/75 precede Whyte's death in May 1977; a 1977 Tabansi reissue could have been issued around the time of his death; Music In Africa's “after his death” account may refer to another choir recording, or may conflict with these discographic entries. Manton's 2015 programme confirms he used original vinyl remasters but gives no catalogue numbers. There is no basis yet to map ARC 1059/1106 to the documentary source.
- A Tabansi historical overview and a 2020 BBE interview/feature explain the label's wider Nigerian catalogue and reissue history, but neither lists these Harcourt Whyte catalogue numbers. They are context, not confirmation. https://pan-african-music.com/en/bbe-revives-legendary-tabansi-records-2/ ; https://files.secure.website/wscfus/10814794/33300875/tabs-records-intro-on-few-of-our-catalogues-concise-design.pdf .
- Next decisive evidence: physical front/back sleeve and both label sides; runout/matrix inscriptions; exact ARC company/pressing identity; original 1974/75 issue and alleged Tabansi 1977 reissue comparison; personnel/choir identity; Manton's source log. No authorised purchase/download URL verified.


## Japan — Aoi-tori Gakudan (青い鳥楽団): 2025–26 preservation-project status update

**New primary institutional evidence (checked 2026-11-15):**
- The Tokyo Niigata Kenjinkai report of composer Umitaro Abe's 11 February 2025 lecture (published 20 May 2025) records Abe's own account of his Aiseien research. He says he had examined Kondo's surviving belongings, concert programmes and repertoire; learned that recordings were made at almost all concerts; and had seen sound sources and materials shown by an event organiser. He set a 2025 goal of preparing a selection from historical audio for public listening and publishing an audio collection under the title **「近藤宏一の過去と現在」** (“Koichi Kondo: Past and Present”). This is a documented target, not proof that the collection was released in 2025.
- The official Nagi Museum of Contemporary Art page for events on 7 and 21 February 2026 still describes Abe and Takeo Toyama as researching Kondo's surviving scores and sound sources, recording toward audio production, and tracing his history through contact with people connected to him. It does not announce a completed historical-audio collection, give a release link, list archival item IDs, or state that original masters have been digitised. **Status: no public release verified as of this search.** This is a bounded finding from the official event page and targeted Japanese-language searches, not proof that no private or local release exists.
- The 2026 page also explicitly frames the Abe/Toyama project as adding new life to Aoi-tori's music. Their contemporary performances and any newly recorded arrangements must be catalogued separately from historical Aoi-tori recordings. The 2023 National Hansen's Disease Museum “青い鳥のハモニカ” programme is likewise a contemporary performance by Abe, Toyama and Itoko Toma, not a historical band recording.
- The 2018 National Hansen's Disease Museum exhibition on sanatorium music displayed instruments, sheet music, costumes, programmes and photographs from sanatoria. Its page does not list audio carriers. The 2007 museum exhibition includes a photograph captioned as an Aoi-tori performance at Nagashima in May 1974 and documents a public event with Kondo on 17 November 2007; neither page identifies a surviving sound recording.
- The museum's credited photo list identifies Zhao Gen-zai photographs B053 (“本番を待つ”, waiting to perform) and B054 (“演奏”, performance), both Nagashima Aiseien, 1970. These help anchor the band and performance context, not an audio accession.

**Assessment / next steps:**
1. Treat Abe's 2025 public-audio goal as an important release lead. Ask whether the planned 「近藤宏一の過去と現在」 audio collection was completed, postponed, renamed, privately circulated or folded into a later project; request catalogue/tracklist, publication date, format, rights-holder and authorised access URL if it exists.
2. Ask Abe / the 2025 lecture organiser to distinguish original concert recordings from later dubs, documentary excerpts, soundscape tapes, newly recorded arrangements and re-performances. Seek source-item IDs, carrier formats, dates, custodians, transfer chain and any consent/rights conditions.
3. Use the 2026 Nagi Museum event page as a current research contact trail, not as evidence of a released album. Its profile confirms ongoing research into Kondo's scores and sound sources and recording toward audio production.
4. Ask Nagashima Aiseien History Museum and the National Hansen's Disease Museum whether their historical collections include sound carriers or only programmes, scores, photographs and instruments; the 2018 exhibition page itself establishes only the latter categories.
5. Keep the 1963 Carville soundscape / Aoi-tori-related tape, 1967 Ibaraki Hospital concert tape and Lighthouse hand-off, 1968 “鶴島哀歌” programme/performance, Sasaki's 2017-held recording(s), and Abe's later source materials as separate objects until a chain of custody or recording identity connects them.

**Sources:**
- Abe lecture report (2025-05-20; lecture held 2025-02-11): https://kouhou.niigatakenjinkai.com/?p=17685
- Nagi Museum of Contemporary Art official event/profile page (2026-02-07 / 2026-02-21): https://www.town.nagi.okayama.jp/moca/news/20260207.html
- National Hansen's Disease Museum 2018 exhibition: https://www.nhdm.jp/en/events/list/2241/
- National Hansen's Disease Museum 2007 exhibition / Kondo event / May 1974 photo: https://www.nhdm.jp/events/list/2327/
- Museum Zhao Gen-zai image list B053/B054: https://www.nhdm.jp/hansen/wp-content/uploads/2023/pdf/list_b.pdf
- National Hansen's Disease Museum 2023 “青い鳥のハモニカ” modern programme: https://www.nhdm.jp/en/events/list/4899/
- Abe's official news archive (bounded negative release check): https://www.umitaroabe.com/news/


## TLM Canada / Effect:Hope — audiovisual inventory and “More Than a Prophet” cross-archive title check (2026-11-16)

**What the public inventory actually establishes:**
- The International Leprosy Association's TLM Canada (later Effect:Hope) archive description, last updated January 2016, lists an extensive audiovisual collection. It states that audio tapes were made at **Purulia, Chandraghona, Bhutan, Irian Jaya, Papua New Guinea and Thailand**. It gives no individual tape IDs, titles, dates, creators/recordists, languages, subjects, durations, carrier formats, music content or access conditions for these six location groups. These are real AV leads, not yet music candidates.
- The same entry lists 11 largely 16mm film reels, many TLM Canada videotapes and some American Leprosy Mission videotapes; none is itemised in the public description. It separately lists boxed-slide Box 14: **80 slides (1978) with a cassette tape and pamphlet notes titled “More Than a Prophet.”** The content of the cassette and slides is not described. Do not infer that it contains music, or that the cassette is one of the six geographic audio tapes.
- The ILA page now lists **Bob MacFarlane** as Effect:Hope contact, in addition to the general info@effecthope.org and +1 888 537 7679. This is a contact route only; no contact has been made. The ILA description's entry date is January 2016, so verify current staff/archival responsibility before relying on the named contact.
- TLM International's separate archive description lists long-running paper files for Purulia (1908–87), Chandraghona (1921–87), Bhutan (1964–72 onward), and Thailand among others. These may provide contextual correspondence, visitor records, project reports or AV transfer references, but they are not themselves evidence that the six tapes are musical or that TLM International holds their masters.

**New cross-archive title collision / possible duplicate lead:**
- Wheaton College Billy Graham Center Archives' OMF audiovisual finding aid independently lists an undated slide-tape programme titled **“More Than a Prophet”**, described as “Work among Malay Muslims in Thailand”: slide Box 28 title cards S1842–S1845; three language tapes T45 (Cantonese), T46 (Mandarin), T47 (English); script Folder 17-5. Source: https://www2.wheaton.edu/bgc/archives/Guides/cn215%20slide-tape%20grid.pdf .
- This is a distinct catalogue context from Effect:Hope's 1978 Box 14, but the exact title and missionary/Thailand context make a shared programme, copy, distribution relationship or simple title reuse worth checking. No evidence yet proves the TLM cassette and OMF T45/T46/T47 are the same production. Do not merge records or claim that the TLM slide set is about Malay Muslims until compared.
- Decisive comparison: TLM Box 14 slide count/order, title cards, cassette label and duration, pamphlet imprint/producer, language; against OMF slide IDs S1842–S1845, tapes T45–T47, Folder 17-5 script and production credits. If they match, trace original producer, distribution/licensing and copy lineage. The OMF catalogue's language-specific tapes could be parallel dubs rather than the TLM cassette; that remains a hypothesis.

**Next exact steps:**
1. Seek item-level inventory / catalogue IDs for the six TLM Canada audio-tape location groups and the Box 14 slide-tape/cassette set; ask for titles, dates, languages, durations, recordist/producer, content summaries, formats, condition, rights/access and any digitised listening copy.
2. Compare “More Than a Prophet” TLM Box 14 against OMF CN215 slide Box 28 / T45–T47 / Folder 17-5; do not assume identity.
3. Search TLM International's Purulia and Chandraghona paper files and regional correspondence for AV programme names, recording visits, cassette inventories or transfer notes. Keep paper context separate from audio.
4. Only if the item-level descriptions identify music, determine whether it is local participant/community music, missionary-performed music, incidental programme music, or a field recording; preserve local names and languages and evaluate colonial/missionary power and consent.
5. No Grok request: no music-bearing item or authorised purchase/download URL has been identified.

**Sources:**
- ILA TLM Canada / Effect:Hope archive (entry updated Jan 2016; contact Bob MacFarlane): https://leprosyhistory.org/archive/archive714/
- Wheaton College Billy Graham Center Archives, OMF slide-tape programme grid, CN215: https://www2.wheaton.edu/bgc/archives/Guides/cn215%20slide-tape%20grid.pdf
- TLM International archive: https://leprosyhistory.org/archive/archive718/


## Austria / Phonogrammarchiv — WWI POW recordings: item-level music match and speech-only exclusion (2026-11-16)

**A specific musical item is now identified:**
- Benjamin Fox-Rosen's 2026 research project, “Objects and the Archive: Episodes in the Development of Music and Minorities Research,” identifies Phonogrammarchiv call number **Ph 2647**: **Psalm 126, “Shirat tsiyon” (“Song of Zion”), and a fragment of “Hatikvah” (“The Hope”)**, sung by **Isaak Isaakon** in the prisoner-of-war camp at **Theresienstadt/Terezín**, recorded by **Rudolf Pöch on 6 October 1915**.
- The item was released on the official Phonogrammarchiv edition **Series 17/1: Armenian–Jewish–Latvian–Lithuanian Recordings**, CD 1, track 8 (2018). The 2026 research project links the audio and protocol image as assets, but its page marks the audio rights “All rights reserved”; treat it as a contextual research/listening route, not an open reuse licence.
- The protocol reportedly labels Isaakon “Bergjude” and a “Tatisch” speaker, likely Judeo-Tat, an Iranian dialect spoken by Jewish communities in Dagestan/Azerbaijan at that time. Keep this as the archive's historical description plus the researcher's qualified linguistic interpretation, not an unqualified identity claim. The protocol includes what appears to be Isaakon's handwritten Hebrew text, transliteration and typed translation.
- **Provenance / ethical context:** this was part of an Anthropological Society project initiated to measure racial characteristics using POWs. The Phonogrammarchiv's own account says Rudolf Pöch and musicologist Robert Lach exploited the wartime camp situation as an unprecedented research opportunity; the archive now foregrounds the coercive/troubled circumstances and prisoners' critical comments. The item is therefore a strong music record but must be contextualised as knowledge produced under wartime captivity and asymmetrical researcher power.

**Important distinction / negative finding:**
- Series 17/1–5 contains approximately 200 language and music recordings of POWs from the former Tsarist army, made especially in Eger/Cheb, Reichenberg/Liberec and Theresienstadt/Terezín in Bohemia. The five subseries identify Armenian/Jewish/Latvian/Lithuanian; Finno-Ugric; Russian/Ukrainian; Turk-Tatar; and Georgian/Avar/Jewish/Ossetian/Svanetian material. They were digitised and issued with audio CDs, data CDs containing original protocol scans, orientation transcriptions and scholarly commentary.
- **Do not conflate Series 17/6:** the Italian POW recordings were made by Karl von Ettmayer at Mauthausen and Marchtrenk in 1918. The official description characterises their selected content as three rural stories / language samples, with extensive protocols and a difficult recording-number mismatch. A 2017 Marchtrenk sound installation used seven speech recordings of named Italian POWs (spoken number sequences and regional folk tales). No musical performance is established by these descriptions; retain as speech-only/context unless item-level evidence proves otherwise.
- The Phonogrammarchiv online catalogue covers about 85% of holdings; research access is governed by agreements with depositors and varies by item. The archive currently notes limited services through January 2027 due to relocation; enquiries for copies: pha@oeaw.ac.at. Publicly available catalogue/edition does not mean unrestricted copying.

**Acquisition/access status:**
- Official edition is a physical CD + data CD-ROM publication; the publisher's catalogue lists Series 17/1 as one audio CD and one data CD-ROM, 2018, ISBN 978-3-7001-8226-9, €25. No authorised downloadable audio file or direct digital checkout URL was verified, so **no Grok download request**. A physical publication route is distinct from an authorised file-download route.
- Primary research links:
  - 2026 item-level contextual identification, Ph 2647: https://www.researchcatalogue.net/view/3355133/4340708
  - Official Phonogrammarchiv Series 17/1–5 description: https://www.oeaw.ac.at/en/phonogrammarchiv/phonogrammarchiv/detail/recordings-from-prisoner-of-war-camps-world-war-i-series-17/1-5-1
  - Official historical-collections publication catalogue / CD identifiers: https://www.oeaw.ac.at/en/phonogrammarchiv/publications/audio-and-video-publications/the-complete-historical-collections-1899-1950/
  - Official online catalogue and access conditions: https://catalog.phonogrammarchiv.at/
  - Official Series 17/6 description: https://www.oeaw.ac.at/phonogrammarchiv/detail/recordings-from-prisoner-of-war-camps-world-war-i-series-17/6
  - Marchtrenk “Angezählt / Counted out” installation: https://www.basis-wien.at/de/events/99197
  - Monique Scheer, “Captive Voices: Phonographic Recordings in the German and Austrian Prisoner-of-War Camps of World War I”: https://www.academia.edu/14483841/Captive_Voices_Phonographic_Recordings_in_the_German_and_Austrian_Prisoner_of_War_Camps_of_World_War_I
  - Official Series 17/1 publication bibliographic details and price: https://www.austriaca.at/0xc1aa5576_0x0039e8cd.pdf

**Next exact steps:**
1. Audit Series 17/1–5 item/track lists for other musical items, preserving each singer, language/community, camp, recording date and Phonogrammarchiv call number.
2. Determine whether Ph 2647 is directly playable from the Research Catalogue page and whether the official edition remains available from the publisher; do not infer file download access from embedded audio.
3. Keep Series 17/6 Italian spoken narratives outside the music candidate list unless a specific musical item is identified.
4. Contextualise each POW recording through the original protocol and current critical scholarship; avoid presenting colonial/racial typologies as neutral identities.


### Chile / Chacabuco — survivor testimony and discographic reconciliation (2026-11-16)

- **New locally grounded source:** Luis Cifuentes Seves' first-person recollection, reproduced in the Chilean small-web archive Discoteca Nacional Chile's post for Ángel Parra, *Chacabuco* (VPA 8344, I Dischi dello Zodiaco, Italian edition 1977), distinguishes the original clandestine concert recording from later studio re-recordings. Cifuentes says the cassette was clandestinely recorded by Alberto Corvalán Castillo with help from other prisoners, using a cassette recorder supplied by an officer/chaplain; Ángel Parra carried the cassette out, and the recording was first issued on Italian vinyl in 1974–75. The blog's editorial introduction dates the Italian pressing to 1977 and says international release was 1975. Preserve the 1974–75 first-vinyl claim and 1977 Italian-edition claim as compatible but not independently label-verified. The blog's sleeve/post title identifies catalogue VPA 8344.
- Cifuentes identifies the prison ensemble as **Los de Chacabuco**, founded and directed by Ángel Parra; he states Parra directed but did not sing or play in the ensemble. Listed members: Víctor Canto, Manuel Castro, Ángel Cereceda Parra (Ángel Parra), Luis Cifuentes, Marcelo Concha, Luis Corvalán Márquez, Antonio González, Manuel Ipinza, Ernesto Parra, Julio Vega and Ricardo Yocelewski. Preserve names only as this source gives them; don't infer which anonymous voices on each recording belong to which individual.
- **Composition correction:** Cifuentes says the cuecas **“El suertudo”** and **“El puntudo”** (variants “El suertúo” / “El puntúo”) were composed at Chacabuco between Nov 1973 and Feb 1974 by himself and Víctor Canto, and performed there by Los de Chacabuco. The original LP sleeve grouped them under “El afortunado”; they appear separately on the archival account's transcription. This is stronger first-person authorship evidence than a thesis table that assigns Roberto Parra music to “El Suertúo”. That thesis also inconsistently writes “Luis Fuentes” in a narrative sentence while its credit table says Luis Cifuentes. Retain the discrepancy, but provisionally privilege Cifuentes' first-person account for authorship; no independent score or contemporary sleeve credit was found. A 2025 article bibliography independently cites Cifuentes' 2014 Cantos Cautivos testimonies for both songs and a 2014 “Recuerdos de Los de Chacabuco” source.
- The Discoteca Nacional post describes LP side A as the clandestine in-camp concert and side B as Ángel Parra songs re-recorded in Europe for better sound. Its side-A tracklist: “Libre” (Conjunto Punto Treinta); “Oficina Chacabuco” (Conjunto Jamarí); “Volver” (Hugo Peñaloza); “Himno a la alegría” (Coro/Conjunto Chacabuco); “Tonada del viejo amor”; “Amalia Rosa”; “Caliche”; “El suertúo”; “El puntúo”. Side B: “Canción de amor”; “Canción para Angelito”; “Canción para Javiera”; “El alma de Chacabuco”; “Canción del adiós” — all attributed there to Ángel Parra. Do not conflate this album's mixed provenance with an all-in-camp recording: the second side is a later re-recording.
- **MMDH object separation remains unresolved:** its Archivo Radial search for “chacabuco” still returns two separate Radio Moscú / *Escucha Chile* records: (1) “Centros de Detención”, 23:36, ceremony recording with ensemble/song list including “El Puntudo”; (2) “Grabación clandestina en Oficina Salitrera ‘Chacabuco’...”, 2:03, separately described as a clandestine recording attributed to Alberto Corvalán and listing the Coro Chacabuco, Ángel Parra, “Canción de la Despedida” and narration. The 2022 MMDH thesis locates “El Suertúo” at 18:30–20:50 in the 23:36 “Centros de Detención” object. This supports a track/timecode relation to the longer object, but does not prove that either catalogue object is identical to the LP cassette master or that the 2:03 clip is an excerpt of the 23:36 object. Keep both archive records separate pending media identifiers/source comparison.
- The thesis assigns conservation code **067-001-002** to both “El Suertúo” and “Cueca de Filistoque”; this is a collection/conservation reference, not a demonstrated direct audio URL. The MMDH search page exposes embedded HTML audio players but the research pass did not verify a stable direct media file URL or explicit reuse licence.
- **Access/rights:** Discoteca Nacional Chile is a volunteer small-web discography and says material posted had already been available online; this is a discovery/source-transcription lead, not an authorised download route. Its own rights notice explicitly says the notice does not include authorisation for publication, reproduction, adaptation, public communication or distribution. Do not link its repost as a Grok download request or recommend ripping it. No authorised purchase/download route verified.
- Sources: MMDH two-object search https://archivoradial.mmdh.cl/buscador_resultado.php?filtro=chacabuco&registros=0&tipo=3 ; Discoteca Nacional Chile / Cifuentes testimony https://discotecanacionalchile.blogspot.com/2019/04/angel-parra-chacabuco-vpa-8344-i-dischi.html ; MMDH thesis https://openbiblio.museodelamemoria.cl/media/digitales/TESIS_EDUCACION%20MUSICAL.pdf ; thesis appendix https://manager.mmdh.cl/uploads/L_Ibro_tesis_20212022_EDUCACION_MUSICAL_Vok_WEB_da5720b67c.pdf ; Núria Bonet, “Negotiating Musical Practices in Chilean Prisons during Pinochet’s Dictatorship (1973–1990)” https://hispadoc.es/servlet/articulo?codigo=10419398 .
- **Next exact steps:** identify MMDH internal object IDs and underlying media URLs for both records; compare audio/timecodes and source genealogy with LP VPA 8344 / 2003 *Pisagua + Chacabuco* CD; locate primary label/sleeve/matrix scans; consult the Cifuentes 2014 Cantos Cautivos entries and the original testimony context; resolve Roberto Parra vs Víctor Canto/Luis Cifuentes credit discrepancy without erasing first-person evidence.


### Japan / Ōshima Seishōen — Takashi Tōjō's three CDs and final recording, *Kokoro* (こころ) (2026-11-16)

**Status: VERIFIED recording/release and official physical-CD purchase route; tracklist/provenance detail still incomplete.** This is a resident's own singing and long-term music-making, not a visiting performance. Preserve the distinction between Tōjō's three personal CDs, the institution's older communal song culture, and Tomoe Sawa's separate research/re-recordings of sanatorium songs.

- **Maker/performer:** Takashi Tōjō (東條高; とうじょう たかし), resident of National Sanatorium Ōshima Seishōen (大島青松園), Kagawa, Japan. He entered the sanatorium aged 16 and lived there for about 80 years. Japanese reporting documents his music-making, including brass-band instruments, choir/hymn singing and participation in inter-sanatorium karaoke gatherings. Do not reduce the work to his diagnosis or confinement.
- **Three CD lineage:** (1) *生かされて* (Ikasarete / “Kept Alive”), 2003, a hymn album recorded with Tomoe Sawa; (2) *この道* (Kono Michi / “This Road”), Feb 2021, described by Sawa as a five-song “small album” with her piano and chorus throughout; (3) *こころ* (Kokoro / “Heart”), third CD, made in April 2026 and described in local reporting as Tōjō's final/culminating recording. Do not assume the three CDs have identical performers, tracklists, production or rights.
- **2026 CD:** KSB's 29 Apr 2026 report confirms Tōjō wanted to preserve his own voice, that Sawa produced the recording, and that he particularly wanted to sing “こころ”, a poem set to music by Sawa. The report also identifies “君といつまでも” (Kimi to Itsumademo / “Forever with You”) as a song included on the CD, performed in memory of his late wife, whom he met at the sanatorium. RSK's 20 Jun 2026 feature describes an eight-day recording plan over four weeks, two days per week, with repeated line-by-line takes and adjustments. The album's complete tracklist, recording dates/session logs, musicianship credits, recording venue, physical catalogue number and mastering details remain unverified.
- **Death / identity care:** Tōjō died 21 Sep 2026, aged 96, after the broadcast; RSK added a postscript. This is a completed recording project, not a current living-person contact lead. Do not seek or publish private family details. Any further provenance enquiry should go through Sawa/Como Esta or an appropriate archive with sensitivity.
- **Authorised acquisition:** Sawa's official Como Esta order/contact page lists **東條高 こころ** at ¥1,000 and accepts CD orders through its “商品の購入” form; it also lists *この道* at ¥1,000 and *生かされて* at ¥2,500. This verifies an authorised physical-CD purchase route, not a digital download. Page: https://comoesta.co.jp/contact/ . **GROK DOWNLOAD REQUEST:** acquire the physical CD *東條高 こころ* through the official order form and digitise/transfer only according to the supplied disc and rights terms. Known track titles from local reporting: “こころ”; “君といつまでも”. **Complete tracklist not published in sources checked**; request/record the disc booklet and exact track sequence before treating this as complete metadata. Do not infer the other tracks. The purchase route does not itself grant reuse rights.
- **Rights lead:** Sawa's 2020–21 crowdfunding report states the master rights for *生かされて* belonged to Tōjō, while other CDs in that reward bundle belonged to Como Esta. This is specific to those named masters and does not establish the rights chain for *この道* or *こころ*. Confirm the *こころ* master, composition, performance and artwork rights separately; the official sale is not a reuse licence.
- **Small-web/local-language discovery path:** the Japanese Blogger page 「明日への言葉」 (28 Jun 2025) preserved an interview summary with Sawa describing her field research across all 13 national Hansen's disease sanatoria, the 23 sanatorium songs she identified, and the dual role of institutional songs as both coercive ideology and shared memory. This was a discovery breadcrumb, then cross-checked against the National Diet Library's Reference Cooperative Database entry supplied by the National Hansen's Disease Museum Library and RSK/KSB reporting. Blog: https://asuhenokotoba.blogspot.com/2025/06/ ; NDL/NHDM reference answer: https://crd.ndl.go.jp/reference/entry/reference/show?asc=desc&fi=3_%E6%96%87%E7%8C%AE%E7%B4%B9%E4%BB%8B%E3%80%80%E4%BA%8B%E5%AE%9F%E8%AA%BF%E6%9F%BB&id=1000358845&lsmp=1&mcbt=200&mcmd=200&mcup=200&oldasc=desc&oldmc=100&oldst=update&page=ref_view&rnk=1&st=update . This song-history corpus is contextual and not automatically a set of surviving recordings.
- **Additional local sources:** KSB, 29 Apr 2026, https://news.ksb.co.jp/article/16531366 ; RSK, 20 Jun 2026, https://newsdig.tbs.co.jp/articles/rsk/2727313 ; Sawa official catalogue/order page https://comoesta.co.jp/contact/ ; Sawa 2021 crowdfunding update for *この道* and *生かされて* rights note https://camp-fire.jp/projects/271521/view/activities/235117 ; 2026 interview on sanatorium music culture and *愛生園挽歌* on Sawa's album https://news.livedoor.com/article/detail/30852155/ .
- **Next exact steps:** obtain the *こころ* CD's booklet/tracklist and catalogue/production credits; verify whether the disc includes any historical sanatorium repertoire or is entirely Tōjō's personal repertoire; identify recording venue/session participants; clarify rights for this specific master; inspect *この道* as a separate earlier recording and avoid conflating it with the 2026 CD. Check whether Tōjō's earlier ensemble performances have independent surviving recordings.


### Austria / Phonogrammarchiv — item-level music screen of the 1915–16 POW collection (2026-11-16)

**Status: several new, exact archival item/plate references found. No direct downloadable audio URLs or track-to-CD mappings beyond Ph 2647 have been verified.** These are historical POW recordings, not a neutral folklore collection: the archive project was entangled with wartime captivity and racial-anthropological research. Keep original protocol terminology attributed to its source; do not convert it into present-day identity labels. No audio auditioned and no archive/family contact made.

#### Confirmed musical items

- **Ph 2607 — Crimean Romani dance tune (August 1915, Eger/Cheb).** Christiane Fennesz-Juhasz's item-level study says this is the *only* Romani recording in the Austrian POW collection: a fast dance tune sung without words, on meaningless syllables, by two unnamed men whom the protocol calls “Kjamil 1” and “Kjamil 2”. One was a 23-year-old basket weaver from Bakhchisaray; the other a 28-year-old smith from Duvankoy (now Verkhnesadovoye), resident in Sevastopol. Pöch had filmed the corresponding dance before the recording; the film shows three men alternating as dancers while the others sing and clap. The film and phonogram are related evidence, not proof of a synchronised audiovisual take. **High discovery/obscurity value; high confidence in item identity and provenance.** No full names are supplied; do not try to identify the two men. The original protocol's “Muslim gypsies” is a historical label and should only be quoted as such. The five language-group CDs of Series 17/1–5 do not list a separate Romani subseries, so inclusion of Ph 2607 in that edition is unverified. Official catalogue lookup route: https://catalog.phonogrammarchiv.at/ (search project “Kriegsgefangene 1915-1918”, item Ph 2607); no stable item page or direct audio URL found.

- **Ph 2624 + Ph 2625 — Armenian folk song “Unglücklicher Umstand” (“Unfortunate Circumstance”), 15 September 1915, Reichenberg/Liberec.** Sung by Arshak Manukjanz from Yerevan, aged 30, a confectioner; recorded by Rudolf Pöch. The scholarly account describes a linked Armenian speech-and-song recording across the two archive plates. The lyrics use swallows as messengers from home and speak of freedom, loved ones and separation. Pöch's protocol notes a long pause between stanzas and a repeated attempt in which the fifth stanza did not fit. The source does **not** specify which of the two plate numbers contains the spoken portion and which the song; do not assign that split without the protocols. **High-value mixed speech/song item; exact archive IDs and provenance verified, CD track mapping not.** Likely relevant to Series 17/1, but exact audio-CD track number not established.

- **Ph 2651 and Ph 2653 — Lithuanian songs by Josef Weiland (1915).** Britta Lange identifies songs sung by Weiland that recount the Lithuanian national myth of Biruta and express Lithuanian patriotism; the citations point to Ph 2651 and Ph 2653 respectively. Weiland was a 32-year-old saddler from Moscheibe, Kovno district. The nearby Ph 2649/2650 protocol sequence is dated 6 October 1915 and names Theresienstadt; do not automatically transfer that date/place to every later Weiland plate without checking its own protocol. The exact song titles and one-to-one item/variant mapping remain open. A third patriotic text attached to the Ph 2652 documentation has **no sound recording** and must not be counted as an audio item. **Promising; exact shelfmarks, performer and content family verified, individual titles and CD track numbers incomplete.** Likely relevant to Series 17/1.

- **Ph 2744 — “K’art’uli marseliosa” / “Kharthuli marcelioza” (Georgian Marseillaise), 26 August 1916, Eger.** Sung into the phonograph by Prokophi Kalandadse, a 27-year-old restaurateur from the Tiflis guberniya; recorded by Hans Pollak. This is a Georgian national/patriotic song urging brothers to rise and fight for freedom, **not a translation of the French Marseillaise**. Lach 1928, Georgian song no. 205, p. 200, prints another version as no. 207, pp. 201–202. The 2013 study discusses the missing context around when/where this song was sung and how its performance changed in the POW camp. **High-value, clearly identified song and recording; strong provenance.** Likely belongs with the Georgian material in Series 17/5; exact CD track number and direct audio URL not verified.

- **Archive plates 2751 and 2755 — two Gurian wedding-song variants, 27 August 1916, Eger.** Sung by Lewarsi Mamaladse, Meliton Mamaladse and Kirile Achaladse; recorded by Hans Pollak under Robert Lach. The two songs correspond to Lach 1928 nos. 234 and 236. Dirr's German translations begin “Ein fröhlicher Wirt / Hat liebe Gäste” and “Uns (wünschen wir) Frieden und Sieg, / Unserem Wirte Reichtum”. The archive also made separate-voice test recordings on plates 2752, 2753 and 2754 (L12–L14), but the 2013 study explicitly says these were **trial plates that did not enter the archive**. Do not list 2752–2754 as surviving archive audio or imply that the three isolated voices can be recombined. The surviving group variants are 2751 and 2755. The singers told Lach that these Gurian songs were normally polyphonic and that each man carried a leading melodic line; the camp performance/recording conditions did not reproduce the full home ensemble. **High-value, strong item and performer provenance; no exact CD track numbers or direct audio URL.** Likely part of Series 17/5.

- **Estonian POW song cluster — nine wax-cylinder recordings (1915 and 1917).** Janika Oras (2012) identifies two Pöch recordings, **Ph 2631–2632**, performed by Adolf Mitt (22, mechanic) and Boris Rea (21, hairdresser), both from the Pärnu region in western Estonia; and seven songs recorded under Lach's supervision on **Ph 2829–2930** (shelfmark range as printed), performed by Otto Õunapu (Lach's spelling “Aunap”, 33) and Johannes Lettermu (“Lettermo”, 35), farmers from the Rakvere region. A further 152 song texts/melodies transcribed from these two men and Johannes Mick (27, bookkeeper, Viljandi) were deposited in the Estonian Folklore Archives in 1954 as **RKM I 2, 101–152**; these 152 transcriptions are not 152 recordings. The 2012 chapter does not give titles for the nine Vienna wax-cylinder items. **Promising item cluster with exact performer/shelfmark references; track titles and item-to-CD mapping open.** Likely relevant to Series 17/2 (Finno-Ugric), but no exact CD track numbers or direct audio URL found. Follow up in Estonian-language sources and the Estonian Folklore Archives rather than importing the Berlin tracklist.

#### Hybrid / non-music controls — do not count as standalone music candidates

- **Ph 2656, Josef Weiland:** two spoken texts, “Ein russischer Soldat” and the longer version of “Jaunas Kareivis” (“A Young Soldier”). The final lines of the first text sound like a sung refrain within speech; the second is a spoken autobiographical/political text. Treat as a hybrid spoken-word item, not a song release. The source notes uncertainty over authorship and that the recording ends before the final lines of the written version.
- **Ph 2626, Artasches Kamuljanz:** Armenian verses/message to parents from behind the barbed wire, spoken/recited, not a confirmed song.
- **Ph 2634, Sergej Objedkov:** Finnish “free speech”/language recording, not music.
- **Ph 2780–2782:** Lewarsi Mamaladse's spoken Amiran myth, not music.
- **Ph 2783:** three poems by Tseretheli, not identified as sung.
- **Ph 2785:** Georgian language sample using textbook sentences, not music.
- **Ph 2652:** a third written text attached to the protocol but explicitly without sound.

#### Georgian community / family source and identity caution

A Georgian-language family-history article by Gaiyoz Mamadze (https://www.georoyal.ge/?MTID=5&TID=47&id=3946) is a valuable local source, not a substitute for the archive protocols. The author identifies his grandfather's brother as **Lavrosi Mamaladze (ლავროსი მამალაძე)** and argues that the archive/Robert Lach's **Lewarsi Mamaladse** is a German rendering of Lavrosi, citing a 1959 article by Galaktion Mindadze. Record both forms and the source of the proposed correction; do not silently overwrite the archive spelling. The article also identifies Meliton Mamaladze, Prokophi Kalandadse, Kirile Akhaladze and other singers by region, and describes Lavrosi as a translator and cultural intermediary for Lach. It says the family received audio versions of Georgian POW songs through German and Austrian embassy activity and can provide a relative's song if a descendant recognises a singer. This is a **potential private family-copy route**, not a public catalogue or permission to copy/reuse. The article does not name the edition, carrier, catalogue number or download URL; the embassy-release claim remains unverified. It also describes the 1928 Lach/Dirr volume as a 254-page collection with more than 300 Georgian songs and explains that many Gurian songs were normally polyphonic although the written notation was monophonic. Preserve the difference between the large written repertoire and the much smaller subset actually phonographed.

#### Edition, access and rights

- Official Series 17/1–5 description and language grouping: https://www.oeaw.ac.at/en/phonogrammarchiv/phonogrammarchiv/detail/recordings-from-prisoner-of-war-camps-world-war-i-series-17/1-5-1
- Official audio/video publication catalogue: https://www.oeaw.ac.at/en/phonogrammarchiv/publications/audio-and-video-publications/the-complete-historical-collections-1899-1950/
- Official publication bibliography/pricing (2018 catalogue): https://www.austriaca.at/0xc1aa5576_0x0039e8cd.pdf . Series 17/1 and 17/5 are listed as physical audio-CD plus data-CD-ROM editions at €25 each (ISBNs 978-3-7001-8226-9 and 978-3-7001-8230-6 respectively). The data disc is the route to protocol scans and supporting documentation; no authorised downloadable audio files were verified. The listed e-book endpoints were not accessible for inspection and must not be treated as audio downloads.
- Research source for Ph 2607 and the Romani POW context: Christiane Fennesz-Juhasz, “Sound recordings of Romani soldiers in German and Austrian prisoner-of-war camps, 1915–1918”: https://mlg-manager.com/uploads/kozhanov_oslon_halwachs_2017_fs_tcherenkov_9858acc4d7.pdf
- Item-level source for Ph 2624/2625, Ph 2651/2653/2656, Ph 2744, plates 2751/2755 and the non-music controls: Britta Lange, “Tonaufnahmen von Kriegsgefangenen”, in *mi-13-0161-01 band 17*: https://austriaca.at/0xc1aa5572%200x002e33f3.pdf
- Estonian item cluster and Estonian Folklore Archives references: Janika Oras, “Recordings of songs by Estonian prisoners of war: repertoire and its representations” (2012): https://www.academia.edu/16864829/Recordings_of_songs_by_Estonian_prisoners_of_war_repertoire_and_its_representations_Ross_Jaan_Toim_Encapsulated_Voices_Estonian_Sound_Recordings_from_the_German_Prisoner_of_War_Camps_in_1916_1918_147_177_K%C3%B6ln_Weimar_Wien_B%C3%B6hlau_2012
- Keep the separate Berlin Phonogramm-Archiv / Lautarchiv POW recordings and the 2014 *Echoes from the Past* publication distinct from the Vienna Phonogrammarchiv items. The 2012 Oras chapter's detailed track tables describe Berlin recordings, not the nine Vienna cylinders.

**Next exact steps:** (1) obtain/inspect the Series 17/1, 17/2 and 17/5 data-CD protocol scans or an archive catalogue export and map each shelfmark to the published CD track; (2) verify whether Ph 2607 has a public catalogue excerpt and whether the related Pöch film has a separate accessible item page; (3) locate Lach 1928 nos. 205, 207, 234 and 236 in a digitised/local Georgian copy and compare original Georgian titles/texts with the protocols; (4) identify the embassy/family audio edition mentioned by Mamadze without assuming it is the Vienna CD set; (5) follow the Estonian RKM I 2, 101–152 collection and Lach's 1918/1929 reports for exact song titles; (6) preserve all POW-capture and archival rights caveats. No direct audio/download route was found for these new items; no Grok request.

### Chile / Chacabuco — exact participant-made cueca testimony pages (2026-11-16)

The two Cantos Cautivos testimony records are now individually mapped:
- **“El suertúo” / “El suertudo” (“Lucky Devil”)** — https://www.cantoscautivos.org/en/testimony.php?query=10654
- **“El puntúo” / “El puntudo” (“A Cocky Fellow”)** — https://www.cantoscautivos.org/en/testimony.php?query=10655

Both pages document cuecas made and performed by Los de Chacabuco in the Chacabuco concentration camp between November 1973 and February 1974, secretly recorded by Alberto Corvalán Castillo with assistance from Guillermo Orrego and Domingo Chávez. The testimony describes a cassette recorder supplied by a guard and hidden beneath a wooden stage constructed by prisoners; Ángel Parra later took the cassette out of the camp. The songs appeared on the Italian *Pisagua + Chacabuco* LP (1974–75; later Italian edition VPA 8344) and on the 2003 CD. Preserve the authorship conflict already noted above: the testimony/first-person Cifuentes account and the LP/thesis credit fields are not fully consistent about the respective contributions of Víctor Canto, Luis Cifuentes and Roberto Parra. Do not collapse the two cuecas into one track merely because some album editions group them under “El afortunado”.

Cantos Cautivos is the exact item/testimony reference, not a verified direct audio-file URL. Its testimony metadata/text carries a CC BY-NC-ND notice, but musical compositions/recordings require separate authorisation from the relevant rights holders. No authorised download or purchase route was found, so no Grok download request. Keep these pages distinct from the two MMDH Archivo Radial objects (23:36 “Centros de Detención” and 2:03 “Grabación clandestina…”): their internal object IDs and direct media URLs remain unresolved, and the two objects have not been audio-compared. No audio auditioned.


### Brazil — Hospital Público Juliano Moreira, Belém do Pará: Raul's patient trio and cassette (small-web LEAD, 2026-11-16)

**Status: LEAD / no surviving tape or transfer located. Do not count as an extant recording yet.**

- A 2010 Portuguese-language Blogger post, “Memórias de um Hospital Psiquiátrico”, reproduces a first-person account by a narrator who says they lived/worked as an academic resident at the now-closed Hospital Público Juliano Moreira in Belém do Pará. The page includes a table of contents for what appears to be a longer memoir/work, including chapters “O sanfoneiro” and “O orador”.
- The account says a patient called Raul wanted his speeches preserved on cassette. A nurse with musical knowledge arranged for him to learn singing; he was joined by another patient who played accordion (sanfona) and a third who played bass drum (bumbo). They rehearsed three times weekly, coordinated by the nurse, and performed at hospital festivities. The narrator states that **five songs and three speeches were recorded on one cassette**. The songs are not titled or described. Raul later left the hospital and wrote that he had acquired a recorder and cassettes and was singing/recording with farm workers; that later activity is a separate, unlocated lineage.
- This is unusually specific first-person evidence for a patient-formed trio and an actual in-hospital cassette, but the cassette's current existence, custodian, recording date, identity of the other two musicians and exact song titles remain unverified. The author is identified as **Luiz Gonzaga de Freitas Filho**; the 1999 book says character names were changed, so “Raul” is a pseudonym and must not be treated as a real name. The source does not name the hospital in the individual anecdote, but the memoir's introductory section explicitly identifies the former Juliano Moreira hospital in Belém. Do not assume all three players were patients: the narrator calls the accordion and drum players patients, and the nurse coordinated the recording.
- The story also contains dated context: Raul's political oratory is placed in 1968; the recording chronology is not explicit. Do not assign the tape to 1968 without further evidence.
- **Source genealogy:** the 2010 Blogger page reproduces material from Luiz Gonzaga de Freitas Filho, *Memórias de um Hospital Psiquiátrico: Contos sobre casos no interior de um hospício*, 1st edition, 1999. The digitised copy identifies the author and includes his biography and the relevant chapter; the author says character names were changed. Blogger page: https://lgicaecomportamento-trabalhoseestudos.blogspot.com/2010/08/memorias-de-um-hospital-psiquiatrico.html ; digitised book copy (discovery/reference only, not a reuse source): https://pt.scribd.com/document/30644936/Memorias-de-um-hospital-psiquiatrico . The Blogger direct open was blocked by anti-bot redirect; Scribd text was inspected. The account is first-person published testimony, not an inspected primary audio object.
- **Local archive route:** the Pará State Secretariat of Culture says the Museu da Imagem e do Som do Pará (MIS-PA) holds audio collections including cassettes, CDs, reel-to-reel tapes, recordings of music and cultural events; contact listed as mis.para@gmail.com. This is a plausible repository to ask about the Juliano Moreira / Freitas Filho recording, not evidence that the tape is held there. Source: https://secult.pa.gov.br/espacosecultpag/19/museus-e-memoriais/ . Also investigate the Arquivo Público do Estado do Pará / 2009 *História, loucura e memória* collection catalogue. Next: search the author's 1999 edition and local library catalogues for recording references; ask MIS-PA whether any tape is catalogued under Juliano Moreira, Luiz Gonzaga de Freitas Filho, the 1960s hospital activities or unnamed patient performances; keep “Raul” as an explicitly pseudonymous character and do not seek a real-world identity. Until then keep as LEAD/context, not main discography and no Grok download request.

### Brazil — Banda Cazuluz, Centro de Ressocialização de Cuiabá (small-web lead, 2011)

- A contemporary Mato Grosso state-government/Sejusp report (18 Sep 2010) says the band had produced **one CD inside the unit**, containing four member-written compositions plus songs by Legião Urbana, Zé Ramalho and Paralamas do Sucesso. A 27 Aug 2011 G1 report preserved in a local Blogspot archive says the group had recorded **two CDs** by then, with five incarcerated members and a makeshift studio in a disused bathroom; a same-day *Diario de Cuiabá* report describes eight members. Preserve the headcount discrepancy and the plausible one-CD-to-two-CD chronology without assuming catalogue identity. A local SoulGuetto radio-program blog also says the show visited the group in Sep 2011. The band leader is named in the report as Ronivaldo Martins Teixeira, with other members also named. Preserve only names already published by the source; do not seek private identities or add sensitive criminal details.
- This is a documented in-prison recording project, but the current existence of the two CDs, their tracklists, recording dates, distribution, rights and whether the group remains active are not established by the 2011 repost. The post itself is a secondary reproduction, not a direct artist source. Do not treat its images/audio (if any) as authorised acquisition.
- Sources: Sejusp/Toque de Alerta (18 Sep 2010) https://toquedealerta.com.br/noticia/22350/Musica_transforma_a_vida_de_reeducandos_do_Centro_de_Ressocializacao_de_Cuiaba ; G1 report preserved at https://liberalcuiaba.blogspot.com/2011_08_21_archive.html ; *Diario de Cuiabá* (27 Aug 2011) https://www.diariodecuiaba.com.br/policia/reconstruindo-a-vida-com-musicalidade/398343 ; local repost https://conselhodacomunidadefoz.blogspot.com/2011/08/bom-exemplopresos-formam-banda-e-gravam.html ; SoulGuetto blog https://programasoulguetto.blogspot.com/ . Next: locate CD artwork/tracklists, who retained the discs, local broadcast/photo collections and the band's authorised archive. No catalogue or acquisition route verified. No download request.


### India — Tinka Tinka Jail Radio / “Music in Jail” corpus (2021–24)

**Status: VERIFIED multi-recording corpus; multiple official public streams, but no authorised file-download/purchase route verified.** Local-language and small-web discovery surfaced the Tinka Tinka Foundation's own Hindi/English documentation of a growing body of inmate-created songs across Indian prisons. The corpus is not one album and should be split into item-level records if individual recordings are selected for the catalogue.

- **Source of record:** Tinka Tinka Foundation's official “Music in Jail” index says more than a dozen songs had been released from different jails since prison-radio work began in 2019; it links official YouTube uploads and gives dates, jails, performer/composer descriptions and episode numbers. https://tinkatinka.org/jail-music-2/ . The foundation's site asserts all rights reserved and prohibits copying/reuse without written permission; public YouTube streaming is not a download or reuse licence.
- **District Jail, Jind, Haryana — Jai Bhagwan:** a 25 Dec 2023 release of a song written by Jai Bhagwan, a prisoner and jail-radio participant; the source describes his ambition to release his own audio album. The opening line “गमे ज़िंदगी में मुस्कुराना सीख ले” is the identifying lyric, but a formal title is not supplied. Official video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Sag02xT9Pig . Separately, on 21 Jan 2024 he wrote and sang **“आए अवध में राम”**, with four other incarcerated musicians: Ramroop (dholak), Shashi (harmonium), Sunil and Govind (matka). It was broadcast as Tinka Jail Radio Episode 81. Official video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=333LGapSFDc . Keep these as separate releases.
- **Jind–Dehradun cross-jail collaboration, Episode 77 (25 Dec 2023):** Tinka's account says a Jind song was shared with District Jail Dehradun and a new collaborative composition was developed by incarcerated musicians Dr Suchit Narang, Arun and Rohit. It may be a reworking of Jai Bhagwan's song, but the exact source relationship and track title are not fully specified; do not merge without comparing the episode audio. Official episode: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dsL1Ddq7q8c .
- **District Jail, Dehradun, Uttarakhand — Tinka Jail Radio signature tune (31 May 2023):** credited to inmate Dr Suchit Narang, a music teacher before incarceration; it opens and closes the podcast episodes. Official video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=L19FMAoUcME . Preserve his credited name as published; don't infer other personal details.
- **District Jail, Karnal, Haryana — theme song (23 Jul 2021):** ten incarcerated people jointly composed and sang the jail's theme song; opening lines “Toofano se rooke na, honsala na ye kam…”. Official upload: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=oE187L3eJqA . Lyrics/title not otherwise identified in the catalogue.
